While large amounts of unlabeled target-domain data are often available, effectively exploiting them requires adaptation strategies tailored to the specific requirements of HTR. We propose DRA-HTR, an unsupervised generative domain adaptation pipeline specifically designed for HTR and built upon the DRANet framework. The proposed approach combines source-domain pretraining, unsupervised adaptation to the visual characteristics of the target domain, relying on the generation of target-style source samples for task-specific training. We further investigate the integration of post-HTR correction to complement visual adaptation with language adaptation, relying on large language models, which we introduce as DRAL-HTR. The proposed framework is evaluated across seven HTR datasets spanning four languages, using a single set of hyperparameters for all source-target combinations. The recognition model achieves state-of-the-art performance on four datasets and competitive results on the remaining three. In addition, the proposed DRA-HTR establishes a new state-of-the-art for domain adaptation across all seven datasets and substantially improves overall robustness and training stability compared to the original DRANet framework: it reduces the Character Error Rate (CER) in average by 13.52 percentage points, and the average standard deviation decreases from 7.23 to 1.11 percentage points. Finally, post-HTR correction further reduces the CER by 2.16 percentage points.|[2611.18143](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18143)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ALoMa: Affine Correspondences Beyond Patches**|David Nordstr\"om et.al.|Affine correspondences (ACs) enable robust and efficient solvers for multiple-view geometry by constraining the local geometry of point correspondences (PCs). Despite their utility in minimal solvers, AC estimation remains understudied. We revisit AC estimation beyond local image patches, extending it to the image and image-pair levels. Rather than estimating the components of the affine transformation separately, we learn the full 4-DoF matrix jointly. Building on this formulation, we introduce ALoMa, an AC estimator using LoMa keypoint descriptions, demonstrating consistent improvements across a wide range of downstream tasks. Code at https://github.com/davnords/loma.|[2611.18170](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18170)|
|**2026-11-16**|**STaG: Human-Annotation-Free Unsupervised Domain Adaptation for Text-Based Person Retrieval**|Ji Yucheng et.al.|Real-world deployment of Text-Based Person Retrieval (TBPR) is bottlenecked by the absence of textual annotations in new domains. Existing Unsupervised Domain Adaptation (UDA-TBPR) methods still assume access to target-domain human-written annotations (albeit unpaired with images), limiting their practicality. We consider a more practical setting: human-annotation-free UDA-TBPR, with labeled image-text data in the source domain and annotation-free, unlabeled images in the target domain. This setting introduces two core challenges: the absence of cross-modal alignment in the target domain, and visual domain shifts from source to target. To tackle these, we propose STaG, a dual-path framework comprising Style-Agnostic Text Alignment (SATA) and Heterogeneous Graph Memory Alignment (HGMA). Specifically, SATA leverages source-domain data to decouple identity-discriminative semantics from machine-generated stylistic artifacts, establishing robust cross-modal alignment for the target domain. Meanwhile, HGMA exploits cross-domain semantic neighborhoods via a memory-based graph to learn domain-invariant visual features. Ultimately, STaG achieves state-of-the-art performance across three benchmarks under this realistic setting.|[2611.18172](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18172)|
|**2026-11-16**|**SemRAG: Semantic-Guided Sentence-Level Retrieval-Augmented Sign Language Production**|Xiao Liu et.al.|Sign language production (SLP) aims to generate sign pose sequences that faithfully express spoken-language text while preserving the rhythm and coordination patterns of signing. Common SLP methods learn text-to-pose mappings from training data but often suffer from semantic inaccuracies in long or complex sentences. Retrieval augmentation offers a way to introduce realistic signing priors to provide more explicit guidance for text-to-pose generation. However, existing retrieval-based SLP methods typically operate at the gloss or word level, relying on gloss-pose dictionaries or isolated sign segments that require costly annotations and fail to preserve continuous sentence-level dynamics. To bridge this gap, we propose SemRAG, a semantic-guided sentence-level retrieval-augmented framework that retrieves full-sentence signing templates as priors without constructing gloss/word-level sign dictionaries. While such templates preserve local motion patterns and global rhythm, they also introduce a semantic mismatch problem: irrelevant motions in the retrieved sentence may interfere with the target semantics. To address this issue, we introduce an SLT-guided template semantic localization module that identifies target-relevant temporal regions within the retrieved template while suppressing irrelevant signing content. To adaptively incorporate the template features into the generator, we further introduce layer-specific learnable gates that regulate their contribution across generator layers. Extensive experiments show that SemRAG improves semantic consistency and motion quality over baselines under both back-translation and motion accuracy metrics.|[2611.18189](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18189)|
|**2026-11-16**|**SCAR: Bridging Coarse Retrieval and Fine Localization through Candidate Reranking**|Shuhan Dong et.al.|Natural-language point-cloud localization typically retrieves candidate map cells and then predicts a position inside each candidate, yet the final output is still determined by retrieval order. A retrieval score measures region-description relevance, not whether the coordinates a region yields are accurate: a relevant region may produce a displaced prediction, and a lower-ranked region may contain a successful one. We study the final selection problem after the candidate pool and Fine predictions are fixed---how to choose the coordinate most likely to localize successfully, and under which conditions the gain holds. We propose SCAR (Spatial Consistency-Aware Reranking). SCAR treats each cell--Fine-coordinate pair as a localization hypothesis, interprets directional relations as functions of position, and reads spatial support at the backbone's actual Fine point (Fine-conditioned Evidence Readout). It organizes this readout with center support, semantic presence, retrieval score, and geometric offsets into a 23-D representation, trains a shared scorer with localization-success labels, and at inference reranks existing hypotheses and returns their original coordinates. Experiments cover four public backbones and a corrected Des4Pos reimplementation across five domains. Excluding one protocol-reference configuration, exploratory TEST point estimates on the remaining 24 configurations show Top-1@5\,m above Original on 23 and Cell R@1 above Original on all 24. On development-set VAL, the Fine readout improves Top-1@5\,m by 5.77 percentage points (pp) over a center surrogate on corrected Des4Pos*KITTI, and the complete scoring scheme improves it by 13.98\,pp over FSR on MambaPlace$\times$nuScenesFull; the latter measures the complete scheme, not learned ranking alone.|[2611.18240](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18240)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Lens: Training-Free Semantic Search for Cross-Tile Object and Scene Retrieval in Satellite Imagery**|Ata Akbari Asanjan et.al.|We developed Lens, a training-free system for semantic object and scene search across large satellite mosaics that requires no detector trained for the class of interest. Lens represents each tile with a frozen, self-supervised vision transformer (DINOv3), removes the positional bias in its dense features by projecting them onto the subspace orthogonal to a positional basis estimated once from a constant image, and centers the debiased features on the mean feature computed across the whole area before comparison. Lens forms a query by averaging the centered features of a small neighborhood of patches around a chosen point, or of an entire tile for scene-level search, and matches it against the index by cosine similarity. It stores the centered patch features in a byte-quantized similarity index that supports real-time range search over a mosaic of thousands of patches, and a coarser whole-tile embedding index for scene-level retrieval. A guided upsampling network (AnyUp) runs once per tile to recover pixel-accurate boundaries beyond the native feature grid, and the result is cached so later queries reuse it. We evaluated Lens on the 5,128-tile DFC2020 test set and ran it as an interactive tool on five Sentinel-2 areas spanning agricultural, industrial, and desert terrain; the pipeline is not tied to a specific sensor. On DFC2020 a single click reaches 0.09 to 0.80 pixel F1 across eight land-cover classes at the per-class best threshold, with a mean F1 of 0.39, and it beats a pairwise INSID3 baseline on six of the eight classes at roughly four hundred times lower query cost. Indexing 1.31 million patches takes 169 s on one CPU thread, and a click returns a rendered overlay in 6 to 10 ms.|[2611.18251](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18251)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Deep Bundle Adjustment**|Pak Hong Chui et.al.|We present \textbf{DeepBA} (\textbf{Deep} \textbf{B}undle \textbf{A}djustment), a graph neural network (GNN) that recovers camera poses, intrinsics, and scene structure from 2D matches across an arbitrary number of views. Rather than solving a non-linear least-squares problem per scene, DeepBA amortizes bundle adjustment into a learned graph network that iteratively refines its estimate from geometric residuals. Where classical SfM computes its updates analytically and needs careful initialization, DeepBA learns them and converges from a cold start. It is trained with scale-invariant supervision (ground-truth rotation, translation direction, and intrinsics, plus reprojection self-consistency), so datasets with arbitrary metric units can be mixed without per-scene normalization. DeepBA is decoupled from the matcher. At 2.04M parameters, it outperforms prior learned SfM from matches on scene reconstruction and on most pose metrics, is competitive with classical SfM at moderate accuracy thresholds and overtakes it in wall time as the view count grows, and approaches monolithic image-to-geometry transformers such as VGGT ($\sim$1.2B parameters) with greater modularity. Project~page: https://cphjsn.github.io/deep-ba/|[2611.18256](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18256)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Structural Self-Teaching for Compositional Generation in Unified Multimodal Models**|Jaeyoo Park et.al.|We propose Structural Self-Teaching (SST), a novel framework that enhances compositional image generation in diffusion-based unified multimodal models (UMMs) by leveraging their internal understanding path as a source of structural supervision. The generation path of UMMs often struggles with dense compositional prompts, leading to missing objects, attribute binding failures, and spatial errors. In contrast, their understanding path localizes the same phrases in images that satisfy these prompts. SST converts this phrase-level grounding into training-time structural signals for the generation path. Specifically, we extract phrase-conditioned support masks from internal attention maps derived from the understanding path and employ them through two mechanisms. Phrase-local contrastive alignment uses these masks as pooling regions and aligns each generated phrase region with the same region of the target image. A structural scaffold builds a phrase-level layout from the same masks and modulates an intermediate spatial state of the generation path during training. The scaffold is phased out during training and removed at inference. SST requires no external structural annotations for training and no additional input at test time. Extensive experiments across multiple UMMs demonstrate that SST improves compositional fidelity over LoRA fine-tuning on the same data, with no inference-time cost. Project page is available at https://bellos1203.github.io/sst|[2611.18261](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18261)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Unlocking Cross-Attention in Label-Scarce Regimes: A Weakly Supervised Approach for Video Semantic Segmentation**|Andrea Marelli et.al.|Weakly Supervised Video Semantic Segmentation (WS-VSS) aims to learn temporally consistent semantic object masks in videos using only video-level labels. While fully supervised models successfully learn temporal relationships by means of cross-attention, training these temporal weights under weak supervision is unstable and even pushes the model below its single-frame baseline. Therefore, existing WS-VSS methods exploit temporal consistency by relying on external priors, such as optical flow. Unfortunately these cues are non-semantic, computationally expensive and noisy, degrading the supervisory signal. Instead of supervising attention weights directly or using external priors, we propose MemCAM, a WS-VSS transformer framework that learns temporal cross-attention directly through feature-space self-supervision. MemCAM introduces a CAM Propagation Algorithm that synthesizes a supervisory signal by propagating past CAMs according to patch-level feature similarities in the latent space. Specifically, we constrain their induced CAMs through a consistency objective between predicted and propagated maps, stabilizing temporal attention learning. Experiments on the YouTube-Objects v2.2 and WS$^2$ datasets demonstrate that MemCAM consistently outperforms the temporal localization and segmentation performance of WS-VSS alternatives, showing that cross-attention can be effectively trained in label-scarce video settings through internal self-supervision.|[2611.18324](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18324)|
|**2026-11-16**|**DIRT: Depth-completed Image-based Rendering using Two-band Blending for Live Novel-view Synthesis**|Mathias Harrer et.al.|State-of-the-art novel-view synthesis (NVS) methods commonly require per-scene optimization before rendering, limiting their applicability in live settings. Live or ad-hoc NVS, however, demands low-latency rendering during capture and strong generalization to new scenes. Some previous work tried to address this task by adapting training- and optimization-based methods, trading quality for low latency. We introduce a live NVS approach following traditional image-based rendering that operates on a posed set of depth sensor observations and images. Our method leverages explicit, sparse point-based geometry obtained from active sensors, resulting in a sparsely filled depth buffer that is completed in the (novel) target view's screen-space. Using this proxy, a posed set of images is projected into the target frame before fusing the resulting observations through a frequency-aware two-band blending strategy. The pipeline is entirely non-neural and optimization-free, requiring no training data or scene-specific optimization. As a result, our approach reduces the delay between capture and novel-view rendering and does not rely on favorable network generalization. We validate the method's applicability to heterogeneous sensor setups - including handheld RGB-D cameras, automotive LiDAR, and thermal cameras - as well as challenging scenarios, such as low light captures. Our experiments demonstrate that the approach outperforms prior methods in live reconstruction scenarios, while maintaining competitive performance in conventional NVS benchmarks.|[2611.18340](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18340)|
|**2026-11-16**|**MaskSCD: Bitemporal Mask Classification for Semantic Change Detection in Remote Sensing Images**|Weikang Yu et.al.|Semantic change detection (SCD) identifies where the land surface has changed and which land-cover transition has taken place. Most deep SCD networks predict the change map and the land-cover map of each date with separate per-pixel decoders. These outputs need not agree, so a pixel can be labeled as changed although it has the same class at both dates, and per-pixel decisions fragment objects. We present MaskSCD, which formulates SCD as the mask classification of a bitemporal label-pair image. Each bitemporal segment, a region of constant land-cover class pair, is predicted as one mask shared by both dates together with one class per date, and the change map is derived from the predicted class pair. This formulation keeps the change and semantic maps consistent and the label space linear in the number of classes. A bitemporal masked-attention transformer decoder implements it by reading each segment in both images and relating the two readings, and a bitemporal fusion pyramid provides per-date and change-aware features. MaskSCD is evaluated on SECOND, Landsat-SCD and Hi-UCD, which span 0.1 to 30 m resolution and four to nine land-cover classes, against fourteen recent SCD models re-trained under one protocol. MaskSCD achieves the best separated kappa, semantic change $F_1$ and transition $F_1$ on all three datasets and exceeds the strongest competitor by about five points in mean separated kappa. It produces no contradictory change pixels, which account for nearly 30% of the predicted change of some multi-task networks. The code is available at https://github.com/EricYu97/MaskSCD.|[2611.18350](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18350)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Adaptable Watermarking: Transforming Image Watermarking Models via Lightweight Adapters**|Mehrdad Saberi et.al.|We explore the adaptability of neural image watermarking models; the extent to which one watermarking method can be transformed into another through lightweight adaptation. We introduce a framework that enables encoder-decoder watermarking architectures to become modular and upgradable, supporting multiple watermarking styles without re-training full models. Adaptation is performed by attaching a compact parameter-efficient adapter and fine-tuning under a multi-domain residual alignment loss (spatial, gradient, and frequency) while preserving bit accuracy via a secret-loss term. We evaluate this approach across a suite of recent watermarking models, including TrustMark, InvisMark, RoSteALS, HiDDeN, and WAM, demonstrating that adapted encoders closely reproduce target residuals and that adapted decoders recover messages with near-optimal accuracy under standard augmentations and distortions. We show that cross-model adaptation, with applications in interoperable and upgradable watermarking, can be achieved with as few as 1-2M trainable parameters per component (e.g., the 2M decoder adapter used in our multi-decoder inference pipeline).|[2611.18363](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18363)|
|**2026-11-16**|**AI-Based Multi-View Echocardiographic Image Synthesis using Dynamic Shape Control**|Jo\~ao Freitas et.al.|Although automated methods have improved echocardiographic workflows, progress remains constrained by the need for large, well-annotated datasets. As the development of clinical databases is often limited by ethical and logistical challenges, synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising alternative. However, existing approaches commonly rely on left ventricle shape-based supervision or are tailored to specific cardiac views, limiting the generation of additional standard and unconventional imaging planes and the preservation of multi-view consistency across cardiac chambers. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework for shape-controlled synthesis of multi-view echocardiographic sequences. First, multi-view cross-sections are extracted from dynamic whole-heart meshes. Ultrasound-like appearance is then introduced through a two-stage AI-based pipeline. In Stage 1, an unpaired GAN-diffusion model translates these cross-sections into realistic echocardiographic frames. As this translation is performed independently at the frame level, Stage 2 employs masked video reconstruction to improve temporal coherence and cardiac motion. Evaluation demonstrated high frame-level realism for both stages, while the integration of Stage 2 substantially improved video-level motion quality and temporal consistency. Overall, the proposed framework enables the generation of realistic multi-view echocardiographic sequences from diverse cardiac models, providing a scalable source of annotated data for dataset augmentation and the development of multi-view cardiac assessment methods.|[2611.18375](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18375)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning**|Tao Hu et.al.|Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.|[2611.18417](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18417)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Do Underwater Image Quality Metrics Agree with Human Perception? A Benchmark with Human Choices, Simple Edits and Generative Drift**|Rishii Kumar Singh et.al.|Underwater image enhancement is challenging to evaluate due to the lack of clean reference images, so researchers rely on no-reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) metrics. However, it remains unclear whether these metrics agree with human judgments. We evaluate 73 handcrafted underwater, learned underwater, vision-language and general-purpose metrics against human preferences on five references: four published opinion-score sets and a new paired-comparison study. For each metric, we measure how often it selects the same image as people when comparing versions of the same scene. We further test whether simple image edits and small adversarial perturbations raise metric scores above the enhancement people preferred, and whether scores decrease as repeated generative edits add noise. Our results show that no metric consistently matches human preferences. The best worst-case Cohen's kappa over the five references is only 0.23 (Q-Align), while two raters in our study agree with a median kappa of 0.41. The widely used UCIQE often favors edits that people reject. On a Nano Banana Pro replica chain that should leave the image unchanged, only 22 of 73 scores decrease at every checkpoint, while noise grows by 2.5 times. We also introduce TrustUIQA, a benchmark that combines the human references, our study choices, the edited, attacked and generated images, and the scores of every metric on every image. The code is available at https://github.com/TrustUIQA/TrustUIQA and TrustUIQA at https://huggingface.co/datasets/TrustUIQA/TrustUIQA.|[2611.18429](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18429)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Toward On-Device Markerless 6DoF Tracking of a Physical Manipulative on a Mixed-Reality Headset: A Preliminary Study**|Guangxi Feng et.al.|AR-Classroom uses physical models and virtual overlays to help learners explore spatial transformations. To align these overlays on a standalone headset, we developed a markerless six-degree-of-freedom(6DoF) tracker for a LEGO airplane on the Meta Quest 3. A compact YOLO model trained on synthetic Unity renders predicts 20 keypoints from dynamic RGB crops resized to 128 by 128 pixels. A perspective-n-point solver estimates the airplane's pose from these predictions and the corresponding 3D landmarks. In a paired evaluation on 1,000 synthetic validation images, cropping guided by ground-truth boxes increased the detection rate on annotated images from 32.5 to 46.2 percent. Mean keypoint F1 increased from 42.9 to 44.6 percent, with lower keypoint precision in the cropped view. In preliminary headset tests, 2 Hz was the highest tested tracking target at which the application maintained approximately 72 frames per second. A headset demonstration illustrates virtual overlay alignment with the physical airplane before and after a manual rotation. Quantitative 6DoF pose accuracy on real headset footage will be measured in future work.|[2611.18438](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18438)|
|**2026-11-16**|**MMFlash: Faster Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models**|Sicheng Feng et.al.|Speculative decoding accelerates generation by letting a lightweight drafter propose tokens for parallel verification by a target model. Diffusion drafters such as DFlash propose an entire token block in a single forward pass, but mainly focus on text-only tasks, leaving multimodal drafting underexplored. To study this setting, we first introduce a metric of token-level visual dependence based on the KL divergence between next-token distributions with and without visual input. We find that acceptance rates decrease from 46% in the lowest visual-dependence quartile to 22% in the highest, with this disparity persisting after multimodal training. Motivated by this observation, we propose MMFlash, a simple and effective framework that adaptively upweights visually dependent tokens that the drafter predicts poorly. For long video inputs, MMFlash further employs sparse attention to reduce redundant attention computation and improve drafting quality. Across various multimodal benchmarks, MMFlash achieves lossless decoding speedups of up to $3.58\times$, outperforming EAGLE-3 and DFlash by up to 195.4% and 17.1%, respectively.|[2611.18482](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18482)|
|**2026-11-16**|**StreamEmbed: Real-Time Indexing from Pixels for Screenshot Retrieval**|Dayoon Ko et.al.|Screen history can serve as searchable visual memory for people and agents, but unlike an offline corpus, it must be encoded and indexed as each screenshot arrives. OCR-based indexing is fast but loses visual information; visual encoding takes over two seconds. Yet screens change incrementally: most pixels repeat within and across screenshots. This raises a question: do visual encoders need to recompute their representations from scratch for every new screenshot? We present StreamEmbed, a system for incremental visual embedding that exploits this redundancy. Guided solely by pixel differences, it combines spatial token pruning, which skips blocks that repeat a neighbor, with temporal KV reuse, which reuses cached keys and values for blocks unchanged across screenshots. A small learned adapter compensates for pruning and reuse to preserve fidelity to full-screenshot embeddings. On ScreenMemEval, our benchmark of 10,426 questions over 184 hours of screen histories from two humans and a computer-use agent, StreamEmbed sets a new accuracy-latency Pareto frontier on an M2 Pro GPU: it encodes up to 3.7x faster than full-screenshot encoding without sacrificing accuracy and improves accuracy over OCR-based indexing by up to 6.3 Recall@10 points at matched latency. A memory-efficient vector index stores a simulated year of screenshots in 0.13 GB of RAM. Together, these results make real-time indexing of screen history in its native visual form practical, laying a foundation for fully local visual assistants.|[2611.18516](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18516)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection**|Donghoon Lee et.al.|Detecting out-of-distribution (OOD) objects is essential for ensuring the reliability and safety of object detectors in real-world deployment. We treat the decision on a detected box as a hypothesis test with the null hypothesis that the box covers an object of an in-distribution (ID) class. The null distribution changes with the size of the box and its contact with the image border, and the detector observes both. The test can therefore rank a box only against ID boxes of the same size and border contact, which gives a distribution-free bound on the false-alarm rate for each of them and uncovers the separation that one null distribution hides. The power of the test also rests on the layer it reads. Layers built only for the ID classes have no reason to keep what sets an unknown object apart, so the test reads the features before them. We propose \textbf{PLUM}, a training-free score that pools the last backbone stage inside the box and ranks its distance to the ID classes among ID boxes of the same size and border contact. On the standard benchmark, PLUM reaches the highest AUROC among the compared methods without external models, with 91.5 and 94.5 on PASCAL-VOC and 95.1 and 97.5 on BDD100K. Code: https://github.com/DDOZMI/PLUM|[2611.18519](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18519)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction**|Abhilekha Dalal et.al.|Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) can classify images using background-dominated patches that contain little or none of the target object, showing that they exploit contextual information. Previous studies detected this behavior through classification accuracy but say little about what CNN hidden layers represent. We investigate whether Concept Induction can reveal interpretable visual properties associated with hidden-neuron activations on contextual image patches. Using five agricultural datasets with high, moderate, and low contextual bias, we analyze four CNN architectures at three network depths and five patch locations. Quantitative image descriptors of each patch are encoded in a knowledge graph and used as background knowledge for Concept Induction, which derives logical expressions characterizing neuron activation. These expressions are tested on held-out images using the Mann-Whitney U test and Cliff's delta. Of 48,013 tested neuron-feature pairs, 13,164 (27.4%) were validated. Validation was most frequent at intermediate depths (mean 40.2% at mid layers versus 12.0% at deep layers), and no patch location dominated across datasets. Among the tested descriptors, edge, Gabor, and Tamura texture features had the highest validation rates. Stronger contextual bias did not correspond to higher neuron-feature validation rates: moderately biased Sugarcane had the highest rate (40.5%) and highly biased Cassava the lowest (19.2%). These results suggest that behavioral context dependence and the semantic interpretability of hidden neurons capture different aspects of CNN behavior, and that Concept Induction can complement patch-based bias detection.|[2611.18530](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18530)|
|**2026-11-16**|**JVT: Just Video Transformer makes pixel-space video diffusion simple**|Jianjun Zhou et.al.|Direct diffusion modeling of high-dimensional raw video data is challenging, and existing pixel-space methods often rely on specialized architectures. We introduce JVT (Just Video Transformer), a simple large-patch DiT-style Transformer for pixel-space video diffusion, without a pretrained autoencoder, auxiliary detailers, cascaded stages, or U-Net-style skip connections. We systematically examine video patch embeddings, register tokens, and adaptive layer normalization to identify design choices that balance generation quality and computational efficiency. The resulting JVT design achieves generation quality competitive with latent diffusion baselines in both class-conditioned and unconditional settings. The pixel-space formulation of JVT also naturally supports joint generation of pixel-aligned modalities. We demonstrate this capability through camera-conditioned RGB-disparity generation, which improves camera following relative to RGB-only baselines and yields explicit scene geometry without an additional reconstruction model at inference. These results highlight the potential of simple DiT-style Transformers for pixel-space video diffusion, and we hope our findings offer useful insights and practical guidance for future research.|[2611.18549](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18549)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Restoring Diversity in Distilled Image and Video Generators by Probing Activations**|Furkan Horoz et.al.|Distilling diffusion models into few-step student models substantially reduces sampling latency, but induces severe diversity collapse: samples generated from different random seeds show minimal variation. In this work, we investigate the root cause of this failure by probing the internal representations of student models relative to their teachers. We find that distilled students maintain total activation variance comparable to their teachers, but concentrate it into substantially fewer directions. This directional collapse is localized to an identifiable intermediate block at the initial denoising step. Consequently, diversity collapse stems from a misallocation of activation variance rather than an inherent loss of generative capacity. Guided by this finding, we propose ReDiverse to reinstate the missing variance directions. At training time, ReDiverse-FT fine-tunes a lightweight adapter that regresses student activations onto the teacher's, reviving the teacher's feature variance characteristic. At inference time, ReDiverse-TF introduces a training-free mechanism that steers activations along precomputed teacher principal directions. Across image and video distillation benchmarks, ReDiverse consistently restores sample diversity while preserving generation quality, further supported by the user study.|[2611.18550](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18550)|
|**2026-11-16**|**BAL: Bidirectional Autoregression in Latent Space for Learning Human Movement Representations**|Genki Kinoshita et.al.|Learning semantically meaningful human movement representations from 3D pose sequences is essential for human behavior modeling. Existing self-supervised methods derive supervision from artificial assumptions, e.g., invariance to augmentation within a certain range or recoverability of masked regions at a certain temporal scale, introducing heuristic hyperparameters that make the learned semantics sensitive to their settings. We propose BAL, a self-supervised framework that instead exploits supervision intrinsic to human movement: the natural ordering structure of everyday behavior. BAL autoregressively predicts in a semantically abstract latent space, analogous to how humans anticipate others' actions rather than their precise joint configurations. Bidirectional autoregressive prediction, both forward and backward in time, fully leverages these ordering constraints while enabling representation extraction using both past and future context at inference. To suppress representation collapse, BAL decomposes each prediction target into a global component capturing sequence-level semantics and a local component capturing context-independent movement. We prove that the local targets retain nontrivial angular diversity unless the representations are completely collapsed, preserving a meaningful training signal. Experiments on large-scale datasets demonstrate that BAL's representations are highly effective for motion captioning, forecasting, and interpolation, outperforming baselines on most evaluation metrics.|[2611.18559](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18559)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Beyond Attack-Based Accuracy: Rethinking Adversarial Robustness Evaluation Through Structural Gradient Alignment**|Hanrui Wang et.al.|Visual adversarial robustness remains a fundamental security property as vision systems evolve from conventional classifiers to vision-language models (VLMs) and large vision-language models (LVLMs), with visual inputs remaining an attack surface across generations. Yet existing evaluation methods often depend on prescribed attacks, in-distribution data, extensive probing, or classifier-specific formulations, limiting external auditing of diverse models. We introduce Structural Gradient Alignment (SGA), a complementary robustness metric measuring the structural alignment between input content and objective-conditioned local model sensitivity. SGA uses a few randomly sampled, unlabeled public probes that need not follow the downstream task distribution and only one input-gradient computation per model--probe pair. Across 38 distinct DNNs, VLMs, and LVLMs, SGA produces rankings consistent with attack-based references. Without robustness references, uncalibrated SGA achieves $83.72\%$ average pairwise agreement across eight public-probe conditions, outperforming the strongest overall baseline in all eight by $9.38$ percentage points on average. With known robustness orders of anchor models, calibrated SGA increases agreement to $85.36\%$. These results demonstrate an attack-agnostic, computationally efficient robustness signal across conventional DNNs, VLMs, and autoregressive LVLMs, downstream tasks, threat norms, probe distributions, and evaluator-knowledge settings. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/azrealwang/SGA.|[2611.17377](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17377)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search**|Maitree Meher et.al.|A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.|[2611.17442](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17442)|
|**2026-11-16**|**A thorough review on Deep Learning approaches to 2D-to-3D Registration in Medical Imaging**|Andrea Borghesi et.al.|2D-to-3D image registration is a key component for several medical imaging purposes such as intraoperative guidance, enabling the alignment between preoperative 3D volumes and real-time 2D images. While conventional optimisation-based methods often struggle with non-convex objectives, sensitivity to initialisation, and high computational demands, deep learning (DL) has emerged as a promising alternative, offering improved alignment robustness and the potential for real-time performance. This review provides a comprehensive analysis of DL frameworks developed to address the challenges inherent in 2D-to-3D registration. We first outline how core concepts of DL are adapted to the registration problem. We then examine recent methodological advancements from multiple perspectives, including pipeline design, architectural innovations, and domain adaptation strategies. Particular attention is given to quantitative metrics, highlighting how performance is defined and measured across studies. Across the 41 included studies, we find that direct regression is the most common pipeline (23 studies, against 18 iterative ones), that hybrid CNN-attention architectures are gaining ground, and that research remains heavily concentrated on X-ray-to-CT registration. We conclude by identifying the absence of per-case uncertainty estimation and the strong neglect of temporal or sequential modelling as the two methodological gaps most consequential for clinical readiness, and discuss state-space models as a way to add temporal memory within intraoperative latency budgets. Further opportunities lie in more principled domain adaptation and in the integration of foundation models for representation learning.|[2611.17468](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17468)|
|**2026-11-16**|**X-Nav Technical Report: Real-World Long-Horizon Indoor-to-Outdoor Navigation on a Humanoid Robot**|Jiaxin Huang et.al.|Long-horizon navigation from indoors to outdoors remains an open problem for humanoid robots: one instruction covers rooms, doors and roads, and the camera moves with the gait. Worken, AGIBOT's measure of productive robot work, depends on robots that reach their workplaces autonomously. We present X-Nav, to our knowledge the first system to take a bipedal humanoid through a language-instructed long-horizon mission from indoors to outdoors. X-Nav covers a pipeline from data to deployment. Its data engine collects navigation data in open-source scenes, in digital twins of deployment sites and on real robots. Its navigation model, MELIOR, takes only RGB images from the robot's built-in camera and comes in two versions: MELIOR-A predicts actions, and MELIOR-N predicts a navigation goal. Its agentic workflow, X-Navarch, turns a brief human instruction into sub-tasks and deploys MELIOR on the robot with explicit target navigation and obstacle avoidance. On the Lingxi X2, X-Nav completes a mission of about a hundred metres from an office lobby to a store in a neighbouring building; LiDAR and odometry are used for target approach and low-level control. The mission is completed with both versions of MELIOR: the robot follows a change of destination and walks through a door from the lobby to the road, and with X-Navarch it also passes static and dynamic obstacles on the route. During deployment, MELIOR is adapted with data collected in a digital twin of the site; the adaptation raises its closed-loop success rate in the twin, and the adapted model is then deployed on the robot at the real site. In addition, MELIOR achieves competitive results on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE benchmarks.|[2611.17598](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17598)|
|**2026-11-16**|**LightTAR: Light and Fast Target-Aware Representations for Multimodal Tabular Learning**|Eilam Shapira et.al.|Multimodal tabular learning involves classification and regression on tables combining structured numerical and categorical features with unstructured text and images. These unstructured fields are commonly represented by pretrained encoder embeddings reduced with principal component analysis (PCA). This reduction prioritizes input variance but may overlook information relevant to predicting the target. We introduce LightTAR, a simple, inexpensive method that adapts dimensionality reduction to the prediction task. LightTAR augments the standard PCA representation with a few target-aware features extracted jointly from the final and an intermediate encoder layer using regularized partial least squares (PLS). We evaluate LightTAR with 21 tabular models, including tree ensembles, neural networks, and tabular foundation models. LightTAR improves average predictive performance for all models and, averaged across models, improves prediction on almost every MulTaBench dataset. These gains come with a small increase in total runtime relative to PCA representations. LightTAR integrates directly into existing encoding pipelines, improving tabular learners' predictive performance without modifying the encoder or the learner.|[2611.17600](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17600)|
|**2026-11-16**|**A Tumour-Aware Multimodal Framework for Explainable Prediction of IDH Mutation in Gliomas from Multi-Sequence MRI**|Tahsien Al-Quraishi et.al.|Accurate prediction of isocitrate dehydrogenase (IDH) mutation status is essential for glioma diagnosis, prognosis, and treatment planning. Although recent deep learning approaches have demonstrated promising performance, many rely on whole-brain feature extraction and provide limited interpretability. This study proposes a tumour-aware multimodal framework for explainable, non-invasive prediction of IDH mutation status from multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). The proposed framework integrates four MRI sequences (T1, T1CE, T2, and FLAIR), tumour subregion segmentation, quantitative radiomic descriptors, and clinical information within a unified deep learning architecture. An ablation study was conducted to evaluate the contribution of tumour-aware learning, radiomic features, and clinical variables. Model performance was assessed using multiple complementary evaluation metrics, including accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, balanced accuracy, receiver operating characteristic analysis, confusion matrix evaluation, and Grad-CAM visualisation. Experimental results demonstrated that the proposed multimodal framework consistently outperformed the baseline MRI-only model, with the Clinical Fusion model achieving the strongest overall predictive performance. The integration of tumour-aware learning, radiomic descriptors, and clinical information progressively improved classification performance, while Grad-CAM visualisations indicated that the model predominantly focused on biologically relevant tumour regions. These findings suggest that tumour-aware multimodal feature integration enables accurate, interpretable, and non-invasive molecular profiling of gliomas and provides a promising foundation for future clinical decision support in precision neuro-oncology.|[2611.17712](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17712)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Ego2Flow: Benchmarking the Traffic-System Impact of Autonomous Driving on Maps Distilled from Real-World Roads**|Yu Dong et.al.|End-to-end autonomous driving is predominantly evaluated through ego-vehicle safety and task completion. Its deployment in urban traffic, however, creates sustained interactions among vehicles whose collective effects remain insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Ego2Flow, a closed-loop benchmark for assessing the traffic-system impact of autonomous driving on maps distilled from real-world cities. To construct realistic 3D urban environments for sustained closed-loop traffic simulation, we develop an agent-guided map distillation framework that integrates real-world maps and observations with complementary geographic information. Through an iterative feedback mechanism and a traffic information graph that jointly guide scene generation, the framework preserves lane topology, critical road geometry and decision-relevant visual conditions while simplifying nonessential details and completing unobserved regions. Using our proposed framework, we generate and publicly release six CARLA maps from the Argoverse 2 dataset, covering the corresponding real-world regions and enabling paired comparisons of driving behavior under real and simulated observations. On these maps, Ego2Flow evaluates how autonomous driving policies affect traffic efficiency, safety, stability and energy consumption across diverse road structures. Compared with existing construction methods, our framework achieves the lowest average displacement error between planning outputs from paired real and simulated observations. We further benchmark representative driving policies to characterize their system-level impact on urban traffic. Project Page: https://github.com/Fisher-007/Ego2Flow.|[2611.17720](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17720)|
|**2026-11-16**|**X-Strata: Separating Aneurysms and Overlapping Vessels into Layers from 2D X-ray Angiography**|Zhihong Wen et.al.|An X-ray angiogram sums every structure along a ray into one pixel. When vessels cross an aneurysm, they obscure part of it and mix their contrast into its filling curve. We present X-Strata, a network that separates a cine angiogram into three layers: the complete aneurysm sac, the vessels in front of it, and the vessels behind it. Contrast timing indicates how much each layer contributes to a pixel, and parallax between two views indicates their order, which a single view cannot determine. X-Strata first estimates the layers and their contrast curves, then uses the second view to assign vessels to the front or back layer, and lifts all layers into 3D. It learns from layer labels projected from same-session 3D rotational angiography, but needs only 2D cine runs and their acquisition geometry at inference. On 220 held-out patients from three centers (122 external), two-view X-Strata ranks first among 14 baselines, including VGGT and C$^2$RV, in recovering the sac where vessels overlap it (Dice 0.693 internal, 0.641 external) and in ordering the layers (balanced accuracy 0.703, 0.659). Its order accuracy exceeds that of all 11 end-to-end baselines by at least 4.1 points internally and 3.3 externally (Holm-adjusted $p < 0.001$) and grows with view separation. Recovered 3D sac surfaces have a median Chamfer distance of 0.34 mm on the internal cohort. X-Strata may therefore provide layered aneurysm and vessel information, including 3D sac shape, for patients who undergo only 2D angiography.|[2611.17743](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17743)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When Does Cross-View Consistency Matter in Multi-View Appearance Augmentation? A Controlled Study for Visuomotor Policies**|Zixuan Jiang et.al.|Multi-view visual augmentation often seeks consistency across cameras, but it is unclear which forms of consistency matter for downstream control. We use simulation-state replay to manipulate cross-view coupling while preserving the per-camera perturbation distributions, physical trajectories, and action labels. Across seven LIBERO manipulation tasks, marginal-matched changes in global appearance, ghost injection, and target color have small average costs for a late-fusion Diffusion Policy: point estimates are near zero or negative, and the largest upper endpoint of the 90% posterior credible intervals is 2.55 percentage points. In contrast, geometrically anti-coupled observations reduce success by 3.30 percentage points relative to coupled observations (90% CrI [0.64, 6.28]), with displacement magnitude, direction marginals, and the number of affected views matched. Camera interventions reveal substantial dependence on wrist-view geometry, while feature analysis shows appearance sensitivity patterns associated with the factors randomized during training. ACT shows a higher target-color cost estimate than DP, with uncertainty that includes zero and sensitivity to task composition. Within the tested tasks and policy configurations, these findings motivate selective consistency constraints: appearance coupling can sometimes be relaxed, whereas task-relevant geometry and cross-view spatial correspondence warrant protection.|[2611.17751](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17751)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Vision-as-a-Tool: Visual Harness for Multimodal Agentic Coding**|Haolin Yang et.al.|Multimodal agentic coding requires robust visual reasoning to analyze observations and evaluate generated artifacts. Current frameworks largely rely on a Thinking-with-Images paradigm, where agents depend on their intrinsic vision to execute visually grounded tasks. However, this architectural coupling introduces a fundamental bottleneck. Agent performance remains strictly bounded by internal perceptual limitations. Furthermore, text-only agents lack native perception. Without specialized tools, they cannot perform visual reasoning. To overcome these constraints, we introduce the Vision-as-a-Tool (VaaT) paradigm. Prior visual tool-use merely manipulates images for an agent's intrinsic vision to read. In contrast, VaaT reframes general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as callable external utilities. As a result, text-only agents can completely offload visual reasoning during agentic execution. Meanwhile, vision-capable agents can dynamically augment their native perception on demand. We instantiate this paradigm in VHarness, a unified framework driven by five atomic actions. We comprehensively evaluate VHarness across Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, and Image-to-3D generation tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that VHarness consistently improves visual fidelity and geometric accuracy across both text-only and multimodal coding agents. These findings highlight that modularizing visual reasoning into callable utilities provides a flexible and scalable foundation for future agentic coding systems.|[2611.17797](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17797)|
|**2026-11-16**|**DiffusionSSD: Achieving Extreme Visual Compression with Dual-stage Diffusion and Cross-level Near-Data Processing**|Kuai Yu et.al.|Growing visual data overwhelms storage on resource-constrained edge devices, making compression essential. Traditional codecs offer inadequate rate-distortion performance, motivating the exploration of diffusion codecs. Diffusion codecs face two limitations: (1) At the algorithm level, diffusion decoders preserve perceptual quality under extreme compression, but require substantial computation. (2) At the architecture level, in NPU-centered devices, neural codecs occupy the NPU, interfering with other AI applications. Meanwhile, the complex codec pipeline incurs long I/O paths, leading to additional overhead and energy cost. To address these issues, we propose DiffusionSSD, a cross-level near-data processing framework for extreme visual data compression on edge devices, using an algorithm-architecture co-design method. Specifically, (1) at algorithm level, we present Dual-stage Diffusion, a lightweight solution tailored for diffusion codecs that employs patchification and single-step diffusion, reducing computation cost while preserving perceptual quality; (2) at architecture level, we introduce a Cross-level Near Data Processing architecture that integrates the proposed computational universal flash storage (cUFS) and LPDDR-PIM, enabling codecs to be entirely offloaded to data side while reducing visual-data movement and avoiding SoC redesign; (3) at system level, combining both the algorithm and the underlying architecture, we propose a Patch-Workload co-aware scheduling scheme to enable more efficient system-level execution. Experiments show that Dual-stage Diffusion maintains comparable perceptual quality while reducing computation cost by up to 92.7%. Compared with NPU+DRAM and near-data-processing design such as SmartSSD, cUFS+PIM improves coding throughput by 2.32 times and 2.91 times on average, respectively.|[2611.17908](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17908)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Measured from the feed: the unsustainable growth of AI video generation**|Tristan Coignion et.al.|Text-to-video generation runs at commercial scale, but its broader environmental impacts remain, to our knowledge, understudied. Providers rarely disclose video volumes or energy needs; we measured the yearly video output of two commercial platforms and built a prospective model of such services' absolute footprint from training to storage. Our projections suggest that reducing emissions depends on both limiting growth in video generation and achieving substantial efficiency gains. Under continued growth, emissions rise nearly seventy-five-fold by 2030 despite technological improvements. When usage remains at 2026 levels, the same improvements reduce emissions by about two-fifths. These estimates are a conservative lower bound: they cover one AI service among many; other services and uses would push the total higher.|[2611.17938](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17938)|
|**2026-11-16**|**D2ToF: One-Step Diffusion for Depth Super-Resolution from Lightweight Single-Photon LiDAR Waveforms**|Pallabjyoti Deka et.al.|Single-photon LiDAR (SPL) sensors offer exceptional temporal resolution and single-photon sensitivity for depth imaging. However, cost-effective SPL arrays typically suffer from extremely low spatial resolutions (e.g. $8\times 8$ pixels), limiting their real-world utility. In this paper, we propose a novel, one-step super-resolution framework, D2ToF, Distilled Diffusion for Time-of-Flight, that bridges the gap between high-fidelity generative modeling and real-time execution. Our approach uses an amortized variational distillation strategy that allows for a measurement-based data consistency term to be incorporated directly into the one-step training objective. This encourages that the synthesized high-resolution depth maps are consistent with the captured low-resolution Time-of-Flight (ToF) histograms. To overcome the severe ill-posedness of upsampling extremely degraded inputs, we introduce a multimodal guidance strategy. By leveraging a foundational monocular depth model to extract structural priors from a high-resolution reflectivity image, our network is guided by a rich triad of the ToF histogram, reflectivity map, and relative depth estimate. Experimental results outperform evaluated baselines, achieving $16\times$ spatial super-resolution (from $8\times 8 \times 100$ histograms to $128\times 128$ depth maps) at an accelerated inference speed of approximately $83$ milliseconds.|[2611.18243](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18243)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Pose-based Neural Clothing for Animated Characters**|Julian N. Heidenreich et.al.|Traditional garment simulation for animated characters is a time-consuming bottleneck for artists due to slow simulations and tedious parameter tunings.Recent data-driven methods offer a powerful alternative, capable of learning from a dataset of simulated data to generate high-quality deformations. A lightweight network design allows data-driven systems to achieve a real-time inference speed. This significantly improves the artist workflow, as they can interactively pose a character and see high-quality folds and wrinkles at animation time, thereby allowing to make creative decisions ahead of time. However, existing neural clothing methods have presented appealing results on datasets based on realistic human models (\eg SMPL), but when trained on animated character datasets, they struggle to generalize to unseen poses. In this paper, we aim at designing neural networks and training strategies to generalize better to unseen poses in real-time to enable the interactive posing applications. We focus on a production-oriented setting where the model is trained for a single garment in a quasi-static setup. Technically, we leverage a smooth, expressive latent space by first training a Variational Auto-Encoder (VAE). We then progressively train the VAE encoder to transition from reconstruction to pose-driven prediction. To guide this prediction, we condition the network on both low-dimensional pose parameters and character body surface displacements. Finally, we introduce a novel local gradient histogram loss that mitigates the over-smoothing effect of reconstruction losses. Extensive evaluations on production-level assets and public datasets demonstrate that our method produces sharp, realistic wrinkles, smooth large-scale drape, and temporal coherence in real-time, offering a robust and easily integrable solution for interactive posing workflows.|[2611.18253](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18253)|
|**2026-11-16**|**TetraCoder: Adaptive Tetrahedral Encoding for Neural Fields**|Daniele Della Pietra et.al.|Implicit and explicit representations of geometry are central in computer graphics applications and come with their pros and cons. With surface sampling being faster on explicit representations and spatial queries being faster on implicit representations, finding efficient conversions between them is a highly desirable property. We present TetraCoder, a single resolution hash-free adaptive tetrahedral encoding that links an explicit tetrahedral spatial encoder with an underlying implicit signed distance field (SDF). The resulting hybrid implicit-explicit space is continuous piecewise affine (CPWA) by design, allowing to obtain an exact correspondence between the implicit SDF and the explicit analytic mesh. TetraCoder's design comes with additional perks, in particular: (a) it scales better than multi-resolution encoding methods due to its single resolution optimizable grid, (b) unlike hash-based methods, it is not affected by collisions, (c) it enables fast tetrahedral ray-tracing, geometry and appearance editing, directly through its adaptive tetrahedral scaffold. We demonstrate the versatility of TetraCoder by showing an improvement over state-of-the-art methods on multiple tasks: 2D/3D fitting from images and point clouds, 3D novel view synthesis, 3D reconstruction, and geometry/appearance editing.|[2611.18267](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18267)|
|**2026-11-16**|**VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation**|Jie Hu et.al.|Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. Yet, existing benchmarks evaluate traversability under a fixed and limited embodiment. We present Vision-Language Accessible Navigation (VLAN), containing 1,000 scenes, 20K navigation trajectories, and 160K keyframes across diverse lighting, environmental conditions, scene semantics, and path geometries. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source at https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN.|[2611.18346](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18346)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems**|Grid Robotics et.al.|The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.|[2611.18359](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent**|Run Luo et.al.|We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.|[2611.18397](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18397)|
|**2026-11-16**|**EventSpikeSeg: Memory-Aware Mixed-Signal Architecture for Event-Camera Spiking Segmentation**|Amir Mohammad Khadem Hosseini et.al.|Event-camera segmentation requires persistent neural state despite sparse input events, making memory organization a central constraint on embedded implementation. We present EventSpikeSeg, a mixed-signal architecture combining resident capacitor memory with shared analog processing in GF180MCU 180-nm CMOS. Its 25-population spiking encoder-decoder has 143,824 synaptic weights but requires 30,976,000 membrane states at 440 x 640 resolution. The mapping specifies chronological 5-ms model updates, digital event/address services and 64-state capacitor banks. Characterization covers 22 module families and 74 operating points, distinguishing transistor simulations from digital-library estimates. Nominal results include 41.711 microwatts biased neuron-core power, 3.338-pJ positive terminal energy for a 1.4-microsecond cell read, and 0.531-mV selected-state drift over a 5-ms extracted-tile hold. Commit and coupling errors are reported separately. The software checkpoint records 27.976% mean intersection-over-union on a held-out six-class DSEC split; this is not hardware accuracy. Component layouts and simulation evidence quantify local implementation costs and expose storage replication and service scheduling as scaling constraints. A detailed SPICE-calibrated behavioral forward simulation of one full-resolution DSEC frame achieves 24.26% mean intersection-over-union, versus 26.22% for the matched PyTorch reference, using a compact 5-fF bank variant and external count/voltage calibration. Physical full-network operation, system energy and mismatch robustness remain unverified.|[2611.18524](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18524)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When No-Reference Image Quality Models Meet MAP Estimation in Diffusion Latents**|Weixia Zhang et.al.|Contemporary no-reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) models can effectively quantify perceived image quality, often achieving strong correlations with human perceptual scores on standard IQA benchmarks. Yet, limited efforts have been devoted to treating NR-IQA models as natural image priors for real-world image enhancement, and consequently comparing them from a perceptual optimization standpoint. In this work, we show -- for the first time -- that NR-IQA models can be plugged into the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation framework for image enhancement. This is achieved by performing gradient ascent in the diffusion latent space rather than in the raw pixel domain, leveraging a pretrained differentiable and bijective diffusion process. Likely, different NR-IQA models lead to different enhanced outputs, which in turn provides a new computational means of comparing them. Unlike conventional correlation-based measures, our comparison method offers complementary insights into the respective strengths and weaknesses of the competing NR-IQA models in perceptual optimization scenarios. Additionally, we aim to improve the best-performing NR-IQA model in diffusion latent MAP estimation by incorporating the advantages of other top-performing methods. The resulting model delivers noticeably better results in enhancing real-world images afflicted by unknown and complex distortions, all preserving a high degree of image fidelity.|[2403.06406](http://arxiv.org/abs/2403.06406)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Evaluating the Generalizability and Explainability of Firn Image Classification with Topological Features**|Jesse Dimino et.al.|In this paper we evaluate the performance of topological features for generalizable and explainable classification of firn image data, with the broader goal of understanding the advantages, pitfalls, and trade-offs in topological featurization. Firn refers to layers of granular snow within glaciers that haven't yet been compressed into ice. This compaction process imposes distinct topological and geometric structure on firn that varies with depth within the firn column. However, the limited and highly correlated training data available results in naive classifiers (such as convolutional neural networks) overfitting to orientation-dependent features. This motivates us to evaluate topological data analysis (TDA) as a way to design features which capture the shape of the firn data while retaining a large degree of invariance. We evaluate two classes of topological features, sublevel set features and distance transform features, together with persistence curves, to predict sample depth from firn images. A range of challenging training-test scenarios reveals that no one choice of method dominates in all categories. In particular, we see a tradeoff between accuracy on the one hand, and generalizability and explainability (via feature importance) on the other.|[2504.16150](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16150)|
|**2026-11-16**|**BusterX++: Towards Unified Cross-Modal AI-Generated Content Detection and Explanation with MLLM**|Haiquan Wen et.al.|The rapid advancement of generative AI has substantially improved image and video synthesis, amplifying the risk of multimodal visual misinformation. Recent MLLMs have shown promise for transparent AI-generated content detection through reasoning and explanation, yet existing approaches largely treat image and video forensics as isolated tasks, leaving cross-modal synergies underexplored. To address this, we present \textbf{BusterX++}, a unified MLLM for joint image and video detection with interpretable reasoning. We also introduce \textbf{GenBuster-Bench++}, a curated benchmark containing balanced image and video samples spanning recent generation models and diverse real-world scenarios, with synthetic images and videos screened using the same human difficulty criterion. Using this controlled setting, we revisit the widely adopted SFT+RL post-training paradigm. Notably, in the unified image-video setting, our results demonstrate that a single-stage pure RL strategy driven primarily by sparse outcome rewards outperforms a strong SFT+RL baseline. We observe that single-stage pure RL maintains higher policy entropy than SFT-initialized RL throughout training. This observation suggests a possible role for exploration in the performance gains observed under unified image-video training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BusterX++ achieves state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the powerful potential of RL for unified cross-modal visual reasoning.|[2507.14632](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14632)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Click, Predict, Trust: Clinician-Guided and Clinician-Evaluated AI Segmentation for Lung Cancer CT-Based Prognosis within the Knowledge-to-Action Framework**|Mohammad R. Salmanpour et.al.|Lung cancer remains the leading cause of cancer mortality, with CT imaging central to screening, prognosis, and treatment. Manual segmentation is variable and time-intensive, while deep learning (DL) offers automation but faces barriers to clinical adoption. Guided by the Knowledge-to-Action framework, this study develops a clinician-in-the-loop DL pipeline to enhance reproducibility, prognostic accuracy, and clinical trust. Multi-center CT data from 999 patients across 12 public datasets were analyzed using five DL models (3D Attention U-Net, ResUNet, VNet, ReconNet, SAM-Med3D), benchmarked against expert contours on whole and click-point cropped images. Segmentation reproducibility was assessed using 497 PySERA-extracted radiomic features via Spearman correlation, ICC, Wilcoxon tests, and MANOVA, while prognostic modeling compared supervised (SL) and semi-supervised learning (SSL) across 38 dimensionality reduction strategies and 24 classifiers. Six physicians qualitatively evaluated masks across seven domains, including clinical meaningfulness, boundary quality, prognostic value, trust, and workflow integration. VNet achieved the best performance (Dice = 0.83, IoU = 0.71), radiomic stability (mean correlation = 0.76, ICC = 0.65), and predictive accuracy under SSL (accuracy = 0.88, F1 = 0.83). SSL consistently outperformed SL across models. Radiologists favored VNet for peritumoral representation and smoother boundaries, preferring AI-generated initial masks for refinement rather than replacement. These results demonstrate that integrating VNet with SSL yields accurate, reproducible, and clinically trusted CT-based lung cancer prognosis, highlighting a feasible path toward physician-centered AI translation.|[2510.17039](http://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17039)|
|**2026-11-16**|**LatentFM: A Latent Flow Matching Approach for Generative Medical Image Segmentation**|Ngoc Huynh Trinh et.al.|Generative models have achieved remarkable progress with the emergence of flow matching (FM). It has demonstrated strong generative capabilities and attracted significant attention as a simulation-free flow-based framework capable of learning exact data densities. Motivated by these advances, we propose LatentFM, a flow-based model operating in the latent space for medical image segmentation. To model the data distribution, we first design two variational autoencoders (VAEs) to encode both medical images and their corresponding masks into a lower-dimensional latent space. We then estimate a conditional velocity field that guides the flow based on the input image. By sampling multiple latent representations, our method synthesizes diverse segmentation outputs whose pixel-wise variance reliably captures the underlying data distribution, enabling both highly accurate and uncertainty-aware predictions. Furthermore, we generate confidence maps that quantify the model certainty, providing clinicians with richer information for deeper analysis. We conduct experiments on two datasets, ISIC-2018 and CVC-Clinic, and compare our method with several prior baselines, including both deterministic and generative approach models. Through comprehensive evaluations, both qualitative and quantitative results show that our approach achieves superior segmentation accuracy while remaining highly efficient in the latent space.|[2512.04821](http://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04821)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Vision-language models lag human performance on physical dynamics and intent reasoning**|Tianjun Gu et.al.|Spatial intelligence is central to embodied cognition, yet contemporary AI systems still struggle to reason about physical interactions in open-world human environments. Despite strong performance on controlled benchmarks, vision-language models often fail to jointly model physical dynamics, reference frames, and the latent human intentions that drive spatial change. We introduce Teleo-Spatial Intelligence (TSI), which captures an important aspect of spatial reasoning: integrating physical dynamics and action goals to reason about actions and their spatial consequences. To evaluate TSI, we present EscherVerse, a large-scale open-world resource built from 11,328 real-world videos, including an 8,000-example benchmark and a 35,963-example instruction-tuning set. Across 27 state-of-the-art vision-language models and an independent analysis of first-pass human responses from 11 annotators, we identify a persistent teleo-spatial reasoning gap: the strongest proprietary model achieves 57.26% overall accuracy, far below first-pass human performance, which ranges from 84.81% to 95.14% with a mean of 90.62%. Fine-tuning on real-world, intent-aware data narrows this gap for open-weight models, but does not close it. EscherVerse provides a diagnostic testbed for purpose-aware spatial reasoning and highlights a critical gap between pattern recognition and human-level understanding in embodied AI.|[2601.01547](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01547)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ISLA: Ensemble learning and domain adaptation for generalizable acute ischemic stroke lesion segmentation in diffusion MRI with clinical validation**|Vincent Roca et.al.|Accurate segmentation of acute ischemic stroke (AIS) lesions on MRI is essential for clinical decision-making. Deep learning methods offer a promising approach to automating this task. However, they often generalize poorly to unseen clinical sites, and few AIS lesion segmentation methods have been externally validated.
