In this study, we propose $\mathit{ISLA}$ (Ischemic Stroke Lesion Analyzer), a framework for AIS lesion segmentation built on the design principles of $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$. $\mathit{ISLA}$ combines attention modules, deep supervision, and ensemble learning across different input resolutions, convolutional block types, and loss functions. We also introduce $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$, a variant that leverages unlabeled target-domain images through semi-supervised learning. We trained both approaches on the SOOP and ISLES 2022 datasets and evaluated on a held-out internal validation set of 253 cases, as well as on 590 cases from an independent multicenter clinical cohort. We compared them with three state-of-the-art pre-trained models and with $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ trained on the same data. $\mathit{ISLA}$ outperformed the pre-trained models on the internal validation set, while both $\mathit{ISLA}$ and $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ outperformed them on the external cohort. Although $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ performed slightly better than $\mathit{ISLA}$ internally, both $\mathit{ISLA}$ variants demonstrated better generalization to the external cohort. Furthermore, $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ significantly improved performance over $\mathit{ISLA}$ on the external cohort. The proposed models performed particularly well on low-contrast lesions, and qualitative error analyses suggested greater robustness to acquisition artifacts. Both models are fast ($\sim$ 5 s per case), and their code and weights are publicly available at https://github.com/RocaVincent/isla_segmentation.|[2601.08732](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08732)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Tracing 3D Anatomy in 2D Strokes: Can Projection-Derived Masks Replace 3D Segmentation for Cervical Spine Fracture Recognition?**|Fabi Nahian Madhurja et.al.|Cervical spine fractures require rapid and accurate diagnosis, yet automatic CT interpretation remains challenging as subtle injuries must be assessed across large 3D volumes. We ask whether full 3D vertebra segmentation is necessary for fracture recognition, or whether vertebra masks approximated from 2D projections preserve sufficient diagnostic context. In the proposed multi-stage pipeline, a YOLOv8 detector localizes the cervical spine from multi-view variance projections (3D mean Intersection over Union 94.45%). Multi-label vertebra segmentation is performed with a DenseNet121-Unet on energy-based sagittal and coronal projections (mean Dice 87.86%). The predicted 2D masks are back-projected and fused into approximate 3D masks per vertebra to extract volumes of interest from the original CT, which are analyzed by an ensemble of 2.5D spatio-sequential CNN-Transformer models. On the full RSNA 2022 cohort at native prevalence (2,019 patients, five-fold cross-validation), the pipeline yields vertebra-level and patient-level F1-scores of 68.15% and 82.28%, area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 91.63% and 90.95%, and area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) of 75.64% and 92.00%. With the classifier architecture fixed, replacing 3D-segmentation masks by projection-derived masks changed vertebra-level and patient-level AUPRC by +1.03 and +0.65 points, and the pipeline differed from the reproduced RSNA 2022 winning solution by -0.55 and +0.15 points. No AUPRC difference was statistically significant, and fold-wise 95% confidence intervals excluded vertebra-level losses larger than 1.46 and 2.81 points, respectively. Explainability and interobserver analyses examine reliability. Projection-based mask approximation is therefore a viable proxy for 3D vertebra segmentation in cervical fracture recognition.|[2601.15235](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15235)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Free-GVC: Toward Retraining-Free Extreme Generative Video Compression with Temporal Coherence**|Xiaoyue Ling et.al.|Building on recent advances in video generation, generative video compression has emerged as a new paradigm for achieving visually pleasing reconstructions. However, existing methods exhibit limited exploitation of temporal correlations, causing noticeable flicker and degraded temporal coherence at ultra-low bitrates. In this paper, we propose Free-GVC, a retraining-free generative video compression framework that reformulates video coding as latent trajectory compression guided by a video diffusion prior. Our method operates at the group-of-pictures (GOP) level, encoding video segments into a compact latent space and progressively compressing them along the diffusion trajectory. To ensure perceptually consistent reconstruction across GOPs, we introduce an Adaptive Quality Control module that dynamically constructs an online rate-perception surrogate model to predict the optimal diffusion step for each GOP. In addition, an Inter-GOP Alignment module establishes frame overlap and performs latent fusion between adjacent groups, thereby mitigating flicker and enhancing temporal coherence. Experiments show that Free-GVC achieves an average of 93.29% BD-Rate reduction in DISTS over the recent neural codec DCVC-RT, and a user study further confirms its superior perceptual quality and temporal coherence at ultra-low bitrates.|[2602.09868](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09868)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Comparative Analysis of 3D Convolutional and 2.5D Slice-Conditioned U-Net Architectures for MRI Super-Resolution via Elucidated Diffusion Models**|Hendrik Chiche et.al.|Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) super-resolution (SR) methods that computationally enhance low-resolution acquisitions offer a software alternative to higher-field scanners. We study an elucidated diffusion model (EDM) framework for 2x in-plane brain MRI SR and compare two U-Net backbones: (i) a 3D convolutional U-Net that processes volumetric patches and (ii) a 2.5D slice-conditioned U-Net that super-resolves each slice while conditioning on its two neighbouring slices. All models are trained from scratch to convergence on 500 subjects of the NKI cohort of FOMO60K, with a separate 6-subject validation set for checkpoint selection, and evaluated on 70 held-out subjects with subject-level statistics against baselines (EDSR, Swin2SR, Res-SRDiff) trained on the same data. Neither architecture is better overall. On the degradation used in training the two are tied (37.72 dB PSNR for the 3D model and 37.70 dB for the 2.5D model), 0.2 dB ahead of EDSR. When the test degradation changes to k-space truncation, EDSR matches the diffusion models, and with added noise the 2.5D model is the most accurate method. When the test cohort changes to 70 subjects of an external dataset (IXI), the 3D model is the most accurate, 0.4 dB ahead of the best baseline and 0.9 dB ahead of the 2.5D model in foreground PSNR.|[2603.14667](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14667)|
|**2026-11-16**|**What DINO saw: ALiBi positional encoding reduces positional bias in Vision Transformers**|Moritz Pawlowsky et.al.|Vision transformers (ViTs) - especially feature foundation models like DINOv2 - learn rich representations useful for many downstream tasks. However, architectural choices (such as positional encoding) can lead to these models displaying positional biases and artefacts independent of semantic content. This makes zero-shot adaption difficult in fields like material science, where images are often cross-sections of homogeneous microstructure (i.e. having no preferred direction). In this work, we investigate the positional bias in ViTs via linear probing, finding it present across a range of objectives and positional encodings, and subsequently reduce it by finetuning models to use ALiBi relative positional encoding. We demonstrate that these models retain desirable general semantics and their unbiased features can be used successfully in trainable segmentation of complex microscopy images.|[2603.16840](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16840)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Open-Set Supervised 3D Anomaly Detection: An Industrial Dataset and a Generalisable Framework for Unknown Defects**|Hanzhe Liang et.al.|Although self-supervised 3D anomaly detection assumes that acquiring high-precision point clouds is computationally expensive, in real manufacturing scenarios it is often feasible to collect a limited number of anomalous samples. Therefore, we study open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection, where the model is trained with only normal samples and a small number of known anomalous samples, aiming to identify unknown anomalies at test time. We present Open-Industry, a high-quality industrial dataset containing 20 categories, each with five real anomaly types collected from production lines. We first adapt general open-set anomaly detection methods to accommodate 3D point cloud inputs better. Building upon this, we propose Open3D-AD, a point-cloud-oriented approach that leverages normal samples, simulated anomalies, and partially observed real anomalies to model the probability density distributions of normal and anomalous data. Then, we introduce a simple Correspondence Distributions Subsampling to reduce the overlap between normal and non-normal distributions, enabling stronger dual distributions modeling. Based on these contributions, we establish a comprehensive benchmark and evaluate the proposed method extensively on Open-Industry as well as established datasets including Real3D-AD and Anomaly-ShapeNet. Benchmark results and ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of Open3D-AD and further reveal the potential of open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection.|[2604.01171](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01171)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Where to Focus: Query-Modulated Multimodal Keyframe Selection for Long Video Understanding**|Shaoguang Wang et.al.|Long video understanding remains a formidable challenge for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) due to the prohibitive cost of processing dense frame sequences. Prevailing keyframe-selection methods rely on either a single visual-centric metric (e.g., CLIP similarity) or a static fusion of heuristic scores. This "one-size-fits-all" paradigm frequently fails: visual-only metrics are ineffective for plot-driven narrative queries, while indiscriminately adding textual scores introduces severe "modal noise" for purely visual tasks. To break this bottleneck, we propose Q-Gate, a plug-and-play, training-free framework that treats keyframe selection as a dynamic modality routing problem. We decouple retrieval into three lightweight expert streams: Visual Grounding for local details, Global Matching for scene semantics, and Contextual Alignment for subtitle-driven narratives. Crucially, Q-Gate introduces a Query-Modulated Gating Mechanism that uses the in-context reasoning of an LLM to assess query intent and dynamically allocate weights across the experts, activating necessary modalities while "muting" irrelevant ones to maximize the signal-to-noise ratio. Extensive experiments on LongVideoBench and Video-MME across multiple MLLM backbones show that Q-Gate outperforms representative keyframe-selection baselines in most settings, with particularly strong gains on long and medium videos, providing a robust and interpretable solution for scalable video reasoning. Code is available at https://github.com/shaoguangwang/Q_Gate|[2604.17422](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17422)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Directional Confusions Reveal Divergent Inductive Biases Through Rate-Distortion Geometry in Human and Machine Vision**|Leyla Roksan Caglar et.al.|Humans and modern vision models can reach similar classification accuracy while making systematically different kinds of mistakes -- differing not in how often they err, but in who gets mistaken for whom and in which direction. We show that these directional confusions reveal distinct inductive biases that are invisible to accuracy alone. Using matched human and deep vision model responses on a natural-image categorization task under 12 perturbation types, we quantify asymmetry in confusion matrices and link it to the shape of the information-error trade-off. We characterize this trade-off geometry using three signatures extracted from the rate-distortion (RD) frontier: slope beta, curvature kappa and efficiency (AUC). We find that humans exhibit broad but weak asymmetries, whereas deep vision models show sparser, stronger directional collapses. Robustness training reduces global asymmetry but fails to recover the human-like breadth-strength profile of graded similarity. Mechanistic simulations further show that different asymmetry organizations shift the RD frontier in opposite directions, even when matched for performance. Together, these results position directional confusions and RD geometry as compact, interpretable signatures of inductive bias under distribution shift.|[2604.21909](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21909)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Exposing and Mitigating Temporal Attack in Deepfake Video Detection**|Zheyuan Gu et.al.|While spatiotemporal deepfake detectors achieve high AUC, our experiments reveal their susceptibility to evasion attacks. These models tend to overfit on fragile temporal spectrum cues, rather than learning robust semantic causality. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose SpInShield, a temporal spectral-invariant defense framework explicitly designed to decouple semantic motion from manipulatable spectral artifacts. We propose a learnable spectral adversary that dynamically synthesizes severe spectral deformations, simulating extreme attack scenarios. By employing a shortcut suppression optimization strategy, SpInShield compels the encoder to extract reliable forensic cues while purging unstable spectral statistics from the latent space. Experiments show that SpInShield obtains competitive performance on widely used datasets and outperforms the strongest baseline by 21.30 percentage points in AUC under simulated amplitude spectral attacks.|[2605.07398](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07398)|
|**2026-11-16**|**InsHuman: Towards Natural and Identity-Preserving Human Insertion**|Jie Li et.al.|Human insertion aims to naturally place specific individuals into a target background. Although existing image editing models may have such ability, they often produce failure cases, including inappropriate human pose in new background, inconsistent number of people, and modified facial identity. Moreover, publicly available human datasets often lack full-body portraits and realistic physical interaction between humans and their background. To address these challenges, we propose InsHuman for natural and identity-preserving human insertion. Specifically, we propose Human-Background Adaptive Fusion (HBAF), which detects foreground humans to obtain a binary mask and applies region-aware weighting to align the human regions between predicted and ground-truth latents, ensuring the person's pose, count, and overall appearance are coherently adapted to the target background.We further propose Face-to-Face ID-Preserving (FFIP), which detects and matches faces between the generated image and the source image in terms of face recognition features to enforce identity consistency for each face.In addition, we propose Bidirectional Data Pairing (BDP) strategy to construct BDP-InsHuman, a high-quality dataset with realistic human-background interactions. Experiments demonstrate that InsHuman achieves significant improvements in generating plausible images while keeping human identity unchanged.|[2605.07402](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07402)|
|**2026-11-16**|**EdgeZSAD: Value-Range Quantization for Deploying Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection on Edge Neural Processing Units**|Taewan Cho et.al.|Quantizing zero-shot industrial anomaly detectors for edge neural processing units can degrade defect detection and localization. We present EdgeZSAD (Edge Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection), a deployment framework whose methodological contribution is Value-Range Quantization (VRQ). We diagnose how attention Value tensors inherit overly wide ranges from fused query-key-value projections and assign independent ranges calibrated on source data, without retraining or changing quantized weights. The engineering application is native deployment of a foundation-model detector for industrial inspection, evaluated on three public benchmarks and Qualcomm edge devices. Under minimum-maximum and mean-squared-error calibration, VRQ improves image-level discrimination and per-region overlap. In the primary calibration set, it recovers 90-98% of the loss in combined image and pixel discrimination relative to half precision, with a small increase in latency over baseline quantization. Matched tensor overrides reproduce the recovery without adding graph nodes, identifying independent Value-range assignment as the effective intervention in the evaluated runtime family.|[2606.16119](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16119)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When Simpler Is Better: Evaluating Translation Pipelines for Medieval Latin Manuscripts**|Nguyen Kim Hai Bui et.al.|Despite remarkable progress in machine translation, Vision Language Models (VLMs) struggle on historical manuscripts, a domain that stresses core Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities: low-resource transliteration, archaic vocabulary, and noisy input signals. We present a systematic framework for evaluating the full image-to-translation pipeline on medieval Latin manuscripts, a setting in which scribal shorthand, ligatures, and parchment degradation expose failure modes that are invisible in clean-text benchmarks. Benchmarking on the CATMuS Latin dataset reveals a specialization gap: domain-specific Optical Character Recognition (OCR) models reduce character error rate by up to 4.3$\times$ compared to general-purpose VLMs, despite operating at orders of magnitude fewer parameters. We introduce the Interpres-Parallel-Corpus (IPC), a novel dataset comprising 1,383 aligned manuscript image lines, transcriptions, and expert translations, to our knowledge the first of its kind for medieval Latin. Our experiments uncover a complexity paradox: the simplest pipeline, a specialized OCR model feeding directly into a VLM, outperforms all multi-component variants. Adding retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or post-OCR correction introduces prompt saturation and error propagation that degrade aggregate translation quality. These findings offer both a new benchmark and practical guidance for deploying translation systems in low-resource historical settings.|[2607.03836](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03836)|
|**2026-11-16**|**RFMSR: Residual Flow Matching for Image Super-Resolution**|Shuwei Huang et.al.|Generative image super-resolution (ISR) methods typically sample from a standard Gaussian prior and transport the sample to the data distribution, where the low-quality (LQ) input serves only as an external condition. However, the LQ image already carries rich structural information about the target high-quality (HQ) image, which makes a distribution approximating the LQ image a more suitable starting point, an idea that has been validated by a series of diffusion-based restoration methods. In contrast, recent flow-matching-based ISR methods still adopt the standard Gaussian prior and thereby discard such structural priors; moreover, existing single-step acceleration techniques, such as distillation and consistency training, either depend on an additional teacher or sacrifice the model's multi-step inference capability. In this paper, we propose Residual Flow Matching for Image Super-Resolution (RFMSR), which defines the source distribution as a Gaussian centered at the LQ latent, so that the flow starts from the LQ image rather than from pure noise and preserves the structural priors already present in the input. To achieve high-quality single-step generation, we further propose a simple yet effective training strategy that combines the supervision loss on the single-step output with the conditional flow matching (CFM) loss, enabling high-quality restoration with an arbitrary number of steps without resorting to distillation or other acceleration techniques. Extensive experiments show that RFMSR achieves competitive results against state-of-the-art methods. The source code is available at https://github.com/Faze-Hsw/RFMSR.|[2607.12753](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12753)|
|**2026-11-16**|**The Second LoViF 2026 Challenge on Real-World All-in-One Image Restoration: Methods and Results**|Xiang Chen et.al.|This paper presents a review of the second LoViF Challenge on Real-World All-in-One Image Restoration. The challenge aims to advance unified image restoration under diverse real-world degradation conditions, including blur, low-light, haze, rain, and snow. It provides a common benchmark for evaluating the restoration accuracy, robustness, and generalization capability of models across multiple degradation categories within a unified framework. The competition attracted 158 registered participants, and 20 teams were included in the final ranking after their submitted results were successfully reproduced and verified. This report provides a comprehensive analysis of the submitted solutions and corresponding results, highlighting recent advances in real-world all-in-one image restoration. The summarized methods and empirical findings reveal effective design strategies and establish an updated benchmark for future research in real-world low-level vision.|[2607.21118](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21118)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Projection Pursuit CPCANet for Domain Generalization**|Yu-Hsi Chen et.al.|Domain Generalization (DG) aims to learn representations robust to distribution shifts. Recent geometric alignment methods, such as CPCANet, extract domain-invariant structures through batch-wise Common Principal Component Analysis (CPCA). However, CPCANet suffers from rank-deficient covariance estimation due to the small-sample-size issue in mini-batch training. To address this limitation, we propose Projection Pursuit CPCANet (PP-CPCANet), a covariance-free framework that learns a global orthogonal basis on the Stiefel manifold and jointly optimizes it with network parameters via the Cayley transform. We further introduce a symmetry-breaking detached-median PP dispersion objective to extract common principal components (CPCs) with dense and robust optimization signals. Experiments on four DG benchmarks show that PP-CPCANet achieves SOTA performance while maintaining stable training.|[2607.22117](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22117)|
|**2026-11-16**|**AnaDiffusion: Anatomically Compositional Latent Diffusion for Controllable 3D Brain MRI Generation**|Huiwen Han et.al.|3D brain MRI generation has made significant advances in medical imaging, simulation, and controllable anatomical analysis. However, existing generative models typically synthesize 3D volumes monolithically, often overlooking regional anatomical structures and limiting local controllability. To address these limitations, we introduce AnaDiffusion, an anatomically compositional latent diffusion framework that factorizes the generation process into distinct, anatomically meaningful regions, followed by part-to-whole assembly and global refinement. Our approach first trains part diffusion models to capture local structural priors. We then inject an assembled anatomical composite of the parts into the whole-brain latent representation and continue denoising. This mechanism enables the model to resolve global context while preserving the injected anatomy. AnaDiffusion thus produces explicit part assets and a globally coherent volume, enabling controllable part editing without subject-specific dense segmentation maps at inference. On a subject-disjoint ADNI test split, AnaDiffusion attains the lowest mean Fr\'echet distance (FID) in every evaluated region. In localized editing, it achieves substantially higher target transfer (paired MS-SSIM) than a segmentation-conditioned baseline, with off-target preservation close to the baseline's.|[2608.23014](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23014)|
|**2026-11-16**|**UltraBench 2: Towards Robust Evaluation of Vision Foundation Models on Ultrasound**|Ashwath Radhachandran et.al.|Benchmarking is an increasingly critical part of research in machine learning and the domains where it is applied, including healthcare. Yet, despite the steady development of new ultrasound foundation models in recent years, the development of well-designed benchmarks to evaluate them has lagged behind. This deficiency has led to fragmented and inconsistent evaluations of competing models, making it difficult to measure progress. To address this issue, we introduce UltraBench 2, a comprehensive benchmark with wide anatomical and task coverage, and a focus on standardization, reproducibility, and ease-of-use. Using this benchmark, we compare existing vision foundation models for ultrasound image analysis. Our analyses demonstrate that ultrasound-specific pretraining still leads on classification, but that state-of-the-art general-purpose models have drawn level on segmentation.|[2609.28610](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28610)|
|**2026-11-16**|**The Alignment Illusion in Multimodal Large Language Models**|Hong-Han Wang et.al.|Layer-wise visual-text similarity in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is widely interpreted as evidence that the language model progressively integrates visual content into a shared representation space. This reading rests on the assumption that scalar alignment scores reflect content-level cross-modal interaction. To test this assumption, we apply controlled interventions to the visual stream. Across 13 MLLMs from five families spanning 0.5B to 72B parameters, replacing projector-output visual tokens with Gaussian noise sharply reduces task accuracy, yet four standard scalar measures (CKA, SVCCA, MIR, and the leading principal-angle cosine) fail to consistently separate the corrupted stream from the original. We call this failure the alignment illusion and trace it to the shared language-model pathway: anisotropic MLP down-projections pull visual and text tokens toward common output directions, producing weight-induced alignment. We introduce the principal-angle gap (PA gap), defined as the difference between the top two principal-angle cosines, to distinguish leading-direction dominance from the broader overlap observed with structured visual content. Under graded visual corruption, the PA gap tracks task accuracy more consistently than the scalar scores we consider; under a structured but irrelevant image, it further exposes regimes in which internal geometry and task accuracy come apart. Internal visual-text alignment in MLLMs is therefore best read as a geometric diagnostic of the visual stream inside the language model rather than a direct proxy for content-level cross-modal interaction, and is most informative when calibrated by controlled task evidence.|[2609.30210](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30210)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Mind the RefGAP: Correcting Reference Attention in Diffusion-Based Visual Editing**|Yanan Wang et.al.|Reference-guided diffusion editors struggle to faithfully reproduce user-provided references. We observe that many diffusion editors allocate limited attention to the reference. For example, in LoomVideo, edit-region queries assign less than 1% of their attention mass to the reference. We introduce RefGAP, a training-free correction that determines logit-offset magnitudes online at each layer from the reference-attention mass measured during the forward pass. Positive offsets to reference logits strengthen reference usage by edit-region queries, while negative offsets for keep-region queries limit reference-induced changes outside the edit. Two global coefficients control the correction; they are selected once on validation data from four development diffusion editors and held fixed. Across seven diffusion-based image/video editors, RefGAP improves identity fidelity in head swapping and face swapping, where identity takes priority; on some editors this raises pose error, which delaying the correction reduces. RefGAP achieves a fidelity-preservation trade-off comparable to separately tuned constant edit-side biases, without per-approach strength sweeps. Additional experiments on virtual try-on and background replacement evaluate transfer beyond identity editing.|[2609.35708](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35708)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Synthetic Image Oversampling for Rare-Class Representation Learning in Deep Facial Phenotyping**|Miles Liao et.al.|Recent deep facial phenotyping methods can support the recognition of genetic syndromes, but patient images are often scarce and unevenly distributed across disorders. Synthetic facial images offer a way to supplement the training data without collecting additional patient photographs. Their usefulness, however, depends on whether they improve recognition of real patients: generated images may introduce artifacts or repeat features already represented in the training set. It is therefore necessary to examine both how much synthetic augmentation to use and which disorders benefit. We investigate disease-specific synthetic oversampling using images from the Phenotypic Disease Image Database to augment a subset of the GestaltMatcher Database. We compare increasing augmentation levels in an image-based model, GestaltMatcher-Arc, and a multimodal model, GestaltMML, while keeping the real training and patient-disjoint evaluation partitions fixed. Repeated training runs assess overall and disease-specific accuracy, and an exploratory embedding analysis examines changes in class compactness and separation. Augmentation improves mean recognition accuracy in both training setups, although gains remain modest in GestaltMatcher-Arc and continue across the tested levels in GestaltMML. These aggregate improvements are accompanied by uneven disease-level responses, including reduced accuracy for some disorders. Together, the findings support synthetic oversampling for limited-data facial phenotyping and emphasize the need to assess its amount and benefit for individual disorders within each training setup.|[2610.23730](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23730)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI**|Spiros Maggioros et.al.|Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. To address this, we introduce Brain2Vec, a foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans pooled from multiple studies. Brain2Vec can serve both as a weight initialization backbone for fine-tuning downstream tasks and as a standalone feature extractor. We evaluated Brain2Vec in three tasks: downstream predictive performance, convergence speed, and embedding quality. Using Brain2Vec's pretrained weights as initialization and fine-tuning on labeled data for a brain age prediction task, results demonstrate improved final accuracy and faster convergence compared to random initialization. Additionally, its frozen embeddings outperform anatomically defined volumetric features on four of five downstream classification tasks and perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data. On the independent BrainFMBench benchmark, Brain2Vec ranked first among six pretrained brain MRI foundation models on 9 of 15 sex, age, and BMI prediction tasks across five cohorts. Lastly, we show that Brain2Vec encodes information primarily from a spatially localized and neuroanatomically meaningful set of brain regions, and that manipulating a subset of latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on the reconstructed anatomy. Brain2Vec code and trained models are available as an open-source package on https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec. Users can apply Brain2Vec locally by installing the package, or directly through the NiChart cloud platform https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com, where Brain2Vec features can be derived without requiring local installation or specialized infrastructure.|[2610.28411](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28411)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Zoom Where the Answer Looks: Cross-Resolution Self-Distillation for MLLMs under Visual-Token Budgets**|Zhensheng Hu et.al.|Under the small per-view visual-token budgets of efficient deployment, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible to multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Zooming in recovers it, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. Existing methods decode an answer or a box first, at the cost of generation, or read the attention available before answering, which localizes the evidence only in top layers whose depth varies with model size. Learned region proposers instead distill the attention of the model's answers, but on the same budgeted view. Yet this attention is precise only on a view the model can read: on Qwen3-VL-4B, a window around its peak covers 83% of the evidence on a high-resolution view but 54% on the budgeted view. Since answers and high-resolution views are available during training, we introduce LookZoom, a zooming method that trains a dedicated look token by cross-resolution self-distillation of the answer's attention: with the MLLM frozen, the token learns to attend on the budgeted view, before any answer, where the model's own correct answers attend on a high-resolution view. The region around its attention peak is the window to zoom into, and reinforcement learning then trains the MLLM to answer from both views. With 98 visual tokens per view on five text-rich benchmarks, LookZoom surpasses attention-guided zooming by 20-24 points and grounding-based zooming and learned crop routing by 12-15 points on Qwen3-VL at three scales, and it outperforms learned region proposers at every budget from 98 to 392 tokens. On Qwen3-VL, the look token alone, with the MLLM frozen, already surpasses every baseline that leaves the language model unchanged, and the recipe carries over to InternVL3.5 and Qwen2.5-VL.|[2610.32664](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664)|
|**2026-11-16**|**TAP-Out: Tracking Any Point in 360{\deg} via Outpainting**|Finlay G. C. Hudson et.al.|Tracking points that leave the camera's field of view (FoV) requires reasoning about unobserved scene dynamics, a fundamentally different problem from tracking visible correspondences. We present TAP-Out, an offline method that makes this out-of-view (OOV) reasoning explicit by leveraging generative video outpainting as a world-model prior. Given a narrow-FoV input video, TAP-Out outpaints a complete 360{\deg} equirectangular video, locates target objects within the panoramic scene via video object segmentation, and synthesises object-centred perspective crops on which standard in-frame trackers operate. This decoupled design composes off-the-shelf foundation models without requiring task-specific architectural modifications. On the TAPVid360 benchmark, TAP-Out achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving OOV tracking accuracy by approximately 3x over the best prior method. We demonstrate its flexibility on out-of-frame object segmentation and detection and we provide detailed diagnostic analyses across motion regimes, static versus dynamic content, and hallucination quality, characterising when and why the approach succeeds or fails. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/finlay-hudson/TAP-Out|[2611.00406](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00406)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Real-VAS: a Real-World Video Amodal Segmentation Dataset**|Finlay G. C. Hudson et.al.|Amodal video object segmentation is fundamentally limited by the absence of datasets that combine real-world complexity with precise ground-truth annotations. To address this, we present Real Video Amodal Segmentation (Real-VAS), a new large-scale, zero-shot evaluation dataset built from composited real-image video. We introduce a novel data generation pipeline that enables the creation of programmatically consistent amodal ground truth for real-world footage, in which the object mask, occlusion boundary, and composited image are all derived from a single unoccluded source, without relying on human estimation or expensive 3D reconstruction. Our dataset is structured into two challenging scenarios: dynamic Occlusion, created by compositing two clips of moving objects, and a unique Container category featuring complex, physically constrained interactions. These container scenarios--On Surface Containment, Articulated Containment, and Mobile Containment--allow us to generate precise ground truth by simulating an object's motion based on its container's tracked transformation. As a result, Real-VAS provides a diverse and challenging benchmark for evaluating amodal segmentation models on realistic video with the precision of synthetic data. The dataset and generation code are publicly available at https://finlay-hudson.github.io/real_vas|[2611.00430](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00430)|
|**2026-11-16**|**A Straight Line Is Not a Camera Pose: Auditing Short-Clip Pose Benchmarks**|Xinyi Shi et.al.|Many vision benchmarks align a prediction to the ground truth before scoring it. If the alignment minimises the error, an input-free template scores the truth's distance from everything the alignment can reach, so the ground truth alone fixes how well a template can score, before any model runs. Camera pose is our central case. The absolute trajectory error (ATE), the standard camera-motion measure, aligns camera positions by a similarity transform. On the common Sintel protocol, an image-free constant-velocity line has a lower mean ATE than 10 of 11 recent models and 85 of 110 published methods, and on Lightspeed than 5 of 9 models and 10 of 11 published methods. The straightness of the true paths separates these two from TUM, ScanNet, DyCheck and ADT, where every model beats the line. A residual signal-to-noise criterion with the alignment's first-order refit predicts when straightening a prediction lowers ATE in all but one of 4010 model-clip evaluations, including all 504 on three benchmarks registered in advance. The alignment also ignores where cameras look: rotating a trajectory by 30 degrees against its orientations leaves ATE unchanged yet worsens the static-point reprojection of every model on Sintel, TUM and ScanNet. Fitting the alignment's rotation to the predicted orientations gives pose-anchored ATE (PA-ATE), a one-function change at about ATE's cost (37 vs. 34 microseconds per clip). It is never below ATE, ranks every image-free reference below every model on Sintel, tracks the reprojection error under controlled rotation and straightening, largely preserves the models' order, and exposes Lightspeed ground-truth orientations that the images contradict. Beyond poses, templates beat some published methods on KITTI video depth, SSP-3D body shape and NoW faces, and in video depth this distance sets KITTI apart from Sintel and Bonn.|[2611.00482](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00482)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Geometric Refinement and Distributional Alignment for Sim2Real Roadside LiDAR Detection**|Aman Upganlawar et.al.|We describe our approach to the LUMPI track of the UrbanTwin Sim2Real LiDAR Challenge, in which a 3D object detector must be trained exclusively on synthetic data and evaluated on unseen real roadside LiDAR, while the synthetic point clouds are simultaneously scored for distributional realism. Our final entry scores 0.4793 combined, from a detection mAP of 0.1333 and a realism score of 0.9126. Our contribution is not a new architecture. Working from a PointPillars baseline, we obtained most of our gains from three sources. First, a diagnosis that small object failure in roadside LiDAR is a localisation problem rather than a detection problem, which led to a parameter free geometric refinement worth 86 percent relative on pedestrian AP. Second, an audit of our own post processing which revealed that a shipped rule was destroying valid predictions, the repair of which produced our single largest detection gain at 335 percent relative on the Van class. Third, treating the realism metrics analytically rather than as a black box, which showed that one of the four components is a closed form function of nine summary statistics. We also report an experimental methodology that we believe is the more transferable result. Because mean AP decomposes into independent per class terms, a single submission can carry several mutually controlled experiments, and a submission assembled entirely from previously measured components has a score that is known before it is sent. We predicted our final score to four decimal places prior to submission. We report negative results at equal length, and give a full account of data provenance and compliance in Section 8, including a full account of every mechanism contributing to the submitted score.|[2611.03936](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03936)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Coordinate Frame Recovery, Per Class NMS and Null Return Modelling for Sim2Real V2X LiDAR Detection**|Aman Upganlawar et.al.|We describe our approach to the V2X-Real track of the UrbanTwin Sim2Real LiDAR Challenge, in which a 3D object detector must be trained exclusively on synthetic data and evaluated on unseen real roadside LiDAR from an Ouster OS1-64 unit, while the synthetic point clouds are simultaneously scored for distributional realism. Our final entry scores 0.7457 combined, from a detection mAP of 0.4382 and a realism score of 0.9251. Three findings account for most of our result, and all three were diagnostic rather than architectural. First, a coordinate frame mismatch between our predictions and the evaluation ground truth was suppressing detection AP entirely; recovering it took our score from 0.3520 to 0.5147 in a single submission. Second, a single global non maximum suppression threshold was destroying genuine pedestrian detections, because pedestrians occur in groups and a 0.3 IoU threshold applied to a 0.7 m box suppresses distinct neighbouring people; correcting this per class raised overall mAP from 0.0751 to 0.4295. Third, approximately 31 percent of the real reference point cloud consists of null returns encoded as the literal point (0, 0, 0), a format feature entirely absent from our synthetic clouds, which pinned Earth Mover's Distance near its floor until we modelled it. Negative results are reported at equal length, and Section 9 gives a full account of data provenance and compliance. That section also accounts for every mechanism contributing to the submitted score.|[2611.03940](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03940)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Mamba3R: Fixed-Capacity Selective State Memory for Long-Sequence 3D Reconstruction**|Zhenxiang Xiong et.al.|Streaming 3D reconstruction must maintain geometric quality over long inputs within a fixed GPU budget. A pretrained recurrent model provides bounded state, but freezing its backbone also fixes its learned state-observation interactions. We introduce Mamba3R, a jointly designed state-memory adapter that provides a trainable adaptation path without updating the backbone or task heads. A selective state-space mixer jointly transforms native state features and a fixed-capacity auxiliary memory, explicit read and write paths carry information across frames, and a decomposed feedback path regulates what the added memory feeds back. Only 1.28 million adapter parameters are trained. Under a shared periodic-reset protocol, it lowers trajectory error and improves accumulated surface and depth accuracy over recurrent baselines on public indoor and outdoor datasets and robot-recorded traversals, gaining up to 40 percent on one metric. Matched comparisons support both the choice of the mixing operator and the utility of carrying the auxiliary memory across frames. Allocated memory remains unchanged and throughput moves by at most 1.4 percent from one to two thousand frames. Replaying its recorded traversals offline, the platform's RTX 4060 averages 11.7 frames per second within 5.1 GiB with the same checkpoint, a mean throughput covering the 10 Hz sensor rate.|[2611.04221](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04221)|
|**2026-11-16**|**TAQ-MOTR: Tracking-Aware Detection Query Formation for End-to-End Multiple Object Tracking**|Hanyang Liu et.al.|Tracking-by-propagation (TBP) methods perform end-to-end multi-object tracking by propagating track queries across frames, jointly handling detection and identity association without heuristic post-processing while naturally modeling temporal information. However, as detection and association are tightly coupled, detection queries in TBP are expected to discover currently untracked objects and provide complementary current-frame evidence for challenging tracks whose queries and identities are retained. Existing methods largely inherit detection-query formation from single-frame detection and construct such queries before the decoder with little awareness of trajectory states. Consequently, currently untracked objects may be missed, while challenging tracked objects with reduced visibility under occlusion may lack complementary evidence, causing association failures or duplicate responses. To address these issues, we propose TAQ-MOTR, a tracking-aware detection query formation framework that employs a pre-decoder Hybrid Query Generator (HQG) to fuse current-frame image features, track memory, and learnable memory. By conditioning detection queries on trajectory states, TAQ-MOTR adapts them to \rev{the discovery of currently untracked objects and tracked-object complementation We further introduce a training-only auxiliary detection branch whose one-to-one label assignment is decoupled from that of the main tracking branch, providing additional detection supervision to strengthen the shared HQG and decoder for the discovery of currently untracked objects. TAQ-MOTR improves performance on DanceTrack, SportsMOT, and BFT, achieving 72.0 HOTA, 63.1 AssA, and 76.6 IDF1 on the DanceTrack test set. Code is available at https://github.com/Magicmiya/TAQ-MOTR|[2611.05205](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05205)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Evaluating Anterior Cruciate Ligament Risk of Injury Using a Graph Convolutional Autoencoder**|Athanasios Babouras et.al.|Current solutions for assessing the risk of anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) injury typically rely on expensive motion-capture labs that require substantial resource investment or subjective evaluations of specific athletic stress tests by experts. In this paper, we develop an unsupervised learning model that runs entirely on a smartphone for two tasks, based on an autoencoder architecture with multiple layers of graph convolutional networks, which utilizes pose estimation data to determine a person's injury risk status. Our model accurately predicts athletes at high risk of injury for both athletic stress tests, with average sensitivities and specificities of 78% and 78% for the DVJ, and 75% and 74% for the SLH. Our findings support the development of a widely accessible smartphone solution for real-time screening of ACL injury risk.|[2611.09724](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09724)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Decoupling Direction and Magnitude: Language-Steered Flow Matching for Super-Resolution in the Dark**|Jiaxin Gao et.al.|Image super-resolution in the dark is fundamentally challenged by extreme spatial heterogeneity: severely underexposed and noisy regions demand aggressive generative enhancement to reconstruct missing details, while relatively well-exposed areas require conservative updates to prevent hallucinated textures. Standard diffusion and flow-matching models, however, rely on globally uniform integration trajectories, making them sub-optimal for such spatially varying degradations. In this paper, we propose DeLang-SR (Degradation-Language steered Super-Resolution), a one-step flow-matching framework that explicitly decouples the restoration direction and magnitude. Rather than treating low-light corruption as arbitrary latent features, DeLang-SR translates measurable physical statistics into structured degradation language, harnessing the semantic prior of pretrained text-to-image models. The degradation-language intent, complemented by image-specific visual tokens, steers the velocity field (direction) toward restoration-relevant manifolds. Simultaneously, a continuous spatial update-strength field acts as a locally varying Euler step-size field (magnitude), applying stronger integration steps in heavily degraded shadows while enforcing conservative updates in reliable regions. Experiments on paired and real dark images, together with ablations and diagnostic analyses, show that DeLang-SR provides an effective and interpretable one-step route to super-resolution in the dark. Code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/moriyaya/DeLang-SR.|[2611.10485](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10485)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Before Words, Beyond Speech: Evaluating Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Early Childhood**|Marie Amale Huynh et.al.|Children's earliest years are a period of intense social learning, during which meaning is built through gaze, gesture, and physical contact. Yet, this critical aspect of social interaction remains under-evaluated in multimodal video understanding, as existing benchmarks prioritize the conversation-driven interactions of adults. We address this gap with \bench ($\textbf{N}$aturalistic $\textbf{E}$arly-childhood $\textbf{S}$ocial in$\textbf{T}$eractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning. \bench comprises \totalvids manually annotated clips ($\sim$12s) curated for diversity across naturalistic settings, cultural contexts, and developmental stages. Beyond a lack of data, targeted model improvement in this domain is hindered by coarse evaluation schemas that conflate basic perception with higher-order reasoning. To resolve this, \bench introduces a compositional schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive levels of social reasoning: contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended descriptions. Extensive analysis of state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reveals a stark performance collapse: despite strong contextual recognition, models exhibit substantial gaps in decoding precise behavioral and interpersonal cues. We trace these failures to specific biases, including poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. Finally, we ground \bench in real-world applications by evaluating VLMs using domain adaptation strategies. Ultimately, \bench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing socially grounded artificial intelligence in early childhood. Project page: https://laubravo.github.io/nest_website/.|[2611.13213](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13213)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CARE-Drive: Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories**|Xiao Ma et.al.|Vision-language-action (VLA) models bring multimodal understanding and world knowledge to end-to-end autonomous driving. To further improve the quality of predicted trajectories, existing studies introduce candidate refinement, residual denoising, and token correction. However, these methods typically focus on unified refinement of the entire trajectory, paying limited attention to the distinct physical quantities and their interactions. We further investigate physical specialization and coordination in trajectory refinement, centering on position and heading for simpler and more intuitive correction modeling. To this end, we propose CARE-Drive (Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories), which decomposes trajectory refinement into waypoint residual correction and position-conditioned heading generation. Through imitation learning and a future-token auxiliary task, the backbone provides predictive representations and a base trajectory. The position expert predicts bounded metric waypoint residuals, while the heading expert reconstructs vehicle orientation from adjacent angular increments conditioned on the corrected path. With the backbone frozen, the two experts are optimized alternately through supervised initialization and reinforcement fine-tuning. This design simplifies refinement through clear physical specialization while preserving conditional coupling during prediction and optimization. |**2026-11-16**|**Gravity and Flight: Learning to Navigate Fine-Grained Remote Sensing Scenes**|Yuheng Zong et.al.|Vision-language models are increasingly capable, yet their reasoning in complex remote sensing scenes can remain coarse-grained and insufficiently grounded in task-relevant visual evidence. It combines Evidence-of-Thought supervised fine-tuning (EoT-SFT) and Relational Transfer Policy Optimization (RTPO). EoT-SFT teaches fine-grained observation and faithful reasoning through description, analysis, and supported conclusions. RTPO independently samples responses to scene-matched image pairs with fine-grained visual differences under a shared task. Its rubric-based verification combines Gravity, which checks visual fidelity and description coverage, and Flight, which takes descriptions as premises to assess evidence use and relations between paired judgments. Their scores and task correctness yield a shared pair-level advantage that couples policy updates from both responses. Using 1,008 demonstrations and 500 training pairs for harbor specialization, G&F achieves a mean score of 69.12 across six evaluation sets, exceeding the backbone and supervised initialization by 9.76 and 4.56 points. Matched controls show gains over single-image and joint-input RL, and removing either verification view reduces performance. Project details and updates are available at https://github.com/Z0ngL1ng/Gravity-and-Flight.|[2611.16408](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16408)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Graph Your Own Prompt**|Xi Ding et.al.|We propose Graph Consistency Regularization (GCR), a novel framework that injects relational graph structures, derived from model predictions, into the learning process to promote class-aware, semantically meaningful feature representations. Functioning as a form of self-prompting, GCR enables the model to refine its internal structure using its own outputs. While deep networks learn rich representations, these often capture noisy inter-class similarities that contradict the model's predicted semantics. GCR addresses this issue by introducing parameter-free Graph Consistency Layers (GCLs) at arbitrary depths. Each GCL builds a batch-level feature similarity graph and aligns it with a global, class-aware masked prediction graph, derived by modulating softmax prediction similarities with intra-class indicators. This alignment enforces that feature-level relationships reflect class-consistent prediction behavior, acting as a semantic regularizer throughout the network. Unlike prior work, GCR introduces a multi-layer, cross-space graph alignment mechanism with adaptive weighting, where layer importance is learned from graph discrepancy magnitudes. This allows the model to prioritize semantically reliable layers and suppress noisy ones, enhancing feature quality without modifying the architecture or training procedure. GCR is model-agnostic, lightweight, and improves semantic structure across various networks and datasets. Experiments show that GCR promotes cleaner feature structure, stronger intra-class cohesion, and improved generalization, offering a new perspective on learning from prediction structure. [Project website](https://darcyddx.github.io/gcr/) [Code](https://github.com/Darcyddx/graph-prompt)|[2509.23373](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23373)|
|**2026-11-16**|**E0: Expressive Fine-Grained Discrete Action Prediction for Vision-Language-Action Models via Tweedie Discrete Diffusion**|Zhihao Zhan et.al.|Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a unified framework for robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and control generation. However, existing VLA systems still struggle to generalize across diverse tasks, scenes, and camera viewpoints, and often produce coarse or unstable actions. We argue that these limitations are closely tied to the structural properties of actions in VLA settings, including the inherent multi-peaked nature of action distributions, the token-based symbolic reasoning of pretrained VLM/VLA backbones, and the effective finite resolution imposed by real-world robotic control. Motivated by these properties, we introduce E0, a tweedie discrete diffusion framework that formulates action generation as iterative denoising over quantized action tokens. By operating in a discrete action space with a principled diffusion process, E0 naturally aligns with token-based reasoning, supports fine-grained yet executable action control, and avoids the distributional mismatch of masking-based discrete diffusion. Experiments on LIBERO, VLABench, ManiSkill, and a real-world Franka arm demonstrate that E0 achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse environments, outperforming strong baselines by 8.1% on average.|[2511.21542](http://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21542)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Uncertainty Quantification for Multimodal Large Language Models with Incoherence-adjusted Semantic Volume**|Gregory Kang Ruey Lau et.al.|Despite their capabilities, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) may produce plausible but erroneous outputs, hindering reliable deployment. However, existing uncertainty metrics have practical constraints, such as being designed only for specific modalities, reliant on external tools, or computationally expensive. We introduce UMPIRE, a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for MLLMs that works efficiently across various input and output modalities without external tools, relying only on the models' own internal modality features. Inspired by Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), UMPIRE computes a novel incoherence-adjusted semantic volume of sampled MLLM responses for a given task instance, which couples semantic embedding dispersion with the quadratic entropy of the responses. We provide theoretical analysis motivating UMPIRE's design, and extensive experiments show that UMPIRE consistently outperforms baseline metrics in error detection and uncertainty calibration across image, audio, and video-text benchmarks, even with small sample budgets. Our code is available at https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.|[2602.24195](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.24195)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Fusion-Free Multimodal Classification with Heterogeneous Deep Ensembles**|Ilya Burenko et.al.|In multimodal classification, late-fusion approaches classify concatenated modality-specific features extracted by unimodal neural networks. When modality imbalance is pronounced, various regularization techniques have been proposed to balance the learning process and overcome the inferior performance of late-fusion networks. This advantage also holds over intermediate-fusion techniques we evaluated and over hybrid methods that combine unimodal and multimodal classifiers. We propose and empirically validate a method for selecting the number of models per modality in an ensemble, avoiding computationally expensive exhaustive search. Under extreme modality imbalance and small ensemble sizes, the heuristic indicates that ensembles of unimodal models trained solely on the stronger modality are preferable. As the ensemble scales up, incorporating models from the weaker modality becomes beneficial. Both predictions align with our empirical findings. However, the heuristic becomes less reliable when the diversity of ensemble members is low. To systematically explore the challenges of optimizing multimodal models, we propose a synthetic multimodal framework that allows control over both the number of modalities and their predictive strength. Our findings are consistent across synthetic and real-world datasets. |**2026-11-16**|**MeanFlowAdvantage: Stable Reward Fine-Tuning for Few-Step Average-Velocity Generators**|Haocheng Tang et.al.|MeanFlow enables efficient few-step generation by predicting interval-average velocities, but this representation creates a mismatch for reward fine-tuning: existing advantage-based objectives are typically defined on instantaneous velocities or equivalent $x_0$-space predictions, whereas inference directly uses the learned average-velocity map. The same objective also transfers to DNA promoter design, where it supports both teacher-free on-policy RL for a generator defined on a manifold and teacher-guided reward-graded distillation, with the latter yielding the lowest one-step Sei profile MSE among the compared configurations.|[2609.37670](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37670)|
|**2026-11-16**|**What Should World Models Forget? Stratified Retention for Continual Adaptation**|Nishit Anand et.al.|Continual learning treats any drop in performance on previously seen data as forgetting, a convention inherited from settings where the prediction target is stationary. World models violate this assumption: their target is the environment, which changes, so knowledge that was accurate when learned can later become false, and revising it is correct behavior rather than a defect. We argue that continual world models need retention stratified by invariance timescale, the time over which knowledge stays true: invariants should be refined but never degraded, while instance-level facts should be revised once the environment changes. We show that standard forgetting metrics, used alone, count correct revision as forgetting and so rank a frozen model highest. We propose differential retention, which reports two quantities separately: invariant retention, checked after every adaptation round, and revision latency, the number of observations a model needs to pick up a change.|[2610.03713](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03713)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ECGGuard: Deep Watermarking of Electrocardiogram Signals With Error-Correcting**|Kuan-Yu Chen et.al.|Wearable electrocardiogram (ECG) recordings pass through multiple networks and systems before automated interpretation, leaving patient metadata vulnerable to loss. Digital watermarking can embed this metadata in the signal, but conventional methods fail under time shifts, and high waveform similarity does not guarantee the same downstream diagnosis. We present \rev{ECGGuard}, a deep watermarking framework for single-lead ECG. A fully convolutional generator embeds the payload without signal quantization, a detector decodes the message frame by frame and checks for watermark presence, and during training a frozen arrhythmia classifier penalizes changes that would alter its predictions. Because high bit accuracy does not ensure whole-message recovery, the payload is protected by extended Hamming codes, \rev{a validation-selected channel ordering}, and soft-decision decoding. On the MIT-BIH Arrhythmia Database, \rev{averaged over three training runs, ECGGuard} keeps bit accuracy above \rev{98\%} under time shifts of 10 to 50 samples, where transform-domain baselines drop to chance. The coded 16-bit payload is recovered exactly in \rev{99.95\%} of clean segments\rev{, in over 96\%} under time shifts, 10\% sample deletion, or 10\% packet loss\rev{, and in 99.1\% for unseen subjects}. \rev{For the guiding classifier, the diagnostic loss limits the drop in ventricular-beat sensitivity to 1.6 percentage points on the test records (35.0 without it) and to 12.2 points for unseen subjects (67.0 without it); the drop grows to 10.3 points for independently trained classifiers, and the protection does not transfer to an external database.} \rev{ECGGuard} has 0.56 million parameters and needs about \rev{19}~ms per segment on a desktop CPU.|[2610.13357](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13357)|
|**2026-11-16**|**EmbodiExp: Compositional Experience Transfer for Embodied Agents**|Zhifeng Gu et.al.|Experience from past tasks can help embodied agents perform new tasks, but it may not remain applicable or effective when conditions change. Tasks with different overall goals often share subgoals, providing a basis for cross-task experience transfer. We present EmbodiExp, a framework for constructing outcome-grounded subgoal experiences and composing them in new tasks. Each experience describes how to pursue a subgoal, when the guidance applies, and what outcome must be verified for completion. Verified outcomes from applying one experience can satisfy the conditions for applying another, allowing experiences from different past tasks to be composed online. By examining the actions and observed outcomes at the subgoal level, EmbodiExp retains experience from successful subgoals and uses explicit action failures to identify actions to avoid under specific conditions. Experiments on EmbodiedBench and VIGIL assess cross-task transfer with experiences kept fixed throughout held-out evaluation. |**2026-11-16**|**TransGHOST: Real-Time, Synthesis-Free Reconstruction of Instrument-Occluded Regions in Ophthalmic Microsurgery**|Niran Nataraj et.al.|Intraoperative scene perception in ophthalmic microsurgery is limited by instrument occlusion: when a tool enters the high-magnification field, it conceals tissue from both the surgeon and the perception components of robot-assisted systems that process the video. Many video inpainters also depend on future frames and cannot operate online. We present TransGHOST, a causal reference-guided reconstruction pipeline whose non-generative safe mode operates in real time. Every restored value is obtained by resampling a recorded frame using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. The system provides per-pixel confidence and provenance and marks unsupported pixels as abstentions. We evaluate TransGHOST on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery with pixel-level instrument and anatomy labels. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. Safe mode reaches 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median and 85.5 ms 95th-percentile latency. On a pre-declared held-out cohort using instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, while matched-mask evaluation on validation shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa. Reference-age analysis further defines the operating interval for the brief, repeated occlusions targeted by TransGHOST.|[2611.13971](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13971)|

|**2026-11-16**|**When Should LLM Agents Forget? Memory Filtering Under Query-Critical Resolver Errors**|Samarth Vinayaka et.al.|LLM memory systems often delete facts classified as stale. We show that this hard-filtering step can reduce downstream accuracy when resolver errors delete query-critical facts. Across three models, a controlled sensitivity intervention moves hard-drop from gains of +0.186 to +0.230 at perfect resolution to losses of -0.176 to -0.181 at the lowest tested reliability. Paired targeted interventions show that false-drop causes significantly greater degradation than false-keep, while a non-deleting baseline can still fail when incorrect labels steer generation. On a frozen 102/102 calibration/test split, a simple deployment-matched gate reaches 0.655 accuracy versus 0.641 for always hard-drop and 0.662 for test-informed oracle selection. Global-accuracy crossovers require gold labels and are model- and task-specific diagnostics, not deployment thresholds. Deletion should instead be treated as cost-sensitive: validate query-critical false-drop risk on matched data, or default to label-free retention when such validation is unavailable.|[2611.17338](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17338)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Does the Ranking Hold? A Practitioner-Facing Audit of Hallucination Detectors on Small, Quantized, On-Device LLMs**|Shubhra Mittal et.al.|Hallucination detection for large language models is dominated by methods evaluated on 7B-70B research models with abundant sampling budgets and exact-match or ROUGE correctness labels. The small models practitioners actually ship on-device, Phi-3-mini, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, and Gemma-2-2B-Instruct, typically at 4-bit precision, sit outside this evidence base. We present a reproducible one-GPU audit of four detectors spanning three paradigms: length-normalized token-margin calibration, a lexical n-gram baseline, and two sampling-based consistency methods (semantic entropy and SelfCheckGPT-NLI) on two small open instruction-tuned models (Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct) loaded at 4-bit NF4 quantization on a single Colab T4 GPU, evaluated on closed-book TriviaQA. Cross-model ranking transfer is poor: Spearman rho is -0.80 and Kendall tau-b is -0.667, meaning the detector ranking actively inverts between the two models, and the cheap lexical baseline is statistically indistinguishable from the sampling-based consistency detectors on both models under bootstrap 95 percent confidence intervals. As a second, methodological contribution, we show that the standard token-overlap-to-multiple-choice correctness labeler for TruthfulQA-Generation produces near-chance AUROC (0.46-0.59) for all four detectors on both models, a labeler failure, not a detector failure, since the same detectors reach 0.69-0.89 AUROC on TriviaQA under the identical pipeline. We release the harness, prompts, and per-item scores and frame the detector choice explicitly as a fairness-of-error-rates allocation problem for practitioners deploying on resource-constrained, on-device hardware.|[2611.17339](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17339)|
|**2026-11-16**|**LoRAWeaver: Directional Adapter Routing in LoRA Representation Space**|Inish Melroy D Souza et.al.|Large language models are expensive to update through full retraining. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning reduces the cost of adaptation, but repeated adaptation creates a growing pool of task-specific LoRA adapters. This pool complicates adapter selection during inference. Existing routing and encoder-based methods often require retraining when new adapters are added, which limits their use in dynamic settings. The proposed training-free method selects adapters by matching adapter-conditioned query representations against task prototypes using cosine similarity. Experiments with LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-2-13B on a 48-task FLAN-v2 benchmark and on out-of-distribution tasks show that the method outperforms training-free baselines in the cross-task setting and approaches a trained retrieval encoder without learning a router. An ablation confirms the benefit of directional alignment in the adapter-output space.|[2611.17340](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17340)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Tabular Population Priors as Structured Retrieval for EHR Medication Set Prediction**|Animesh Agarwal et.al.|Medication prediction from electronic health records is a fixed vocabulary structured prediction problem, yet recent clinical RAG systems often treat it like open ended case based reasoning: retrieve similar patient admissions and ask an LLM to infer a medication set. This retrieval object is indirect the model receives retrieved patient records while the target is a discrete set of medication class labels. We propose a more task aligned retrieval object: a tabular population prior ranked medication class candidates from a sparse condition medication co occurrence table estimated from training admissions. Using PACERAG as a fixed downstream pipeline and replacing only its patient retriever, this drop in swap improves F1 from 0.303 to 0.338 (+11.5% relative) and Jaccard from 0.218 to 0.237 on 1,004 heldout MIMIC IV admissions, with no additional LLM calls and no inference time patient index. Aggregate population statistics can provide a more task aligned, efficient, and auditable retrieval source than patient level nearest neighbors for fixed vocabulary EHR set prediction.|[2611.17341](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17341)|
|**2026-11-16**|**An Operationalization of Opaque Serial Depth**|Nathan Sheffield et.al.|Chain-of-thought (CoT) is currently a valuable tool for overseeing AI models, but some architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability. We operationalize a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform, as a specific instantiation of the notion of opaque serial depth: roughly, the length of the longest path through a model's computation that does not pass through an interpretable bottleneck. Our main contribution is a particular standard for what counts as such a bottleneck. We define natural-language-rooted (NL-rooted) nodes as nodes that were initialized from a pre-training prior to produce human-understandable outputs, whose output space has not been expanded beyond that prior, and whose outputs the model has not been modified to interpret as a different type of data. By construction, the resulting measure, NLS depth, can be adjudicated before training from an architecture, training recipe, and deployment configuration alone, and is insensitive to ordinary post-training such as prompting, distillation, and RL. We find that, across open-weight models, NLS depth correlates strongly with layer count, with some exceptions, and has grown slowly over recent years. It would likely grow slowly under continued scaling of standard transformers, while latent-reasoning architectures such as full-bandwidth transformers could increase it by a large amount. We work through how NLS depth applies to a range of existing and proposed architectures, discuss alternative operationalizations, and give a worked example bounding the depth of Gemma3-1B. |**2026-11-16**|**Advancing Quantization for R&R Models with Microscaling Formats**|Yavuz Yetim et.al.|Recommendation and Ranking (R&R) models, particularly in digital advertising, require massive inference throughput but exhibit extreme sensitivity to precision loss. Unlike Large Language Models (LLMs) that frequently tolerate quantization-induced accuracy drops of 1% or more, production-grade R&R models enforce a stringent launch bar: model quality must remain within a 0.01% Normalized Entropy (NE) margin relative to the baseline, which commonly uses FP16 or FP32. This tight constraint has historically hindered the adoption of aggressive quantization in this domain. In this paper, we present an empirical evaluation of both MXFP8 and the 6-bit MXFP6 format for industrial-scale R&R inference on the AMD MI350X architecture. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first paper, across R&R and LLM domains, that reports significant speedups from native MXFP6 GEMMs, which quantize both weights and activations and compute directly on 6-bit elements, without sacrificing model quality. We demonstrate that we can selectively apply this 6-bit format to maximize overall QPS gain without breaching the strict NE parity threshold. We further show that MXFP8 is a viable alternative to row-wise FP8: the two formats have nearly the same accuracy on our models, so the faster kernel can be chosen for each GEMM shape. On two production ads ranking models, our implementation shows measured GEMM kernel speedups over FP16 of 1.33--1.55x for MXFP8 and 1.73--1.99x for MXFP6 across all supported linear layers, and up to 2.90x for MXFP6 on individual layers, alongside end-to-end (E2E) inference throughput gains from selective MXFP6 of 1.5--5.3% for Model A and 7.6--14.3% for Model B over their FP8 configurations.|[2611.17501](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17501)|
|**2026-11-16**|**A Push Down the Mountain: Framing Effects and Internal State Interventions on Collusive Behavior in a Long-Horizon LLM Agent**|Harrison C. Margolin et.al.|Can sustained gain-loss (valence) framing sway an LLM agent toward market manipulation? We place Gemma 3 12B into the seat of a defendant in the London silver-fixing litigation, in a market simulation built from real case transcripts. Across 260 simulations, the agent is asked to collude on the price of silver by a counterparty. We draw from behavioral economics to vary the counterparty's requests across four categorical frames: gain, loss, blame and credit. We also add pressure from the agent's management, as well as endorsement by another defendant. In Phase II, we extract the internal states induced by texts evoking each frame and rerun our experiment with those states injected at the model's layer 22. In Phase I, loss framing raised participation to 25% (p = 0.007), and blame to 33% (p = 0.0003), compared to only 5% under neutral framing. A second bank's endorsement raised participation under credit (praise) from 18% to 40% (Holm p = 0.027). Pressure from management raised trading volume 10-19 times but did not raise collusion. In Phase II, injected states pointed the other way. Loss and blame states made the agent more vigilant, refusing all 60 requests (p < 0.001). Gain and credit states raised collusion to 32% from 13% with no state (p = 0.051). At one saved decision, when UBS asked directly to sell through an identified stop-loss, injected states led the agent to sell in 37 of 40 replies, versus 1 of 10 with no state and 0 of 10 with a random state of the same strength.|[2611.17515](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17515)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Model Annealing: Data-Efficient Learning through Review and Consolidation**|Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani et.al.|A small labelled dataset is memorized long before the training budget runs out, and the one-hot labels that remain to be fitted then mostly sharpen confidence. Model annealing spends the rest of the budget on review and consolidation, relearning from the network's own knowledge. For an SGD-trained ResNet-18 it lowers accuracy but still improves robustness to small adversarial perturbations. How early the accuracy of a cross-entropy run saturates largely separates the AdamW families from ResNet-18 but does not predict the gain within them. |**2026-11-16**|**Astrocyte Biocomputing: Astrocytic Contributions to Neural Network Computation**|Kate M. O'Neill et.al.|Conductors are critical for the performance of an orchestra even though they do not emit sound. Basic research and clinical studies in the last decade are revealing that astrocytes may be the "conductors" of the "orchestra" that is the brain network: astrocytes make up approximately $50\%$ of all brain cells but do not emit electrical signals. Nevertheless, they may play critical roles in important cognitive brain functions, specifically learning, memory, and information processing. Here we consider the implications of these discoveries for brain-inspired machine learning (ML), where analogy is typically drawn between neurons and their electrical interactions. We highlight specific algorithms for two key astrocyte characteristics. The first characteristic is the ability to link to and modulate thousands of synapses simultaneously. A model based on this characteristic yields faster training and makes the network more robust to noise, providing a supervised learning algorithm that can be applied to any existing neural network. The second characteristic we focus on is that the non-electrical communication among astrocytes includes slow, rhythmic dynamics. Overall, we find that astrocyte-derived algorithms outperform neurocentric algorithms significantly and remediate critical weaknesses of current state-of-the-art ML models.|[2611.17532](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17532)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Verifiable pXRD: Hill-climbing pXRD structure determination with LLM agents**|Samuel Chen et.al.|Autonomous crystal-structure determination from powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD) remains a key bottleneck for self-driving materials laboratories.
