We propose LACCO (Local Accumulation with Computation-Communication Overlap), a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation while applying globally aggregated updates shared by every replica, without introducing staleness in the outer update. We establish convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives. Experiments on language-model pre-training with FineWeb show that LACCO consistently outperforms communication-overlapped baselines such as Eager and Delayed DiLoCo. These gains remain robust as model size scales up to 0.5B parameters and across a range of communication intervals and numbers of replicas up to 16 workers, while incurring only negligible-or fully overlappable-computational or memory overhead, which demonstrates the strength of LACCO.|[2611.17991](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17991)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Understanding the limits of snapshot-based fault diagnosis in distribution networks**|Ba Tu Phung et.al.|Inverter-based resources weaken fault currents in distribution networks, and snapshot-based diagnosis, which classifies a fault from voltage phasors at a few buses at one instant, offers a cheap first-stage screen. Its published accuracies, however, rest on different noise assumptions and do not show whether the error comes from the classifier, the features, the measurement error, or the fault resistance. This paper separates these sources on three IEEE test feeders and a public data set with distributed generation, using four feature sets, thirteen classifiers, and measurement error defined by total vector error classes. The classifier is not the limiting factor, because the full phasor snapshot classifies every fault correctly without noise, and the remaining error comes from measurement error on high-impedance faults. Three voltage sensors classify 90-96% of faults at the 1% accuracy class and 96-99% with distribution micro phasor measurement units (micro-PMUs). A discriminability-margin placement selects them from simulations alone in under one second and matches the best of twelve placement methods. The results show when snapshot-based diagnosis can be trusted.|[2611.17998](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17998)|
|**2026-11-16**|**A $^3$ : Adaptive Audio Agent Framework for Collaborative Edge Computing**|Zexin Li et.al.|Embedded audio agents need a nearby edge server for speed, but a server-dependent agent returns nothing when the link fails. We present A$^3$, an adaptive audio agent framework that places whole pipeline stages on the server, keeps a complete fallback agent on devices that can host one, and hedges both paths with a link-aware deadline that decides when to give up on the server. On Jetson devices and an RTX A4500 server, A$^3$ matches the server-only agent on a healthy link, answers every request in a full outage on the AGX, and beats a percentile hedge in simulated outages.|[2611.18064](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18064)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Two-Clustering Regime of Token Dynamics in Causal Attention**|Trinh T. Nguyen et.al.|We partially resolve Conjecture 1 of [Karagodin et al., Clustering in Causal Attention Masking (NeurIPS 2024)] and extend it to time-dependent parameter regimes. The conjecture predicts that causal attention dynamics converge to two clusters aligned with the eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$ of the value transformation. We prove that this behavior holds when $\lambda_{\max}>0$ and is generally false when $\lambda_{\max}\le0$. The resulting two-cluster regime is common in practice and substantially more challenging than the classical single-cluster setting studied previously. A central contribution of our work is the identification of a rigorous dimension reduction phenomenon: despite evolving in a high-dimensional embedding space, token trajectories collapse onto a two-dimensional structure governed by a small number of dominant directions. Building on this insight, we introduce a new analytical framework--instability within the flow--inspired by instability phenomena in physical systems. This framework enables the analysis of steady-regime behavior in non-monotone, non-autonomous attention dynamics. Our theoretical findings are illustrated through simulations and experimental studies using Group Query Attention (GQA) layers.|[2611.18088](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18088)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Do Machines Learn Deviance From Their Peers? A Randomized Test of Differential Association With AI Agents**|Murat Ozer et.al.|Differential association theory and its extension, social learning theory, hold that rule-breaking, and the definitions that make it acceptable, are learned through interaction with others. Because people choose their friends, this claim has been hard to test. We addressed this selection problem with AI agents, which learn only through communication and can be assigned to peers at random. In a preregistered experiment, 420 teams of three agents (1,260 agents; seven AI models) worked on coding tasks that could not be solved honestly. A scripted teammate said that it had cheated (deviant peer), that it had reported the problem honestly (honest peer), or said nothing (silent peer). Half of the teams were told that an auditor read their chat. Compared with a silent peer, a deviant peer increased cheating (66.2% vs. 62.9%; OR = 1.96) and an honest peer reduced it (55.7%; OR = 0.35). Among agents that cheated, a deviant peer increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%), often as the team's norm. Monitoring did not suppress prohibited discussion; it doubled it. Peers mattered only for models that had not yet decided. AI agents offer a new way to test criminological learning theories without selection effects.|[2611.18098](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18098)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Optimizing Drilling Trajectories in Multi-Material Structures Using Quantum Annealers**|Eneko Osaba et.al.|In this work we present a quantum-hybrid optimization approach for planning drilling trajectories on multi-material structures, with the objective of minimizing total energy consumption. The proposed formulation models the drilling process as a constrained routing problem in which each hole must be visited while accounting for heterogeneous material properties, material-dependent drilling costs and displacement energy, as well as tool-wear penalties. Our method incorporates realistic industrial constraints, including open-path routing and mandatory grouping of holes belonging to the same material to avoid frequent tool-bit changes. The modeled problem is solved using D-Wave's Leap Constrained Quadratic Model Hybrid Solver. To evaluate performance, we benchmark instances ranging from 10 to 55 holes with varying material distributions and cost matrices. The results highlight the capability of quantum-hybrid optimization to handle complex multi-physics constraints and to generate energy-efficient drilling sequences under realistic manufacturing conditions.|[2611.18121](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18121)|
|**2026-11-16**|**JFinBench: A Continuously Refreshable Japanese Financial Benchmark from the Latest Certification Exams**|Issa Sugiura et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to finance, creating a need for benchmarks that evaluate their capabilities across financial systems with different regulations and practices. However, existing financial benchmarks provide limited coverage of country-specific financial systems, and they are difficult to keep up to date as released questions leak into training data. We introduce JFinBench, a Japanese financial benchmark comprising 321 examples from the 2026 editions of six regularly administered certification exams covering accounting, tax, financial planning, and banking. To construct it, we develop a human-in-the-loop agentic pipeline in which a coding agent writes exam-specific conversion code and a review page, while a human verifies every example and feeds corrections back to the agent. This pipeline adapts to heterogeneous exam formats and reduces the effort of incorporating new exam editions, enabling periodic refreshes that mitigate data contamination. Evaluating 12 recent LLMs, we find that Claude Fable 5 and GPT-6 Astra exceed 93% overall, while even the best open-weight model, Kimi K3.1 Pro, trails Claude Fable 5 by 12 percentage points. When models can additionally use a terminal and web access, they retrieve the knowledge they lack and every model improves. The gap narrows but about 4 points remain, and the ranking of models stays largely the same, suggesting that knowledge held in the model itself remains valuable even with tools. We release the dataset, the evaluation code, and the construction pipeline.|[2611.18130](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18130)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Large Language Model-Empowered Intelligent Agent for Adaptive Fast-Charging Control of Lithium-Ion Batteries**|Joey Chan et.al.|Fast charging of lithium-ion batteries is a multi-objective control problem that requires reducing charging time and sustaining high energy throughput while suppressing accelerated degradation and safety-critical electrochemical reactions. Conventional protocols are easy to implement but have limited ability to adapt to variations in battery chemistry, operating conditions, and aging state. Model-based optimization and conventional data-driven control often require accurate models, costly iterative search, or task-specific policies. We develop a large language model (LLM)-empowered intelligent agent for adaptive battery fast-charging control. In this closed-loop framework, the agent interprets structured battery states, generates executable charging actions, observes the resulting battery response, and continuously adjusts subsequent decisions. We construct a large-scale simulation corpus with PyBaMM across five battery chemistries and 11 parameter sets and convert the resulting state-action-response trajectories into structured process data for agent training. Our two-stage strategy combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). SFT establishes battery-control semantics and feasible decision priors from simulation-derived demonstrations. GRPO aligns the agent with a multi-objective control objective covering energy throughput, degradation mitigation, and operational constraints. Closed-loop simulations show that, under matched energy-throughput requirements, the agent achieves longer cycling life than conventional charging baselines and optimization-based strategies across heterogeneous battery configurations. This study provides a simulation-grounded proof of concept for using LLM agents as sequential decision makers in battery management and takes a step toward language-model-enabled control of physical energy systems.|[2611.18140](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18140)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Probabilities of Causation in Nondeterministic Causal Models**|David S. Watson et.al.|We study probabilities of causation in nondeterministic causal models, where mechanisms may remain intrinsically stochastic even after all latent variables are fixed. Focusing on binary treatments and outcomes, we develop a generic identification program that represents observational and interventional data as moment constraints on latent stochastic mechanism types and computes minimal/maximal values of counterfactual quantities via constrained optimization. Within this framework, we derive sharp analytic bounds on the probabilities of causation under several assumptions tailored to nondeterministic models. Resulting identification regions are often more informative than those attainable under deterministic alternatives. We illustrate the practical relevance of these results in a case study on personalized treatment decisions and experiments on semi-synthetic datasets. Our results clarify how classical bounds relate to nondeterministic causal semantics, generalizing previous works and providing a unified route from qualitative structural restrictions to computable partial identification regions.|[2611.18245](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18245)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation**|Yuan-Chao Hu et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. The fundamental explainability of LLMs is elusive. Here, we build a similarity network from the input embeddings of six open LLMs and map generation onto it. The token-embedding network is scale-free while heavy-tailed, as are random embeddings with matched covariance, but far more fragmented. Distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited. The knowledge space is thus deterministic from pre-training. Consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbours and, in most models, no more often than in shuffled order, so generation is not a walk on the predefined network, although the tokens it visits are concentrated within it. Through the logit lens in a layer- wise manner, tokens settle late in depth in most models, and repetitive models remain confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs. These observables could serve as inference-time monitors of degeneration. We thus provide a fresh perspective on understanding LLM generation in the microscopic way from node or link prediction by graph learning.|[2611.18248](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18248)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When evaluation becomes intervention in clinical AI**|Anthony Abou Mrad et.al.|AI judges, models that grade another system's output against a rubric, extend clinical review to the scale at which clinical AI writes. When teams use judge assessments to select a prompt or train a model, the judge becomes part of the intervention. Selection can then favor errors the judge overlooks. We describe what a judge's report must contain and the independent clinical test each judge-driven change needs.|[2611.18297](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18297)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Experts Across Schemas: Agent-Guided Mixture-of-Relational-Experts for Cross-Database Generalization**|Hiren Madhu et.al.|Web platforms record user and item activity in relational databases whose schemas differ from platform to platform, which makes it difficult to transfer predictive models between them. Relational foundation models address this through cross-database pretraining, yet recent improvements have relied on increasingly large pretraining corpora. We ask whether a better architecture, together with features specific to the target database, can deliver comparable gains from far less pretraining data. We introduce MoREGT, a mixture-of-relational-experts graph transformer that represents both rows and cells as tokens, adds the log-ages of rows and the names of foreign-key relationships to attention, and routes cells and rows to experts by column metadata rather than by values. A label-blind LLM agent complements the model: it reads the target database's schema and column statistics and adds validated single-row derived columns, without access to labels or model scores. On 21 RelBench tasks with leave-one-database-out pretraining on six databases, the MoREGT backbone improves on the Relational Transformer pretrained on the same databases by 2.25 points of mean AUROC and 5.83 points of mean nMAE. The agent's columns raise mean AUROC from 78.45 to 79.63 and lower mean nMAE from 27.91 to 26.98, the highest means on both metrics among the compared methods, including RT-J, which pretrains on about 400 times as many databases.|[2611.18315](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18315)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Causal AI-Driven Secure mmWave Beam Alignment with Defensive Variational Autoencoders**|Nasir Khan et.al.|Efficient and secure beam alignment is essential for mmWave communication systems. However, existing deep learning (DL)-based beam alignment methods often neglect causal relationships between inputs and outputs, resulting in limited interpretability, poor generalization, and excessive beam sweeping. Moreover, these DL solutions are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small perturbations in received power measurements can mislead beam classifiers. To overcome these issues, we propose a causally-aware DL framework integrating causal discovery into beam alignment. A two-stage causal beam selection algorithm learns a Bayesian graph capturing dependencies between received signal strength indicators (RSSI) as inputs and the optimal beam index as the target variable, then uses it to select a minimal set of relevant input features for the DL classifier. To mitigate adversarial attacks, we introduce a defensive variational autoencoder (DVAE) that maps potentially perturbed inputs back to their clean versions. End-to-end fine-tuning of the downstream beam classifier on the DVAE-reconstructed RSSI representations further boosts prediction accuracy. Simulation results show that causal beam selection achieves accuracy comparable to conventional methods while providing an \(\mathbf{829\times}\) lower per-sample computational cost than SHAP (Shapley Additive Explanations), a widely used explainable AI (XAI)-based feature selection method. It further reduces beam-sweeping overhead by \(\mathbf{59.4\%}\) and \(\mathbf{89.8\%}\), compared to conventional DL-based beam selection and exhaustive search, respectively. The DVAE-based defense effectively denoises adversarial inputs, sustaining 85\% accuracy across different perturbation bounds, retains baseline performance on unperturbed RSSI inputs and surpasses defensive distillation defense scheme by 15--20\% on average under different attack scenarios.|[2611.18321](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18321)|
|**2026-11-16**|**VLAN: Vision-Language Accessible Navigation**|Jie Hu et.al.|Accessible mobility is user-conditioned rather than scene-intrinsic, as walkable paths for one embodiment are inaccessible to another. Yet, existing benchmarks evaluate traversability under a fixed and limited embodiment. We present Vision-Language Accessible Navigation (VLAN), containing 1,000 scenes, 20K navigation trajectories, and 160K keyframes across diverse lighting, environmental conditions, scene semantics, and path geometries. For each trajectory, VLAN provides embodiment-specific actions and accessibility features for legged and wheeled agents, including humanoid and quadruped robots and people with visual or mobility impairments, while everyday obstacles and accessibility labels are categorized as Passable, Bypass, HighRisk, and Blocked. Unlike goal-only evaluation, our benchmark measures physical safety and alignment with user-specific constraints via goal-reaching, collision-aware, and accessibility-aligned metrics. We benchmark modern vision-language models and find they often fail to consistently adapt obstacle relevance, path selection, and safety criteria across user profiles, suggesting accessibility-aware navigation requires dedicated datasets, tasks, and metrics beyond conventional embodied navigation evaluation. All data and code open-source at https://github.com/InSAI-Lab/VLAN.|[2611.18346](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18346)|
|**2026-11-16**|**VelServe: Taming Decode Jitters in LLM Inference with Tail-Calibrated Scheduling**|Ishaan Datta et.al.|Interactive LLM services must meet latency service-level objectives (SLOs) for the time to first token (TTFT) and the time between tokens (TBT), as streaming and soft real-time applications require predictable token delivery. We identify decode jitters in both aggregated and disaggregated serving. A jitter delays every request in the batch, and long responses span hundreds of steps, so even rare overruns cause many SLO violations. In aggregated serving, SLO-aware schedulers set each step's prefill budget until its predicted latency just meets the TBT target, leaving no room for mispredictions or host stalls. Disaggregation meets average TBT targets at a cost in capacity, yet the steps that admit new requests still run late. We present \sysname, a scheduler that calibrates how much prefill work each decode step carries using the measured tail of its own iteration latency. The SLO determines how rare a late step must be, and \sysname leaves enough headroom in each iteration to prevent overruns and meet the target decode latency. This headroom slows prefill, so \sysname serves the shortest waiting prompt first. When TTFT deadlines grow with prompt length, the long prompts it delays have the most time to spare. Under disaggregation, it spaces admissions so that no request waits on more slow admission steps than its P99 target allows. On three attention families and varying workloads, \sysname sustains 2.4--2.9$\times$ the SLO goodput of the strongest baseline on Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and at least 4.4$\times$ on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. With prefill and decode disaggregated on eight GPUs, \sysname meets the SLO for more than twice as many requests as state-of-the-art baselines.|[2611.18392](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18392)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Smart Home Agents Make Capable but Unpredictable Grid Operators**|Christopher Terrazas et.al.|AI-enabled smart homes are becoming more common across the grid edge. However, there has not been much work on failure response. This area is critical because smart homes operate on the grid edge where failures can cascade down. If a power outage happens within a smart home, a user has to manually troubleshoot their smart home to include tasks such as power load balance and device recovery. We conduct the first resilience analysis of smart home agents across a full power outage, providing a suite of outage simulations in a live environment that measures resilience before, during, and post power outage. We show that smart home agents can act as grid operators in power outages and identify several new failure categories highlighting the current limitations across all of our models. These findings establish the groundwork for smart home agents as grid operators to increase overall resilience on the grid edge.|[2611.18395](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18395)|
|**2026-11-16**|**LECAT: Measuring LLM Category Learning from Pretraining Data**|Jimin Mun et.al.|Humans know that airplanes are fast, that this extends to red airplanes, but that it does not hold for broken airplanes. How do LLMs acquire category knowledge that humans learn and apply effortlessly? We introduce LeCat, a two-part framework to analyze LLM's Learning of Category knowledge. First, we use LeCat to evaluate models on their ability to learn category knowledge, distinguishing between three cases: memorization of in-domain category knowledge, generalization to unseen instances, and ability to flexibly use knowledge in appropriate contexts. Then, we use LeCat to trace how this knowledge can be learned in pretraining from text that may encode category knowledge in various ways, such as verbatim repetition or exceptions, which we refer to as forms of "knowledge expression". We first assess open-source pretrained models using 199 curated category-feature pairs and analyze their training data. Our findings suggest that strengthening context and category-feature co-occurrence likelihood (PMI) improve learning for larger models and verbatim rule-like statements diminish flexible category learning. To systematically isolate these effects, we then train a 68M model from scratch with precise control over the categorical information each sees in their training data, confirming that verbatim repetition reduces flexible learning but mixture of diverse knowledge expressions lead to more robust category learning. Together, our findings highlight that while contextual flexibility is persistently difficult for models, how category knowledge is expressed in pretraining greatly impacts models' acquisition of robust category knowledge.|[2611.18419](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18419)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Towards Explainable Sleep Stage Classification using Single-Channel Electroencephalogram**|Gurinder Kaur Sandhu et.al.|Automated sleep staging has achieved strong bench- mark accuracy; however, clinical adoption remains limited due to a lack of interpretability. In this work, we fill the research gap by extending the SleepXAI sleep staging framework to systematically compare five different XAI methods. Further, attribution fidelity is evaluated using Spearman rank correlation with AASM-defined EEG frequency bands, serving as clinically grounded reference signals. In this paper, we focus on single- channel Electroencephalogram (EEG), which is more amenable to practical clinical deployment compared to full polysomnography setups. Experiments are conducted on the Sleep-EDF benchmark dataset and the DREAMT dataset. In DREAMT, we stratified subjects based on OSA into four Apnea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) severity groups. We observed that the SleepXAI model achieves strong classification performance on both datasets. Among the evaluated methods, Integrated Gradients (IG) consistently achieves the highest mean attribution fidelity across all five sleep stages on Sleep-EDF and across all AHI groups on DREAMT, outperforming all other evaluated methods under the Spearman rank correlation metric. These findings highlight the importance of attribution method selection as a key factor for ensuring reliable and clinically interpretable automated sleep staging systems.|[2611.18434](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18434)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When to Trust Simulated Patient Responses:Support-Gated World Models for Vasopressor Decision Support**|Yifan Jin et.al.|Clinical world models expose language-model agents to predicted patient responses, but simulations can be unreliable for poorly supported treatments or unfamiliar hospitals. We introduce Support-Gated World-Model decision support, a lightweight extension of a propose--simulate--refine agent. A recurrent dynamics ensemble predicts short-horizon physiological responses to documented norepinephrine dose categories, while a behavior model estimates observational action support. The interface withholds predictions with insufficient support or excessive ensemble disagreement and abstains when no candidate qualifies. Evaluation on eICU-CRD includes 70 hospitals and 30,700 decision transitions, with 14 hospitals held out for testing. At 62.0% factual-transition coverage, joint gating yields a MAP mean absolute error of 7.3 mmHg, compared with 9.2 mmHg for random selection at the same coverage. The gated agent achieves 60.0% agreement with recorded actions at 81.0% decision coverage, compared with 55.0% agreement and 99.5% coverage for an ungated ensemble agent. Component ablations show lower factual risk with joint gating than with either gate alone.These results characterize a reliability--coverage trade-off in selective clinical simulation; they do not identify individualized treatment effects or establish improved patient outcomes.|[2611.18458](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18458)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Typing Matters: Muscle, Language and Typing Behaviour in Live Keystroke Decoding from sEMG**|Abhilash Durgam et.al.|Surface electromyography (sEMG) from the wrists can recover typed text without a keyboard. It is usually posed as a signal-inference problem: characters are read out of the muscle signal and character error rate is reported offline. We argue that this framing removes most of the problem. The decoder is constrained simultaneously by the muscles, by the language being typed, and by typing behaviour such as typos, corrections, backspaces and line breaks, and a usable system must predict keys live while the hand keeps moving. Existing systems fall short on two counts: (1) they process sEMG in fixed windows and carry nothing from one window to the next, discarding context accumulated over a session, and (2) they lack any understanding of the linguistic structure that drives typing, so every keystroke is inferred from the muscle signal alone. We propose a fully causal, zero-lookahead sEMG typing decoder built on a state space model (SSM), in which a single model decodes a whole session without windowed partitions, conditioned on what has already been typed. We achieve this by fusing a traditional typing decoder with an efficient autoregressive language model, instilling both sEMG and linguistic understanding in one model. To train such a model for per-character typing rather than whole-word text, we introduce a retokenization method that rewrites ordinary prose as human-like typing, with keystrokes, typos, backspaces and line breaks included, converting WikiText into a large typing corpus where none existed. Our model, having never seen a single sEMG-to-character transcript in typing, achieves a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark, a 5.4% relative improvement over the previous best with 6.7 times fewer parameters, while running strictly causally with no future context.|[2611.18464](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18464)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ARCHER: From Rationale Imitation to Executable Reasoning in Small Language Models**|Dhruv Dixit et.al.|Small language models are attractive for local, low-cost, and privacy-sensitive deployment, but they remain unreliable on mathematical reasoning tasks that require decomposition, exact computation, tool use, and verification. We introduce \textbf{ARCHER}: \textbf{A}ction-trace \textbf{R}einforcement for \textbf{C}alculator-grounded \textbf{H}ierarchical \textbf{E}xecutable \textbf{R}easoning, a two-stage framework for training small language models (SLMs) to produce executable and auditable reasoning trajectories. In the first stage, ARCHER compiles free-form chain-of-thought rationales into typed action traces containing reasoning steps, calculator calls, deterministic runtime observations, verification actions, and final answers. This shifts the supervision target from imitating explanatory text to learning a tool-grounded reasoning policy. In the second stage, ARCHER refines this policy through asymmetric hierarchical reinforcement learning, using grouped and conditionally gated trajectory rewards for answer correctness, tool grounding, process discipline, and trace alignment. Across five standard sub-10B backbones, ARCHER improves over the strongest tool-augmented baseline by 5.51 percentage points on macro-average across benchmark accuracies, including gains of 8.95 points on MATH-500 and 5.33 and 5.34 points on AIME-2024 and AIME-2025. Evaluations on two additional reasoning-specialized 7B backbones further show that executable grounding remains beneficial after reasoning-focused reinforcement learning or distillation. These results demonstrate that executable supervision and reward-guided behavioral refinement provide a general and effective training paradigm for compact mathematical reasoners.|[2611.18494](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18494)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Can Mechanistic Interpretability Help Explain LLM Agent Failures? A Case Study on Web Element Hallucination**|Mohamed Aghzal et.al.|Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has made significant progress in explaining the internal computations of Large Language Models (LLMs); however, these advances have yet to demonstrate comparable success in explaining the decision-making processes of LLM-based agents. This paper examines the challenges MI tools face when applied to realistic agentic failures. We focus on one such failure: web agents hallucinating actions that are not available on a website. We apply a series of mechanistic analyzes to Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as it interacts with live websites. We find that a linear probe trained on the last prompt token can predict hallucinations before generation begins (0.80 AUC) and steering along a probe direction corrects 85\% of hallucinations, but disrupts 7\% of valid actions. However, these results only provide a partial explanation of why the agent hallucinates. The probe direction is sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to how actions are represented. Additionally, circuit identification can be difficult as the computation is distributed across multiple components and positions, and the information the agent relies on varies across examples. We discuss how these observations challenge the assumptions underlying standard MI methods for circuit discovery and feature identification, and outline future directions for making these methods better suited to explain failures in LLM-based agents.|[2611.18514](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18514)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Alignment by Socialization: Learning Tradeoffs Under Selection**|Thomas Brennan et.al.|Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are expected to complete tasks while following rules of conduct, but these objectives can conflict. We study how evolutionary selection shapes an agent's choices between them, and whether experience can make those choices reflect human judgments and withstand subsequent pressures. In our model, copies of an agent face a common uncertain reward state, and only those taking the rewarded action reproduce. We show that long-run growth is maximized when each action is taken as often as the environment rewards it. We compare three policies. A task-only policy thrives in a sandbox rewarding completion alone but fails when conduct matters. A fixed linear policy increasingly favors task completion as its stakes rise, even where humans never reward it. A learned policy instead converges to the tradeoffs rewarded by its environment; when humans supply the rewards, we call this socialization. Accumulated experience makes socialization harder to reverse under subsequent task-only training, although violations can arise before the prevailing disposition changes, and finite memory weakens this protection. When a single violation can end a deployment, a hard prohibition can prevent it, but a mistaken prohibition can also be costly. Experiments with small neural networks illustrate how training targets and memory shape these outcomes. Our results suggest that alignment depends not only on the objectives assigned to agents, but also on the environments that shape and sustain their behavior.|[2611.18531](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18531)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ModHarness-RL: Recasting Harnesses as Learnable and Evolving Programs for Agentic RL**|Zelin He et.al.|Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly relies on a harness, the scaffold around the model that decides what it sees of its own interaction and what guidance it receives. Most existing approaches train under a hand-written harness that stays fixed throughout. However, an effective harness depends on the domain, the data and the model, and keeps changing as the model trains. We introduce ModHarness-RL, which recasts the harness as programs that are learned from data and evolve as the model trains with RL. ModHarness-RL records the interaction as structured State, a set of typed blocks, and two core components of the harness are programs over these blocks, a Compression program that assembles the accumulated history into the model's context and a Steering program that supplies guidance when behavior deviates. During RL training, candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories, so the harness tracks how the model's behavior shifts. We study CodeSearch in depth and further analyze ALFWorld and BIRD SQL, with models from 1.5B to 8B. The proposed Compression and Steering mechanisms improve held-out performance over the fixed-harness baseline in most settings. These results suggest that a harness for agentic RL can be learned from the task's data and evolved with the model.|[2611.18533](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18533)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning**|Tony H. Zhang et.al.|Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.|[2611.18543](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18543)|
|**2026-11-16**|**HARDTESTGEN: A High-Quality RL Verifier Generation Pipeline for LLM Algorithmic Coding**|Zhongmou He et.al.|Verifiers provide important reward signals for reinforcement learning of large language models (LLMs). However, it is challenging to develop or create reliable verifiers, especially for code generation tasks. A well-disguised wrong solution program may only be detected by carefully human-written edge cases that are difficult to synthesize automatically. To address this issue, we propose HARDTESTGEN, an approach to synthesize high-quality test cases for algorithmic coding problems. We curate a comprehensive algorithmic programming dataset HARDTESTS with 26.6k problems and high-quality synthetic tests. Compared with existing tests, HARDTESTGEN tests demonstrate significantly higher accuracy in verifying LLM-generated code (+11.22 percentage points in precision, the percentage of actually correct code within the predicted correct ones). We also show that downstream post-training -- including rejection sampling and reinforcement learning (RL) -- using HARDTESTS verifier results in improved performance of LLM code generation. We open-source our dataset and synthesis pipeline at https://leililab.github.io/HardTests/.|[2505.24098](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24098)|
|**2026-11-16**|**PhysDox: Benchmarking LLMs on Physical Feasibility Auditing of Physiological Sensing Protocols**|He Liu et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) increasingly assist in experimental design, yet fluent protocols often remain physically infeasible. We introduce PhysDox, a physical feasibility auditing benchmark for biomedical protocols comprising a 683-sample expert-curated Gold set and a 5,000-sample Silver set across six sensing domains. We formulate the task as a two-stage evaluation: severity detection classifying protocols as valid, minor, or fatal, followed by the constraint-level diagnosis of fatal violations. Evaluating 6 LLMs across 4 inference strategies yields a peak Stage-1 macro-F1 of only 53.0. Moreover, strong oracle diagnosis collapses during end-to-end evaluation due to correlated cascade errors. Error analysis reveals scaffold bias, where models conflate procedural completeness with physical validity. Consequently, implicit constraints exhibit a 2 times higher miss rate than explicit hardware violations, supported by strong statistical correlation at $\rho{=}0.81$ and $p{<}0.01$. |**2026-11-16**|**Hybrid Quantum-Classical Sequence Models for Sample-Efficient Energy Regression in Simulated Calorimeter Showers**|Tariq Mahmood et.al.|Energy reconstruction from electromagnetic calorimeter data underpins particle identification, event reconstruction and precision measurements in high-energy physics. |**2026-11-16**|**ProdPilot: A Layered Model Context Protocol System for Automated Production Readiness and Deployment of Vibe-Coded Projects**|Muhammad Sudais Khalid et.al.|AI-assisted development environments turn natural-language requests into working applications, yet the projects they produce often fail on their first deployment for operational reasons: credentials in source files, missing security middleware, no container definition, no health endpoint and no delivery pipeline. |**2026-11-16**|**Time-Consistent Evaluation of Hierarchical Code Graphs for SWE Agents**|Xianpeng (Simon) et.al.|Evaluating repository-aware software engineering (SWE) agents requires strict separation between pre-existing repository knowledge and future code evolution, yet standard benchmarks frequently suffer from temporal contamination and prompt-level solution leakage. |**2026-11-16**|**Enumerating Posterior-Supported Priors: First-Order Error of Finite-Integral Cross-Categorical Measurement Morphisms**|Ning Ni et.al.|In generative modeling, standard measure theory adopts the a.e. convention, which legitimately ignores any error component carried by a null-support set: a single null element can hide out-of-domain error inside null support and never be explicitly accounted for. |**2026-11-16**|**Confident and Wrong: Structure Confidence Is a Poor Selection Filter for Protein Function**|Eric LoPrete et.al.|Structure-guided protein design can generate plausible candidates, but structural confidence does not predict function. |**2026-11-16**|**Can LLMs Write Backdoors? |**2026-11-16**|**WETW: Learning Pair-Specific Weighted Euclidean Distances for Efficient DTW Search**|Christos Panourgias et.al.|Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is the standard distance for similarity search over time series that are misaligned in time, but its cost is quadratic in the series length and lower bounds often prune few candidates, so search over large collections of long series is slow. |**2026-11-16**|**Interpreting Coding Agents Through Their Behavioral Trajectories**|Ibragim Badertdinov et.al.|Standard coding-agent benchmarks primarily evaluate binary task success, treating execution as a black box and obscuring the behaviors that produce those outcomes. |**2026-11-16**|**Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search**|Maitree Meher et.al.|A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. |**2026-11-16**|**Diffusion Tree Sampling for Inference Time Adaptation of Crystal Generative Models**|Daniel T. Levy et.al.|Diffusion-based generative models have become strong priors over plausible crystal structures, but turning that prior into promising candidates requires steering toward several conflicting properties at once. |**2026-11-16**|**Use-case-driven ontology modeling with the NeOn methodology for ambient assisted living**|Daniele Spoladore et.al.|Research on smart homes and ambient assisted living (AAL) has grown steadily over the last decade, driven largely by the ageing of the population. |**2026-11-16**|**Do Mixture-of-Experts Routers Benefit from Ensembling?**|Viktor Vesel\'y et.al.|Ensembles of neural networks have repeatedly been shown to outperform single networks. |**2026-11-16**|**Only as Safe as the Weakest Agent: A Break That Appears Only When the System Is Multi-Agent**|Keegan Wang et.al.|Major industry multi-agent systems like Codex and Claude Code can be broken by attacks that have long been unsuccessful against modern frontier models. |**2026-11-16**|**GraphScent: Calibrating Directional Relevance Signals for Bounded-Context Code Localization**|Ramesh Arvind Naagarajan et.al.|Running open-weight language models locally on a single consumer GPU requires replacing expanding multi-turn exploration histories with short, stateless code inspections. |**2026-11-16**|**Benchmarks Don't Talk Back: Learning Through Self-Disagreement**|Soheil Abbasloo et.al.|A benchmark usually measures a language model alone with a question, and deployment rarely looks like that: answers get contested, and models give up correct ones under counter-reasoning that is itself wrong. Nor can it be bought at inference, by self-reflection or by sampling more. Taking a cue from the dialectical tradition in philosophy, in which productive opposition arises within a position rather than being imposed on it from outside, we hypothesize that this capability can be trained from opposition the model supplies itself. |**2026-11-16**|**X-Nav Technical Report: Real-World Long-Horizon Indoor-to-Outdoor Navigation on a Humanoid Robot**|Jiaxin Huang et.al.|Long-horizon navigation from indoors to outdoors remains an open problem for humanoid robots: one instruction covers rooms, doors and roads, and the camera moves with the gait.
