Across six benchmarks spanning REL and OVL, CoTTAGE achieves state-of-the-art correlation with human judgments while producing human-preferred explanations to accelerate TTA development.|[2611.17644](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17644)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Binarization as an Attack Surface in Weightless Neural Networks**|Lucas Senos Coutinho et.al.|Weightless Neural Networks (WNNs) are lightweight classifiers that binarize their inputs and learn by storing binary patterns in lookup memories, which makes them attractive for resource-constrained tasks such as intrusion detection. Because a small perturbation that crosses no binarization threshold leaves the binary input unchanged, WNNs may appear naturally robust to adversarial examples; a perturbation that does cross a threshold, however, can still change the prediction. We study this tension in WiSARD, the canonical RAM-based WNN, and in two variants that replace its memories with Bloom filters, Bloom WiSARD and BTHOWeN. A continuous perturbation reaches these classifiers only by crossing an encoding threshold, changing at least one binary tuple, and redirecting a memory query; we formalize this mechanism with a threshold-crossing bound and a response-margin certificate for standard Boolean WiSARD. Controlled MNIST experiments compare linear and data-driven thermometer encodings under model-agnostic sensitivity probes, a score-based Simulated Annealing attack, and surrogate-transfer attacks. Changing only the encoding while keeping each model otherwise fixed shows that encoding granularity can trade a small clean-accuracy loss for a large decrease in attack success. On the Bot-IoT, CICIDS, and UNSW-NB15 intrusion-detection datasets, adversarial perturbations lead WNN detectors to classify many attacks as normal traffic and to raise false alarms, even when their aggregate accuracy remains high.|[2611.17646](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17646)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed Loop Cost**|Christopher Malili et.al.|Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $\alpha = V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $\alpha$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $\alpha$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $\alpha$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $\alpha$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.|[2611.17658](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Agent-written heuristics versus commercial solvers on unsolved job-shop benchmark instances**|Andre Albrecht et.al.|Commercial solvers are the standard tool for difficult scheduling problems. We study whether a frontier large language model, used as a coding agent with open-source tools only, can match them in quality and speed. We report every run, and independent checkers verify every schedule. On the 100,000-operation job shop tai_100_1000_1, an agent-written solver reached 525,515, below OptalCP, Hexaly, and CP Optimizer. Unchanged, it improved all ten instances of the family and closed two. With equal budgets on the same workstation, it ended 0.0-1.8% above the lower bound, against 2.6-5.8% for CP Optimizer and 2.4-4.4% for Hexaly. On the Behnke-Geiger flexible job shops, an agent-written tabu search improved 11 of 32 open instances. With two transport robots, an agent-written solver improved seven of ten MKT instances. New lower bounds show that four published MKT values cannot be attained on the distributed instance files. Agent-run improvement campaigns closed eight further instances.|[2611.17682](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17682)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Logically Consistent Accurate Forecasts**|Kate Yuan et.al.|Agentic forecasting systems based on large language models (LLMs) are now competitive with expert human forecasters, but these systems still suffer from a critical limitation: the inability to produce forecasts that are logically consistent. For example, forecasts for related events often fail to satisfy the axioms of probability, which in turn implies that related forecasts cannot simultaneously be correct and undermines their utility for decision makers. Enforcing consistency is not trivial -- naive approaches (e.g., projection onto the set of consistent forecasts) can degrade accuracy. We argue that the key to overcoming the apparent tradeoff between consistency and accuracy lies in the reasoning which supports each forecast. We finetune a supervisory language model that leverages this reasoning to jointly reconcile inconsistencies while improving the accuracy of related forecasts. On ForecastBench, our method improves the Brier score of a retrieval-augmented GPT-5.1 forecaster by 9.3% while reducing measures of structural inconsistency by 82-90%. We interrogate our method via a comprehensive series of baselines and ablations, and find that no alternative method matches our gains in consistency and accuracy simultaneously.|[2611.17735](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17735)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Superfluid: Local AI Server for Distributed LLM Inference**|Prabod Rathnayaka et.al.|Running large language models (LLMs) on local devices keeps data private, removes per-token cost, cuts network latency, and keeps working without a connection. Two shifts now strain how such models are served. On one device, several agents share a locally served model with an interactive user. Across devices, a developer or small team often owns several capable machines from different vendors, which sit idle most of the day because every local server serves one runtime on one machine. We present Superfluid, an open-source LLM server built for local inference at both scales. On one device, Superfluid drives llama.cpp, MLX, or BaseRT in isolated worker processes through one scheduler that moves interactive requests ahead of agent work, computes a prompt shared by many requests once, and writes every token to a durable log before it reaches the client. Across devices, one Superfluid server turns a fleet of machines into a single API endpoint. Machines with different hardware serve copies of the same model, receive work according to their measured speed, and can join or fail under load without losing a request. For Qwen3.8-27B on an Apple M5 Pro, a chat sent while eight agents are working receives its first token in 1.5 s, against 291-612 s on llama-server, Ollama, and mlx_lm.server. On six machines from three chip vendors serving 64 simulated users for one hour, Superfluid answers all 5,041 requests while machines join one at a time and one is killed and rejoins; throughput grows from 112 to 534 tok/s and the median first-token time falls from 99 to 12 s. On twelve Mac Studio M3 Ultras it sustains 10,656 tok/s across 38,563 requests from 384 agents without a failure. Superfluid is available under the Apache-2.0 licence at https://github.com/basecompute/superfluid.|[2611.17753](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17753)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Creative Product Design under Brand Image Constraints**|Takeshi Onishi et.al.|Industrial design requires creative visual exploration while satisfying constraints that are often product-specific and implicit. Existing text-to-image (T2I) approaches can generate creative designs through concept blending or multi-agent self-enhancement, but they provide limited mechanisms for ensuring that generated designs remain consistent with such constraints. We focus on brand image as a representative design constraint and propose a framework for brand-aware redesign generation. Given a product name, the framework constructs a brand image survey report that summarizes product and market information, derives a creative direction from the report, and integrates this direction into a self-enhancement loop in which generated images are critiqued and prompts are revised. We evaluate the framework on 50 redesign tasks across five product categories, comparing it with Naive T2I, IP-Adapter/IT-Blender, and unconstrained self-enhancement under the same T2I backbone. By a VLM-as-Judge protocol to measure creativity and brand identifiability, the proposed framework achieves a more favorable trade-off between the two objectives, with particularly clear gains in highly constrained categories such as automotive and motorcycle, where the plausible design space is relatively limited compared with that of other product categories. These results suggest that dynamically eliciting and textualizing brand image can guide creative product design generation under brand image constraints.|[2611.17762](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17762)|
|**2026-11-16**|**GDP.xlsx: Benchmarking Frontier Models on Professional Workbooks**|Suhaas Garre et.al.|We introduce GDP_xlsx, a benchmark of 70 realistic workplace requests written by professionals in 13 domains, each paired with an expert rubric of binary criteria and with the complete workbook or CSV file it refers to. Together the files hold 2.18 million populated cells, and the rubrics 791 criteria. Agents work in a container with spreadsheet tools, and an agentic judge that can open the file applies the rubric. Completeness and exclusivity criteria require set-valued answers to be found in full and with nothing extra. We rank models by strict pass@1, the share of attempts that meet every criterion. Across 20 model configurations, including frontier models from Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, the top-scoring one, Gemini 4 Argon, passes only 38.3% of attempts, and the median score is 19.3%, leaving wide headroom. We release the evaluation harness and three public tasks; the other 67 are held out to protect the leaderboard from contamination.|[2611.17766](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17766)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Activation Steering the Nan-Shan in Typhoon**|Megan Wei et.al.|Typhoon is an audiovisual installation based on Joseph Conrad's novella, in which Captain MacWhirr steers the steamer Nan-Shan directly into and through a catastrophic storm. The novella is a study of what happens when the scale of a phenomenon exceeds the practical and conceptual frameworks available to comprehend it and of the paralysis that sometimes follows a demand for action in such overwhelming circumstances. We chose the text of Typhoon to reflect on human culture's current AI moment. We prompt a large language model, seeded with Conrad's original language, to feed endlessly on its own output, and explore when and how the text becomes increasingly alien and unwieldy as time progresses, including deviations from the narrative, digressions into the model's own instructions and thinking, and long periods of incoherent rumination. We employ methods from activation steering, including a novel adaptive steering method we introduce, to control the rhythm of the narrative and the model's digressions over long stretches of generation. The simultaneous efficacy and unpredictability of this steering showcases the tension between the perceived interpretability of AI, and the reality of a system that remains outside our comprehension and control.|[2611.17768](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17768)|
|**2026-11-16**|**LuthorSQL: A One-Pass Text-to-SQL Model as System 1 for a Slower LLM Agent**|Ibrahim Memon et.al.|Frontier LLM agents that query databases with tools are among the most accurate text-to-SQL systems, but per-query calls to a hosted model are slow, costly, and expose sensitive organizational queries to external APIs. For example, a Claude Opus 5.5 agent serving 10,000 queries a day costs over $70,000 a year at list price. To what extent can local hardware already owned by an organization offload this burden without compromising the agent's accuracy? We present LuthorSQL, a non-autoregressive 7B text-to-SQL model that serves as a rapid System 1 gate for a slower LLM agent (System 2), escalating queries only when uncertain. LuthorSQL makes a single forward pass to predict slot scores over a bounded grammar for query plans, compiles and runs up to 32 candidate plans, and chooses an answer using a trained validator. On its own, LuthorSQL reaches 68.03% execution accuracy on BIRD's hidden test set at 0.84 s per question on a single GPU, maintains its accuracy in 9 GB with 8-bit quantized weights, and transfers zero-shot to Snowflake, BigQuery, MySQL, PostgreSQL, and DuckDB within 3.1 points of its native SQLite accuracy. Combined with Claude Opus 5.5 in a pre-registered confidence cascade over 227 questions on databases published after Opus's training cutoff, LuthorSQL answers nearly half of all questions locally and cuts API cost by 33.3% at the same accuracy. Finally, contamination probing shows that Opus reproduces 48-92% of public benchmark gold queries verbatim without seeing the schema; however, its tool-assisted accuracy holds on renamed schemas and reaches 95.6-97.4% on post-cutoff databases. Code and data: https://github.com/IAMIbrahimmemon/luthorsql|[2611.17780](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17780)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Towards Seconds-level Video Representation: Quadratically Gated Feature Grid with Adaptive, Prediction-free Sampling**|Tie-Yun Li et.al.|Implicit neural video representations are notoriously slow to build, which does not cross the basic practical threshold for most industrial applications. Here, we present Q-GAS, a coordinate-queryable video representation that couples multi-resolution explicit 3D feature grids with a compact SwiGLU-style quadratic-gating (QG) decoder and an error-aware, prediction-free sampler. The explicit grids avoid hash-collision coefficient sharing at the cost of modestly higher storage yet greatly boost the expressive capacity; the QG decoder introduces low-rank local second-order interactions to improve the nonlinear feature response. During fitting, a global error map periodically refreshes a high-error pool, and each batch mixes pool samples with uniform samples, which is free from the auxiliary model to predict difficult samples, thereby further escalating the efficiency. On UVG Beauty/Bosphorus/Jockey, Q-GAS fits and quantizes under 50 s on one RTX PRO 6000, decodes at over 370 FPS with mixed precision and about 146 FPS in FP32. In our PyTorch implementation, Q-GAS trains and quantizes 5.5-7.2 times faster than the evaluated Instant-NGP baseline and 19-28 times faster than RNeRV. Under a 90 s budget, it improves PSNR/MS-SSIM/LPIPS over the evaluated NeRV-family pipelines at comparable BPP over the measured overlap. We have shared our code at https://github.com/MillenRosen/Q-GAS for readers' free use.|[2611.17786](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17786)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Can Large Language Models Optimize Pretrained Neural Network Weights? A Multi-Phase Empirical Evaluation of Gradient-Free Editing in YOLO26 Object Detection**|Mohamed Abdallah Salem et.al.|Direct weight editing offers a gradient-free alternative to fine-tuning pretrained detectors, but its generalization and the value of large language model (LLM) guidance remain uncertain. We evaluate five experimental phases using YOLO26 models and MS COCO, comparing deterministic scaling, random perturbations, differential evolution, CMA-ES, structured interventions, real local-LLM proposals, failure-aware guidance, and hybrid LLM-CMA-ES against a matched random-policy control. Optimization uses native Ultralytics mAP50-95 on 1,000 COCO Val2017 images; selected candidates are assessed on a disjoint, previously examined 1,500-image subset, with COCO API AP50-95 reported separately where available. Increasing the CMA-ES budget from 8 to 250 evaluations raised development gains from +0.11 to +0.28 percentage points, while selection changes remained negative (-0.03 to -0.12 points); the 250-evaluation result uses one seed. The hybrid LLM approach showed no statistically established advantage over random-policy guidance (p = 0.612, 0.369, and 0.694 at budgets 8, 25, and 50). Five-seed YOLO26m replication yielded a COCO API AP difference of +0.004 points (95% CI: -0.026 to +0.034). Of 225 archived LLM proposals, 53 satisfy the final strict schema; early tensor-level execution cannot be fully verified. Genuine LLM-mediated weight editing is feasible, but the tested approaches did not demonstrate consistent transfer beyond the development subset or a measurable LLM advantage.|[2611.17789](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17789)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Multimodal Person Concept Model for Person Identification under Varying Observation Conditions**|Mizuho Okitani et.al.|Service robots in domestic and office environments must identify users to provide personalized services based on their preferences and previous interactions. This is challenging because only a few observations may be available for learning, while observation conditions change owing to appearance, environmental noise, and utterance content. To address this challenge, this study proposes a multimodal person concept model that learns person concepts from a small number of observations, without person labels, and enables robust identification under changing conditions. The model employs a nonparametric Bayesian generative model based on the Chinese restaurant process and integrates visual, acoustic, and linguistic features extracted using Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) and Contrastive Language-Audio Pretraining (CLAP), followed by principal component analysis (PCA). We evaluated six participants under eight conditions in which appearance changes, additive noise, and transcription errors were introduced individually and in combination. Averaged over the eight conditions, the proposed model achieved an identification accuracy of 0.89 while inferring the number of person concepts from the observations, which is comparable to k-means clustering that was supplied with the true number of participants (0.88). We also examined PCA dimensionality, modality availability, and variation strength, and demonstrated the model on a Toyota Human Support Robot. These results show that multimodal person concept learning supports robust person identification under changing conditions, and that identification can succeed even when the number of concepts is not correctly recovered. The project page and demonstration video are available at https://okitamisan01.github.io/person_concept/.|[2611.17802](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17802)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CarryOn: Learning What to Keep in Context for Long-Horizon LLM Agents**|Lihuan Li et.al.|Long-horizon LLM agents can coordinate tools and applications to complete complex, multi-step tasks. As their interaction histories grow, effective context management becomes essential for reliable and efficient execution. Existing methods reduce context through summarization, pruning, or history selection. However, shorter contexts do not necessarily support better decisions: summaries can omit exact details needed for later actions, while retaining entire interactions can carry substantial irrelevant information. We propose CarryOn, a lightweight context manager that pairs compact execution state with fine-grained, source-linked evidence. A learned selector and adaptive retention policy keep relevant evidence available without additional LLM calls for context management. We introduce two complementary evaluation metrics: $S_\alpha$ prioritizes task success while accounting for resource use, and normalized cost per success (NCS) measures the resources spent per successful task. Across AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Tau2 Telecom with three LLM Actors, CarryOn achieves 75.7% macro-averaged task success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.7 percentage points. It also achieves the best average scores on both metrics.|[2611.17808](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17808)|
|**2026-11-16**|**SceneAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Portable Game Scene Generation**|Xingyi Li et.al.|Moving a scene generator to a new game engine requires more than exporting its final output: planning, construction tools, and stored experience may also depend on the original backend. We present SceneAgent, a multi-agent framework for reusing the construction process and its experience across backends. A canonical SceneContext maintains the evolving world, while backend adapters isolate coordinate conventions, asset bindings, and export. Structured handoffs let fresh Builder sessions query this persistent state without inheriting the full construction transcript. Across generations, human-approved traces are distilled into asset-ID-free Layout skills and semantic Content skills that can be retrieved and recombined. We introduce GameSceneBench, comprising 69 indoor and outdoor prompts. Under a common asset and evaluation protocol, SceneAgent obtains human tie-adjusted preference scores of 78.2-89.4% against five baselines. Ablations show that reusable skills and the multi-agent protocol primarily improve prompt alignment and scene completeness, with additional runtime. Deployment in a second engine preserves the shared construction protocol and skill library while replacing backend integration components. Together, these results support separating persistent scene state, task-local reasoning, and reusable experience for portable scene construction.|[2611.17821](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17821)|
|**2026-11-16**|**V-JEPA-LM: Video-Language Models That Read Dynamics Beyond Static, Text-Aligned Representations**|Keuntae Kim et.al.|World-model video encoders such as V-JEPA learn how scenes change (direction, speed, order, state) from video alone. To answer questions, they are joined to a language model trained on captions and question-answer pairs. But each caption or question names only a few attributes of a clip, so the model learns to retrieve those attributes rather than to read the clip, and the dynamics the encoder carries go unused: on the same items, a linear probe reads speed from frozen V-JEPA features at 87% while the VLM answers at chance. We show where dynamics lives in a frozen encoder: in a subspace shared across videos for predictive encoders such as V-JEPA, but not as a shared component of frame-wise, text-aligned encoders such as SigLIP. We then build counterfactual supervision that makes the language model read it: pixel transformations turn public videos into answer-balanced variants whose answers are set by the transformation and differ only in the change. Trained in a single stage on 4.53M public samples with 32 visual tokens per frame, where open video LLMs use several stages, 9M to 172M samples and 64 to 256 tokens per frame on average, the resulting V-JEPA-LM models (1B to 8B, Qwen3 and Llama) lead every size class in average score across eleven video benchmarks; our 4B model scores 72.1, against 61.1 for the best open 4B and 64.8 for the best open 8B model. A controlled encoder x supervision study shows that the encoder decides what can be read and the supervision what is read: at a matched budget, counterfactual supervision adds 4.5 points on average and 38 on the speed questions of natural benchmarks. Code and models: https://github.com/kkt94/Llama-V-JEPA-LM.|[2611.17831](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17831)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Tracing Uncertainties of Extreme El Ni\~{n}o Prediction to Mesoscale Ocean Currents**|Bo Qin et.al.|The predictability of the El Ni\~{n}o-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has historically been linked to large-scale errors in models' initial conditions such as heat content and surface wind stress. Here we demonstrate that these slowly evolving errors may ultimately originate from mesoscale errors in initial oceanic states that are common in current models and ocean observation systems. This hidden source of ENSO forecast uncertainty, in terms of mesoscale ocean-current errors, is convincingly exposed by the conditional nonlinear optimal perturbation (CNOP) method applied to a differentiable ocean AI model. The CNOP-identified most influential current errors are strongly event-dependent. For the 1997/98 El Ni\~{n}o, the initial current errors in the eastern Pacific results in the largest forecast error through locally suppressing original subsurface warming. For the 2015/16 event, the dominate error originates from the western Pacific, which reorganizes upper ocean temperature structure and affects the eastern Pacific through the equatorial waveguide. Further analysis reveals that these current errors perturb mesoscale eddy count and eddy kinetic energy triggering large-scale temperature errors that hinder El Ni\~{n}o development. The timing of the maximum growth rate of the obtained CNOP error is well aligned with the spring predictability barrier of ENSO, and the error structures are reflected in simulated analysis errors caused by unresolved mesoscale currents. These findings extend the potential source of ENSO predictability beyond large-scale thermal signals, reveal for the first time an uncharted route of ENSO forecast failure, and provide an important physical basis for targeted ocean observations and data assimilation in the future.|[2611.17854](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17854)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Active Learning of Temporal Specifications through Distinguishing Queries**|Rajarshi Roy et.al.|Temporal specifications are essential for verification and monitoring, yet writing formulas that capture the intended behaviour is difficult. Active learning derives such specifications through interaction between a learner and a teacher, but remains underexplored for temporal logics. Existing approaches require the teacher to answer equivalence queries and to construct counterexamples, and they often rely on SAT-based learning procedures that restart the search after each new example. We present an active-learning framework for LTLf in which the teacher only labels traces and answers natural-language questions about the intended behaviour. The learner maintains an enumerated version space of candidate formulas, generates traces that distinguish candidates, and uses the teacher's hints to rank candidates or to restrict the search. We prove consistency with the observed examples, identification within bounded hypothesis classes with a bound on the number of queries, and a PAC guarantee for the final hypothesis. In experiments with a simulated teacher on 36 targets, including targets in four standard environments, the learner identifies every target when it compares all rivals, with fewer than seven queries beyond an initial sample of ten traces on average. Correct hints reduce the number of queries by up to 16% and the number of generated candidates by a fifth, and hints used for ranking never prevent identification, even when incorrect. Our enumeration-based version space is faster than the SAT-based approach of previous learners at every target size.|[2611.17866](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17866)|
|**2026-11-16**|**UltraBERT-JEV: Encoder models for System One Applications**|Astarag Mohapatra et.al.|We study encoder models that pick one of several options in a single forward pass, without generating text. This technical report compares recipes for fine-tuning UltraBERT-380M with one mask per option. The four recipes differ in how an option is scored and how the scores become probabilities; one follows the expert gate of DeepSeek-V3. Trained on the same data as Qwen3-0.6B with the LLM-as-Jev recipe (67.6% test accuracy), the encoder reaches 69.7% to 72.1%. Scoring an option by "yes" minus "no" instead of "yes" alone gives the larger gain, from 69.7% to 71.3% on the test set and from 57.8% to 59.6% on external benchmarks (56.1% for the decoder). Sigmoid normalisation adds 0.8 test points; a square-root softplus adds none. One request at a time on an NVIDIA A100, the encoder takes 29 ms per decision against 68 ms, but uses more peak memory (2.8 against 2.2 GiB). An auxiliary head, trained on human-marked evidence and on labels selected with a reward adapted from SelfCite, predicts in the same pass the sentences annotated as support for the answer.|[2611.17867](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17867)|
|**2026-11-16**|**DySENSE: Dynamic Spatiotemporal ENergy Synthesis and Emulation with Satellite Imagery**|Kailai Sun et.al.|Cities account for over two-thirds of the world's energy and more than 70% of global carbon dioxide emissions. City energy is essential for achieving the 2050 net-zero target and the UN's Sustainable Development Goals 7 and 11. Although existing studies have made progress, they are limited by data and methodological gaps. Existing studies are largely predictive for existing cities rather than generative for future cities; fine-grained, aligned monthly dynamic energy data are scarce; and existing GenAI models struggle to generate dynamic urban functional monthly energy. We present DySENSE, a joint multimodal GenAI framework that synthesizes static urban satellite imagery together with spatially aligned, dynamic monthly building energy consumption maps. We decouple long-term static urban morphology from short-term dynamic energy consumption. By conditioning on static urban land use, DySENSE can generate realistic, diverse and new static urban imagery with aligned energy maps. By conditioning on dynamic monthly and climate prompts, DySENSE can jointly generate dynamic, monthly energy consumption maps while keeping the urban morphology static. Experiments across four cities (New York, Washington, Norfolk, Busan) demonstrated that DySENSE can generate annotated high-fidelity synthetic energy data using less than 25% labelled data, boosting downstream prediction performance by 8% mIoU, and achieving strong energy dynamic consistency. Compared to existing methods, DySENSE significantly improved prediction performance (8.7% accuracy) and reduced prediction error (9%-18% CVRMSE, ASHRAE). DySENSE offers an effective foundation for energy-efficient cities, peak-load analysis, building performance simulation and dynamic demand response for city, energy, and building science. The data and code: https://huggingface.co/datasets/skl24/MUSE2 and https://github.com/kailaisun/DySENSE|[2611.17878](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17878)|
|**2026-11-16**|**From Local Prediction Error to Final Outputs: A Theory of Expert Sharing in Multi-Step Reasoning**|Tiantong Wang et.al.|Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models can share experts across layers or computational steps, pooling training information from different contexts. This can reduce local prediction error, but multi-step systems are ultimately evaluated by their final outputs. We study when these local gains survive composition. In specified linear regression models, we derive exact sharing and merging criteria by balancing variance savings against bias from sharing across roles with different target behavior. Under a common fixed linear propagation map, we characterize expected squared final-output risk, retaining error directions and correlations. We construct cases where sharing lowers average local error yet increases final-output error. Within this setting, we also identify when orthogonal full pooling with isotropic noise preserves every local improvement at the final output. For general routed computations, we derive conditional end-to-end error bounds that account for distribution shifts, errors in surrounding components, and changes in expert selection. Separately, we show that correct predictions and route labels alone do not establish that individual experts implement the attributed operations. Controlled experiments realize a strict local-to-final risk reversal. In low-data symbolic settings, trained Transformers exhibit statistically supported teacher-forced one-step gains without statistically resolved free-running final-output gains. Separate intervention tests distinguish successful multi-layer actions from single-expert attribution and generalization to unseen transitions. Together, these results distinguish when sharing improves local estimation, when those gains survive to the final output, and what evidence is needed to attribute reusable operations to individual experts.|[2611.17879](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17879)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Photogrammetric 3D Model-Based Animal Behavior Classification**|Olaf Hellwich et.al.|Video-based behavior estimation is often implemented end-to-end from image sequences. We advocate a two-stage alternative: reconstruct 3D body and object geometry before classifying behavior. This makes ambiguities of the ill-posed 2D-to-3D inverse problem explicit and potentially correctable. We demonstrate the strategy with simulated interacting mice and extend it to real video.|[2611.17881](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17881)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Securing Multi-Agent Systems by Design**|Xilong Wang et.al.|Multi-agent systems solve complex tasks by decomposing them into subtasks, delegating the subtasks to agents, and aggregating the agents' responses using an aggregation rule. Malicious agents can manipulate the final solution by providing malicious responses to their assigned subtasks. In this paper, we first propose response manipulation attacks (ReMA), which iteratively optimize malicious responses to maximize attack effectiveness. Specifically, we formulate untargeted and targeted attacks as optimization problems and develop an approach to approximately solve these problems by iteratively refining malicious responses based on feedback from a surrogate multi-agent system. To defend against such attacks, we further propose SecMAS, a secure aggregation rule that leverages the semantic similarities among agent responses to select the response that is most consistent with a sufficiently large subset of the other responses. We theoretically establish security guarantees for SecMAS against both untargeted and targeted attacks. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that existing aggregation rules are vulnerable to ReMA, whereas SecMAS effectively defends against all evaluated attacks, including adaptive variants of ReMA, while maintaining utility comparable to that of existing aggregation rules.|[2611.17883](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17883)|
|**2026-11-16**|**More Genes, Faster Inference: Efficient Large-Panel Spatial Transcriptomics Prediction from Histology**|Songming Zhang et.al.|Spatial transcriptomics (ST) prediction from histology aims to infer spatially resolved gene expression profiles directly from tissue images. This is a challenging problem that is currently often addressed with complicated model architectures, additional inputs, and complicated training procedures, while remaining limited to fixed, small gene panels. This raises a natural question: Is there an efficient method for large-panel ST prediction? Here, we present GeneFlow, a simpler yet effective framework for large-scale spatial gene expression prediction that unifies flow-matching generative modeling with structured multimodal fusion. Specifically, GeneFlow first encodes both histological morphology and cross-gene interactions in spatial spots through histology and gene tokenizers. The model directly predicts spatial gene expression, rather than an intermediate velocity field, while being trained with a velocity-based flow-matching objective. We show that, even without relying on heavy generative pipelines, GeneFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple ST benchmarks, with up to 56.8% gains in 1,000-gene prediction. Extensive experiments further show that GeneFlow produces biologically meaningful spot profiles while reducing computational cost by orders of magnitude, thereby improving efficiency and scalability, particularly in clinical settings. Code is available at: https://github.com/ZZhangsm/GeneFlow|[2611.17899](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17899)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Condensing Law of Security Information**|Qi Tan et.al.|Scaling laws indicate that the generalization error of a large language model (LLM) decreases monotonically with model size once data is sufficient, and the corollary that larger models outperform smaller ones is now widely accepted. We argue that this corollary fails in security: decisions there hinge on anomalous inputs, which are long-tailed (e.g., zero-day attacks) and therefore poorly served by likelihood fitting. Worse still, the capability brought about by scale (e.g., inferring the intended whole from a corrupted part) leads a LLM silently \emph{repair} the anomaly before any downstream security head ever sees it. We formalize this as a tradeoff between two quantities: the cognitive capability $K(W)=I(W;P)$ that a model holds about the world distribution, and the security-information retention $R(W)=\frac{I(Z;S)}{I(A;S)}$ that survives in its intermediate representation. We derive three information-theoretic bounds to connect them: an upper bound on restoration accuracy in terms of $K(W)$, an upper bound on $R(W)$ in terms of the restoration error, and a Fano lower bound on security-task error in terms of $R(W)$. These theorems approximately imply that usable security information is a product of how much security information survives and how well it can be extracted, indicating that optimal usable security information can be achieved at a certain model size. We state this as the \emph{condensing law of security information}: usable security information is unimodal in model size, so security generalization error is U-shaped and is minimized at a finite $N^{\star}$ rather than decreasing without bound. The experiments on various real-world LLMs validate the condensing law, indicating that there is an optimal model size to solve security issues.|[2611.17918](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17918)|
|**2026-11-16**|**MineralExp: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Experimental Design in Real-World Mineral Exploration**|Bing Wang et.al.|In mineral exploration, verifying geological hypotheses relies on designing a variety of geochemical experiments. While large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate downstream geoscientific data analysis, a crucial upstream capability remains largely unexplored: automated geochemical experimental design, i.e., deciding which experiments to conduct before committing to costly laboratory work. Evaluating this capability of LLM agents is difficult for two reasons. First, geochemical experiments exhibit high methodological diversity and subjectivity: to test the same hypothesis, different geologists may choose different minerals and instruments, so no single protocol can serve as the reference answer. Second, open-ended experimental plans resist representation as structured labels, which makes objective scoring difficult. To address these challenges, we introduce MineralExp, a benchmark that reformulates geochemical experimental design as a hypothesis-based experiment selection problem, i.e., a multi-label classification problem. We abstract concrete instrument names and procedural details into a shared taxonomy of 19 experimental types across five mineral-system processes: Source, Transport, Deposition, Modification, and Preservation. This taxonomy yields deterministic multi-label targets while accommodating diverse experimental protocols. Constructed from published ore-deposit literature and verified by domain experts, MineralExp comprises 277 cases and 1,572 reference experimental plans. Given a geological hypothesis and initial context, an LLM agent selects the relevant tasks and generates concrete experimental plans, which are scored by multi-label classification metrics and an LLM-as-a-Judge metric. The experimental results show that current agents generally achieve high recall but low precision, tending to propose more experiments than are necessary to test the hypothesis.|[2611.17931](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17931)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Can You Trust Your Finance Agent? Drift, Diagnosis and Auditable Intervention on One Shipped Financial Research Agent**|Cosimo Galeone et.al.|Can you trust a finance agent you did not build? We take the reference agent shipped with the Vals Finance Agent Benchmark v2, leave it untuned, and ask four questions a leaderboard score does not answer. Is the score stable? Seven identical runs on 18 questions pass between 105 and 123 of 149 rubric criteria, and the bill varies by up to 17%. When the agent fails, who owns the fix? We classify 20 failed trajectories, from three runs of nine held-out questions, by the layer whose owner would remedy them, under a taxonomy frozen in advance: 13 belong to the agent's instructions, 3 to its tools, 4 to the model, none to its data. Can that diagnosis be trusted? Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.|[2611.17939](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17939)|
|**2026-11-16**|**From Images to Grounded Edits: A Real-World Benchmark for Chinese Grammatical Error Correction in Handwritten Essays**|Weiru Zhang et.al.|Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (CGEC) is typically formulated as a text-to-text problem. To bridge this gap, we introduce Grounded Chinese Grammatical Error Correction (G-CGEC), an image-to-grounded-edit task requiring systems to predict both corrections and their positions on essay images. For evaluation, we adapt IoU-based metrics from object detection and propose a more tolerant alternative based on center-distance matching. Using this benchmark, we evaluate open-source and proprietary vision-language models under a direct single-pass setting, as well as settings with auxiliary components (OCR and character grounding) coordinated via pipelining or agentic harnessing. Results show that even top-tier proprietary models perform surprisingly poorly in the single-pass setting, while pipelining and agentic harnessing yield meaningful improvements. Notably, an error-type-based multi-agent workflow achieves the best performance, establishing it as the strongest baseline for future G-CGEC research. Through ablations and fine-grained analysis, we find the task remains far from solved, with linguistic error discovery and character-level grounding as the primary bottlenecks. This underscores the need for models that better integrate visual perception with linguistic reasoning and visual grounding.|[2611.17966](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17966)|
|**2026-11-16**|**RL $^{\mathrm{RT}}$ : On-Device Reinforcement Learning Runtime**|Zexin Li et.al.|On-device reinforcement learning runs a periodic actor and a learner on one embedded GPU shared with other workloads. Because the learner produces the actor's weights, existing designs stall the actor or let its policy drift. We present RL$^{\mathrm{RT}}$, an on-device runtime that makes this coupling a tunable parameter: a refresh bound limits actor releases between refreshes, and a GPU permit with slack-aware admission prioritises the actor and fills spare time with training atop any base scheduler. Under the real refresh cost on two Jetson boards, RL$^{\mathrm{RT}}$ keeps a 16.8 M-parameter policy's deadline misses below 1%, against 85 to 100% without it.|[2611.18035](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18035)|
|**2026-11-16**|**TelePhysica: Agentic Reconstruction of Physically Interactive Scenes**|Ruiqi Shu et.al.|Direct image-to-video diffusion models entangle pixel synthesis with implicit latent motion, precluding persistent 3D mechanical equilibrium, mid-rollout intervention, and counterfactual force control. Conversely, single-pass programmatic 3D reconstruction from an uncalibrated photograph leaves monocular depth and support clearance underconstrained, causing reconstructed scenes to interpenetrate or collapse under gravity. We present TelePhysica, a closed-loop framework that reconstructs an executable, multi-regime physical world from a single photograph to drive stateful 3D interaction and trajectory-grounded video synthesis. A vision-language coding agent synthesizes separable visual meshes and declarative physical models within a two-stage verification loop that pairs pixel-aligned silhouette critique with a zero-force gravitational equilibrium preflight. The verified world compiles into a unified solver coupling rigid bodies, articulated chains, co-dimensional cloth shells, and 3D tetrahedral elastodynamics, whose simulated rollouts condition pretrained video diffusion models through pixel-aligned structural streams. On a 100-scene single-image benchmark, closed-loop execution raises equilibrium-verified world recovery from $0\%$ in single-pass synthesis to $81\%$. Conditioning video diffusion on simulated trajectories increases dynamic motion-mask overlap by $2{\times}\text{--}5{\times}$ and preserves motion direction under counterfactual force reversals where direct image-to-video models fail.|[2611.18078](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18078)|
|**2026-11-16**|**AG-JEPA: Anatomically Guided Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture for Neurodegenerative Representation Learning**|Azad Singh et.al.|Brain MRI can reveal early signs of Alzheimers disease (AD) before measurable cognitive decline becomes apparent. We evaluate AG-JEPA across five downstream tasks spanning Alzheimer's disease diagnosis and progression prediction. |**2026-11-16**|**EcoVGGT: Pinned-Key Inference for Efficient and Streamable Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction from Long Videos**|Hongsen Cao et.al.|Feed-forward multi-view transformers such as VGGT recover the cameras and geometry of a video in one pass, but the cost of their cross-frame attention grows quadratically with the frame count, and their accuracy drops on long videos. Most remedies approximate attention over all frames within a budget or select a few keys by their content. We hypothesize that the keys of a long video can be chosen without looking at its content, and our experiments on VGGT and $\pi^3$ support it: a few dozen evenly spread frames can serve as the keys of every frame. We present EcoVGGT, a training-free inference framework whose keys form a Pinned Key Set of 31 such frames. A pin pass stores their tokens in a Scene Cache, and the other frames read it in independent batches that reproduce joint inference with these keys exactly, at a constant cost per frame. An Online Stream updates the pinned frames during recording. Six middle global blocks align the frames and form a reusable VGGT module: the Alignment Score reads their attention to flag failures, and Alignment Refocusing reduces them by fine-tuning only these blocks (3.6 GPU hours). |**2026-11-16**|**An In-Silico Audit of Topographic Organization in a Multimodal Brain-Response Model**|Jakub Strawa et.al.|TRIBE v2 (d'Ascoli et al., 2026) predicts fMRI responses across the cortical surface from video, audio and text. There is no tonotopic gradient in primary auditory cortex: best frequencies span an interquartile range of only 0.14 octaves across core and belt, and the null persists with loudness-equalized tones. An eccentricity-like gradient appears only when the ring geometry changes, consistent with global image statistics rather than a positional code. In contrast, motion selectivity is strong and precisely localized: 22% of the 1% most motion-selective vertices fall in MT+, which covers 0.42% of cortex. |**2026-11-16**|**Interpretable weight-space programming builds transparent transformers and brain encoding models**|Yash Mehta et.al.|Neural networks are powerful predictors, yet their opacity limits their usefulness in science, where the goal is explanation rather than mere prediction. In neuroscience, for example, deep encoding models accurately predict brain activity in response to natural stimuli, but struggle to reveal which stimulus features drive those predictions. Here we introduce interpretable weight-space programming (iWSP), a method in which an LLM agent iteratively builds interpretable models by explicitly programming their weights and features in code, without gradient-based training (backpropagation). Without backpropagation to tune parameters, the agent must draw on its prior knowledge and reason over previous trials to hypothesize, hand-craft, and refine human-interpretable computations mirroring the workflow of a human scientist. LLM audits confirm the interpretability of the hand-crafted models. We first validate iWSP by tasking it with constructing transformers for canonical benchmarks such as arithmetic and sentiment classification, finding that LLM agents can successfully craft compact, accurate networks directly. Next, we apply iWSP to build neural encoding models across vision, language, and audition. In the visual cortex, iWSP outpredicts existing interpretable baselines, uncovers distinct computations across early/higher visual areas, and generalizes to unseen data. |**2026-11-16**|**PUMA-Former: Uncertainty-Aware Agentic Computation Offloading in MEO-LEO Satellite Networks With Incomplete Information**|Yi Jing et.al.|Satellite edge computing distributes mobile workloads across moving processors whose service capacity must be coordinated over time. We propose PUMA-Former, an uncertainty-aware agentic framework for cooperative offloading under incomplete information that balances completed work, service delay, and execution cost. A MEO coordinator maintains distinct-report memory and closes the loop between available evidence, capacity estimates, allocation commands, and execution feedback. Latent-delay attention weights plausible measurement-age hypotheses, and historical innovations refine CPU estimates around the latest received report. Queue-error-aware fusion incorporates auxiliary pressure while accounting for backlog uncertainty. The inferred resource moments condition a structured Transformer allocator for joint destination, partition, bandwidth, CPU, and power decisions. Bounded risk-aware calibration then adjusts workload splits to reflect uncertainty and shared destination load. |**2026-11-16**|**Who Should Be on the Panel? Task-Conditioned Persona Selection for LLM Trading Agents**|Boming Yang et.al.|Multi-agent LLM systems are built by fixing a cast of roles and then designing how the cast coordinates. Because nothing is returned to the traders, the panel stays the panel that was selected, and it can trade a new stock from an empty record. On eight US stocks over the out-of-knowledge year 2025, against three prior LLM trading agents re-implemented in the same framework and against rule-based, predictive and reinforcement-learning baselines, the selected panel is ahead of a fixed panel of the same size and of the prior agents run as their papers prescribe, with a shallower drawdown than buy-and-hold on every stock; it keeps its performance with no warm-up and with no record at all, whereas the memory-augmented agents' returns swing with their warm-up; and neither more seats nor the manager's reading of the arguments substitutes for selecting the right ones. Who is in the group is a design decision to be made per task, before how the group coordinates.|[2611.18150](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18150)|
|**2026-11-16**|**T2ST: Data-Efficient Spatio-Temporal Foundation Models via Temporal Data and Knowledge Transfer**|Yue Xu et.al.|Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Our key insight is that temporal pattern learning can benefit from abundant external time series, while spatial dependency learning remains grounded in real spatio-temporal observations. Guided by this insight, T2ST retrieves matched sequences to reinforce sparsely observed temporal patterns and selects reliable complementary sequences to broaden temporal coverage. Temporal relation distillation aligns relationships among historical patches, while multi-scale prediction distillation transfers forecasting behavior at fine and coarse temporal resolutions, enabling knowledge transfer across heterogeneous architectures. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. |**2026-11-16**|**From Pen-and-Paper to Machine-Checked Proofs: Lean for System Identification**|Manas Mejari et.al.|Mathematical proofs in scientific papers are often long and intricate, relying on chains of references to earlier results. This paper serves as an introduction to the Lean 4 proof assistant for a system identification and control audience, assuming no prior exposure to formal methods. We first show how a Lean proof is read using two elementary examples: the first on identifiability, the second on set invariance. We then illustrate how Lean can be used to analyse results from the literature, taking as example a classic lemma on the inversion of the noise filter in prediction-error identification (Ljung, 1999, Lemma 3.1). Since in Lean every step must be justified, the formalization brings to light a subtle point: the original claim of that lemma holds almost surely, although not for every realisation, as its statement may suggest. A simple counterexample makes this concrete. Finally, the paper discusses guidelines and perspectives on the use of Lean in system identification research.|[2611.18220](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18220)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Personalization of Basic and Moral Values Guiding LLM-Controlled Assistant Robots**|Mateusz Wozniak et.al.|Personal assistant robots powered by large language models (LLMs) are likely to enter everyday life within the next decade, but the values that should guide their behaviour remain largely unspecified. LLMs prompted to act as robots shifted only weakly toward the desired profile, indicating that default model behaviour and prompt engineering are no substitute for explicit value personalization.|[2611.18230](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18230)|
|**2026-11-16**|**RACE: Ranking-Uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation**|Tianqi Liu et.al.|Efficient sequential recommendation has emerged as a promising paradigm for providing real-time personalized service. To deploy models under resource-constrained environment, model compression becomes a necessity, yet existing methods typically apply uniform compression ratios, overlooking the heterogeneity in user intent complexity. To address this, we propose RACE (Ranking-uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation), a framework that dynamically adapts both model structures and input sequences with uncertainty-aware compression ratios. To ensure capacity-adaptive inference, RACE captures the model's intrinsic uncertainty of its predictions and transforms it into a proactive resource allocator. Specifically, we derive a list-wise ranking-uncertainty metric from the distributional stability of top-$K$ predictions, which serves as a measurable proxy for instance complexity. |**2026-11-16**|**ParetoM $^3$ : Learning on the Pareto Set under Preference Guidance via Min-Max-Min Optimization**|Pei Tang et.al.|Learning across multiple inherently conflicting objectives requires selecting desirable trade-offs from the Pareto set. Under a local Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz assumption, we prove that ParetoM$^3$ finds an $\epsilon$-stationary point of the M$^3$ problem that is also approximately weakly Pareto optimal for the original multi-objective problem when the penalty parameter is sufficiently large, with explicit non-asymptotic convergence rates. We also show that this stationarity notion recovers existing optimality criteria, including approximate preference stationarity and approximate Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, under the same assumptions used in prior works. |**2026-11-16**|**From Bytes to Behavior: Request-Level Attribution from Live GPU Hidden States**|Jonathan Pan et.al.|Existing memory forensics for machine learning systems typically targets static artifacts, such as model weights or prompt logs, rather than the transient computational states of a live inference process. This gap limits an examiner's ability to analyze the specific behavior of a running large language model (LLM) service that cannot be restarted or modified. We present a method for acquiring per-layer residual-stream activations from a live GPU process using a non-modifying debugger, without requiring prior knowledge of the model's architecture parameters or weights. As a use case, we apply linear probes trained on standard framework activations to the recovered states, successfully distinguishing refusal from compliance with an AUROC of 0.88 on held-out prompts. This work establishes that live, observational access to intermediate hidden states is feasible on named inference platforms, providing a new source of evidence for examining the operational behavior of deployed AI systems.|[2611.18272](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18272)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Right Token, Right Time: From Per-Token Gating to Trajectory-Aware Activation Steering**|Nima Eshraghi et.al.|Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning. |**2026-11-16**|**PrivacyPR: A Dataset of Real-World Privacy Fixes for Benchmarking Privacy-Aware Code Review**|Hua Yang et.al.|Coding agents increasingly review code, yet whether they catch the privacy defects that laws such as the GDPR require software to avoid remains largely untested, partly because few datasets connect real privacy defects to their accepted fixes and review context. Its evaluation harness reverts each fix to reintroduce the defect. On Small, two coding agents detect only 42-51% of defects under a generic review prompt, but 88-91% when explicitly asked to check privacy, while flagging 11-15% of non-fix PRs. These results show that privacy defects can be detected by coding agents, but that detection depends strongly on review intent and comes with false alarms. Data, code, reviews, judge verdicts, and an extensible evaluation harness are publicly available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23744765.|[2611.18293](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18293)|
|**2026-11-16**|**AI for Parameter Selection in Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization**|Doriela Grabocka et.al.|Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) methods provide a way to compute molecular ground-state energies, but their accuracy and runtime depend sensitively on configuration choices such as the subspace dimension, propagation timestep, and the allocation of a fixed sampling budget across circuits, for which no formal selection method exists. In this work, we explore whether oracle models can predict the quantum runtime and approximation error from the Hamiltonian and the candidate configuration. Using synthetic Hamiltonians with controllable spectral properties, we show that machine-learned models can map a Hamiltonian directly to the best-ranked configuration within a predefined grid for the SKQD algorithm, validated against classical Krylov propagation, achieving strong rank correlations between true and predicted error and runtime, Spearman $\rho = 0.969$ and $\rho = 0.905$, respectively. Extending the approach to 50 molecular Hamiltonians (20-50 qubits), with reference ground-state energies from classical calculations, we train regressors that predict the simulation error and runtime for a given molecule--configuration pair ahead of execution, and classifiers to distinguish, from 600 SqDRIFT quantum-hardware runs, whether a configuration achieves a ground-state energy within chemical accuracy ($1.6\times10^{-3}$ Ha $\approx 1$ kcal/mol). For the molecular Hamiltonians, the models additionally provide an a priori feasibility and resource estimate, predicting whether a molecule can reach chemical accuracy on current hardware and at what runtime, thus correctly classifying held-out molecules at the molecule level, achieving balanced accuracy 61.8% in-distribution and 100% for five larger held-out molecules.|[2611.18307](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18307)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems**|Grid Robotics et.al.|The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.|[2611.18359](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment**|Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI) et.al.|Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($\pi_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.|[2611.18372](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18372)|
|**2026-11-16**|**AI-Based Multi-View Echocardiographic Image Synthesis using Dynamic Shape Control**|Jo\~ao Freitas et.al.|Although automated methods have improved echocardiographic workflows, progress remains constrained by the need for large, well-annotated datasets. As the development of clinical databases is often limited by ethical and logistical challenges, synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising alternative. However, existing approaches commonly rely on left ventricle shape-based supervision or are tailored to specific cardiac views, limiting the generation of additional standard and unconventional imaging planes and the preservation of multi-view consistency across cardiac chambers. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework for shape-controlled synthesis of multi-view echocardiographic sequences. First, multi-view cross-sections are extracted from dynamic whole-heart meshes. Ultrasound-like appearance is then introduced through a two-stage AI-based pipeline. In Stage 1, an unpaired GAN-diffusion model translates these cross-sections into realistic echocardiographic frames. As this translation is performed independently at the frame level, Stage 2 employs masked video reconstruction to improve temporal coherence and cardiac motion. Evaluation demonstrated high frame-level realism for both stages, while the integration of Stage 2 substantially improved video-level motion quality and temporal consistency. Overall, the proposed framework enables the generation of realistic multi-view echocardiographic sequences from diverse cardiac models, providing a scalable source of annotated data for dataset augmentation and the development of multi-view cardiac assessment methods.|[2611.18375](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18375)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent**|Run Luo et.al.|We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.|[2611.18397](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18397)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning**|Tao Hu et.al.|Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.|[2611.18417](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18417)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Between Obligations and Evidence: A Participatory Study of Translating the EU AI Act into Technical Requirements**|Helma Torkamaan et.al.|The EU AI Act imposes technical and organizational requirements for high-risk AI systems, but applying these requirements involves contextual choices about what to measure and what evidence supports a compliance claim. We investigate these challenges through a participatory workshop involving 32 researchers, policymakers, and practitioners from different disciplines, using realistic scenarios in healthcare, recruitment, and public transport to examine how regulatory obligations are interpreted, translated into technical requirements, and evaluated in practice. Through a thematic analysis of workshop data, we identify recurring tensions between how regulatory obligations are translated into measurable system properties, how responsibility and human oversight are exercised in practice, and how the validity of compliance evidence is affected by changing operational conditions and system uses. For example, participants highlighted how fairness metrics could conceal discrimination in recruitment, how time pressure and limited information could undermine human oversight in public transport, and how changes in patient populations could invalidate previously established evidence of system performance. Our findings suggest that technical, organizational, and evidentiary challenges are interdependent, and that establishing and maintaining compliance requires attention not only to technical assessments but also to how AI systems are used, governed, and monitored over time. We discuss implications for supporting context-sensitive and ongoing compliance.|[2611.18439](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18439)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles**|Wang Ma et.al.|Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.|[2611.18453](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18453)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation**|Disha Sheshanarayana et.al.|LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability.
