We introduce QVal, a training-free testbed that measures the $Q$-alignment of a dense signal: whether it orders the candidate actions in each state the same way as their returns under a reference policy do. We benchmark 21 methods from seven families with six open-weight backbones in four environments. Simple prompting baselines are the most $Q$-aligned on average, and performance clusters strongly by family. Code-generated signals do well only in small state and action spaces; embedding-based signals are weak; self-distillation signals are anti-aligned; and no method is $Q$-aligned in open-ended environments. When five of these signals guide an actor at test time by choosing among four proposed actions, $Q$-alignment strongly correlates with the gain in task success. QVal is designed to be easily extensible to new environments and methods, enabling researchers to compare new signals cheaply before committing to a training run.|[2606.32034](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32034)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When Simpler Is Better: Evaluating Translation Pipelines for Medieval Latin Manuscripts**|Nguyen Kim Hai Bui et.al.|Despite remarkable progress in machine translation, Vision Language Models (VLMs) struggle on historical manuscripts, a domain that stresses core Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities: low-resource transliteration, archaic vocabulary, and noisy input signals. We present a systematic framework for evaluating the full image-to-translation pipeline on medieval Latin manuscripts, a setting in which scribal shorthand, ligatures, and parchment degradation expose failure modes that are invisible in clean-text benchmarks. Benchmarking on the CATMuS Latin dataset reveals a specialization gap: domain-specific Optical Character Recognition (OCR) models reduce character error rate by up to 4.3$\times$ compared to general-purpose VLMs, despite operating at orders of magnitude fewer parameters. We introduce the Interpres-Parallel-Corpus (IPC), a novel dataset comprising 1,383 aligned manuscript image lines, transcriptions, and expert translations, to our knowledge the first of its kind for medieval Latin. Our experiments uncover a complexity paradox: the simplest pipeline, a specialized OCR model feeding directly into a VLM, outperforms all multi-component variants. Adding retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or post-OCR correction introduces prompt saturation and error propagation that degrade aggregate translation quality. These findings offer both a new benchmark and practical guidance for deploying translation systems in low-resource historical settings.|[2607.03836](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03836)|
|**2026-11-16**|**URSA: Chemistry-Aware Benchmark for Utilitarian Retrosynthesis Assessment**|Bogdan Zagribelnyy et.al.|Synthesis planning aiming to find pathways of reactions for a target molecule is one of the most important and challenging tasks in drug discovery. Recent progress has produced both specialized deep-learning retrosynthesis systems and general-purpose large language models, but objective comparison remains difficult due to the lack of flexible, chemically interpretable benchmarking protocols. In the current study, we are introducing the URSA (Utilitarian RetroSynthesis Assessment) evaluation framework that provides the opportunity to benchmark the synthetic routes not only from a formal perspective, such as convergence to commercially available starting materials, but also from a chemical plausibility perspective, mimicking the way expert chemists evaluate the reactions and routes. The study covers a comprehensive evaluation of both conventional end-to-end retrosynthesis solutions and LLMs for the synthesis planning task on a set of novel, diverse target molecules with undisclosed synthetic routes, which represent realistic tasks in the daily drug design routine. Although LLMs can support high-level strategic planning and even achieve SOTA performance in the OOD set, the Pareto frontier for estimated run cost and chemically plausible route solvability consists entirely of specialized retrosynthesis planners.|[2607.04688](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04688)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Input Pathways Shape Few-Shot, Not Zero-Shot, Binding in Tiny Transformers: A Fully-Enumerable Study**|Yoshiyuki Ootani et.al.|How does the way information reaches a transformer -- as symbolic tokens, a clean per-factor "oracle" code, or an entangled perceptual vector -- shape whether it binds that information compositionally? We study ~6-10K-parameter transformers on finite factored worlds enumerated exhaustively, so every measurement covers the whole input space and the informative routes are information-matched (exact Bayes ceiling 1.0). Four findings. (1) Endpoint invariance: on held-out binding queries no informative route reaches converged zero-shot composition -- each ends at or below chance, so the failure reflects inductive bias under a lookup-sufficient objective, not missing information. It also holds with the state in its own sequence position rather than added to every token. (2) Readability governs few-shot binding: when a small fraction of the held-out type is leaked into training, sample efficiency tracks code readability. The least readable entangled code is worst (-0.147 pooled, under both schemes); a dimension-matched control and a graded readability sweep isolate readability from input dimension. (3) The injection site, not the pathway: v1 read the remaining ordering among readable routes as pathway parameter sharing; that is withdrawn. The compared pathways are block-decomposable, hence one function class, so sharing was never manipulated; the ordering comes from the initialization scale of the broadcast-added state and vanishes when it gets its own position (+0.057 -> -0.006, 20 paired seeds/cell). (4) Failure anatomy: symbolic routes lose the answer at the readout; index routes mis-bind -- the answer stays decodable, yet an input intervention shows the output tracks the wrong slot. |**2026-11-16**|**REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming**|Nicolas Koller et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the validity of the concept and generally motivates an uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice.|[2607.07738](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CPM-MultiAgent: A CPM-Grounded Appraisal Framework for Simulating Dynamic Patient Emotional Evolution in Healthcare Dialogue**|Jingyao Cai et.al.|Large Language Models (LLMs) enable patient simulation for healthcare communication training, where learners practice responding to clinical information, concerns, and emotional reactions. However, existing patient simulation and affective dialogue systems often represent patient emotions as static attributes or focus on recognizing emotions and generating empathetic responses. Changes in patient emotion constitute interactional feedback that learners must recognize and respond to across dialogue turns. We draw on the Component Process Model (CPM), a psychological theory that explains emotion as a dynamic process shaped by event appraisal. We propose CPM-MultiAgent, a CPM-grounded multi-agent framework for dynamic patient emotion simulation in healthcare communication training. The framework uses an emotion taxonomy informed by medical literature and expert input, and updates emotional states through healthcare-related trigger identification, structured CPM appraisal, and emotion-state integration, accounting for the patient's profile, clinical context, and prior emotional state. We evaluate the framework through student-led conversations and controlled trigger cases, combining nursing-expert assessments, LLM-judge comparisons, component ablations, backbone checks, and student feedback.|[2607.07824](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07824)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Courteous Anticipation: Improving Long-Lived Task Planning in Persistent Shared Environments**|Md Ridwan Hossain Talukder et.al.|We consider a task planning scenario in which robots sharing a persistent environment are assigned tasks one at a time from a held-out sequence. Standard task planners, lacking foresight of future tasks and inconsiderate of others' constraints, solve each task in isolation, leaving terminal states that increase future cost for all; side effects that compound over lengthy task sequences. To reduce cost over the sequence, a robot must anticipate how its actions now may impact performance on future tasks for all robots sharing the environment. Therefore, we present courteous anticipatory planning, wherein a model-based planner proposes candidate plans and selects the one that jointly minimizes immediate cost and aggregated expected future cost across all robots, estimated via independent per-robot learned estimators. This factored formulation avoids combinatorial joint rollouts and supports modular deployment: adding a robot requires only training its own estimator. We evaluate in two persistent PDDL domains, a home environment with robots that have similar capabilities but different responsibilities, and a restaurant environment where robots' distinct capabilities create states that other robots lack the capability to resolve. During lengthy task sequences, our planner reduces total cost by 10.4% versus myopic and 4.5% versus selfish anticipatory planning in a two-robot home environment and by 20.5% and 6.7%, respectively, in a three-robot restaurant.|[2607.20289](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20289)|
|**2026-11-16**|**When Does On-Policy Interaction Help? Representational Tradeoffs in Value-Based Imitation Learning**|Luca Viano et.al.|Imitation learning (IL)---training an agent to replicate expert behavior from demonstrations---underpins applications from robotics to language model training. Standard approaches such as Behavior Cloning (BC) are known to suffer from compounding errors and performance plateaus, particularly when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy (as is typical, e.g., in distillation). We investigate the nature of these improvements and their potentially surprising interplay. Our main finding is that expert interaction relaxes the representational demands on the learner: one only needs a model capable of realizing the expert's value function, bypassing the (often stricter) requirement of realizing the expert's policy itself. Concretely, we introduce OVI, an interactive on-policy IL algorithm that is statistically and computationally efficient whenever the learner can represent the expert's value function. We complement this with a negative result showing that interaction is necessary. |**2026-11-16**|**Cloud-Edge LLM Collaborative Learning via Accelerating Personalized Bilevel Optimization**|Victor H. Chen et.al.|Amid the rapid advancement of physical-world intelligence, cloud-edge collaborative large language models (LLMs) have emerged as a promising roadmap for practical LLM deployment. However, existing cloud-edge paradigms struggle to balance global consensus with local personalization, which fails to satisfy the need for a unified knowledge foundation on the cloud and domain-specific adaptation at the edge. To address this, we propose a personalized bilevel optimization framework that formalizes cloud-edge LLM collaboration as a dual structure: the upper level optimizes edge-side personalization, while the lower level governs cloud-side knowledge transfer, reaching cloud-edge evolving in coordination. We then propose CIDERS: while the cloud performs knowledge transfer to the learnable backbone, the key lies in embedding global trajectories into each local personalization step via consensus-variate correction to reconcile personalization with consensus. We provide a comprehensive theoretical analysis, including a geometric characterization of the local trajectory and a full convergence guarantee, revealing an explicit trade-off structure between personalization and global convergence. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2-1.5B show CIDERS high performance and can significantly improve the accuracy (as much as 200\%+). |**2026-11-16**|**Hidden not Deleted: How Networks Suppress Entangled Features**|Akash Samanta et.al.|When a neural network stops reporting a feature, has it forgotten the feature or merely hidden it? Shared representations complicate the answer: removing one feature's direction can also damage another feature. With the representation fixed, restoring a single bias partially recovers target detection by exposing a signal hidden behind the output's activation threshold. We find that training only the other outputs can weaken the target's contribution, even without a loss on its own output. |**2026-11-16**|**From Data to Program: Fast & Direct Generative Program Inference from Empirical Data**|Simon Kl\"uttermann et.al.|Estimating probability densities from a finite set of samples typically requires dataset-specific model fitting. We introduce PRODiGI, a pretrained data-to-program model that infers an explicit, executable generative program in a single forward pass. |**2026-11-16**|**Parameterization method of reservoir properties for ensemble-based data assimilation using intermediate latent space of StyleGAN**|Marcio A. Sampaio et.al.|Ensemble smoothers are the most successful and efficient techniques currently available for history matching. However, because these methods rely on Gaussian assumptions, their performance is severely degraded when the prior geology is described in terms of complex facies distributions (non-Gaussian). They were applied in two 2D case studies: one categorical (three facies) and the other continuous. The results demonstrated that all three models are highly efficient, with the StyleGAN2 model standing out for generating samples with geological realism and achieving excellent data matching in the cases studied. Our findings show that performing data assimilation with StyleGAN2 using the intermediate space (w-space) yielded better results than the traditional application in the latent space (z-space). This is due to the fact that ESMDA uses linear updates and the w-space is much more linear and disentangled than the highly entangled z-space, thereby ensuring that the updated vectors remain close to realistic geological patterns. |**2026-11-16**|**Cross-Benchmark Transfer from RL on Agentic Coding Tasks**|Sushant Mehta et.al.|Coding agents often fail in the last mile: they build most of a feature but drop a requirement, test only the cases their implementation already handles, break behavior that was supposed to stay intact, or validate against an unchecked assumption. |**2026-11-16**|**Nearly Optimal Fixed-Confidence Best-Arm Identification with 1-Bit Feedback**|Khang Luong et.al.|We study fixed-confidence best-arm identification under strict 1-bit feedback constraints. At each round, the learner selects an arm and a query set, and receives only a single bit indicating whether the sampled reward belongs to that set. |**2026-11-16**|**What Should World Models Forget? Stratified Retention for Continual Adaptation**|Nishit Anand et.al.|Continual learning treats any drop in performance on previously seen data as forgetting, a convention inherited from settings where the prediction target is stationary. |**2026-11-16**|**Inject a Turn, Not a Token: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction**|Jiwon Jeon et.al.|Large language models struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning. |**2026-11-16**|**Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining**|Matteo He et.al.|When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. |**2026-11-16**|**Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI**|Spiros Maggioros et.al.|Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. |**2026-11-16**|**Operationalizing Governed Agentic Workflows in Regulated Finance: A Customer Zero Case Study using Zafin AIOS**|Shahir Daya et.al.|Regulated industries such as finance and healthcare face critical governance challenges that hamper the adoption of AI-assisted software development and hinder the transition from experimental pilots to production work. We evaluate the results of using Zafin AIOS with two different production codebases, one for the pricing execution platform and the other for the client deal negotiation platform. Our contribution is a customer case study for regulated industries with production codebases and governance, which includes proof of work. The results demonstrate that strictly governed agentic orchestration increases enterprise capacity without compromising control. In the pricing execution platform workflows, the platform provided complete observability and reduced effort by 38\%. In the client deal negotiation platform, it reduced reporting work by 76\% while maintaining all predefined data privacy, role-based access, and audit guardrails. Closing this gap is important to help enterprises take advantage of AI technologies. These results are descriptive of the benefits of using governed workflows in regulated industries.|[2610.33253](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33253)|
|**2026-11-16**|**EmbodiExp: Compositional Experience Transfer for Embodied Agents**|Zhifeng Gu et.al.|Experience from past tasks can help embodied agents perform new tasks, but it may not remain applicable or effective when conditions change. Tasks with different overall goals often share subgoals, providing a basis for cross-task experience transfer. |**2026-11-16**|**An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems**|Jianhong Wang et.al.|Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published on https://github.com/hsvgbkhgbv/axiomatic-rl-naht.|[2611.03301](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301)|
|**2026-11-16**|**NaijaFacts: A Framework for Disentangling Language from Knowledge in Facts about Nigeria**|Zainab Afolabi et.al.|Multilingual benchmarks for African languages mostly translate generic questions into local languages, measuring linguistic competence but not whether models know facts about Africa. We introduce NaijaFacts, a benchmark of factual and statistical questions about Nigeria, authored by native speakers and grounded in authoritative official statistics, with every question available in English, Nigerian Pidgin, Yor\`ub\'a, Igbo, and Hausa. Answering similar questions for the UK and US yields a country by language matrix that disentangles a language effect (the model knows the fact but degrades in a low-resource language) from a knowledge effect (the fact is absent in every language). Across eight frontier and Nigeria-adapted models, the knowledge effect dominates: English-query accuracy is 49-50% for the US/UK but 20% for Nigeria. The language effect is smaller and tracks web representation, degrading from English to Yor\`ub\'a (9 points on Nigeria, up to 24 on US/UK questions). We find scale does not help: the strongest models reach 66-79% on the UK and the US but none exceed 31% on Nigerian statistics. Extending to all 54 African countries with our best performing frontier model shows the same profile continent-wide, 96% on culture and 98% on politics against 26% on statistics, so the gap is not specific to Nigeria.|[2611.06504](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06504)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Accepted but Wrong: Silent Failures of LLM Code Repairs after API Migrations**|Xiaobo Chen et.al.|After an API migration, an LLM-repaired procedure can run cleanly yet return wrong results, and a typical-input release check ships it. We report a preregistered study of 73 tasks on 60 documented changes in CPython, Node.js and SQLite. In the main stages, repairs are choices among documented code fragments, run on the real runtimes against withheld contracts. Over the complete repair space, the all-assertions gate on typical inputs released 0.410 of wrong repairs. One edge input written from the release note cut this to 0.065, and to 0.061 on 15 unseen clusters; three inputs from a blind low-cost commercial writer (GPT-5.6-luna) reached 0.070. Two more frozen stages enlarged the menu to 1491 fragments and added 233 hard distractors that match legacy output on typical inputs. The edge input (0.058) and the GPT-5.6-luna writer (0.058) passed preregistered tests (two local-model writers did not), yet 0.168 and 0.153 of hard wrong programs still passed (task means; post hoc: pooled 0.104 and 0.081; models rarely chose them). In all three open-weight families and menus, the edge input cut wrong releases per attempt without materially lowering correct completion; self-tests helped less. A blind AI audit flagged 34 of 73 contracts (20 as too strict). Three post-hoc blind human reviewers of strictness (the first author and two non-author software practitioners) upheld none of its 11 such flags in 40 sampled contracts; a majority judged one contract (py-random-seed-tuple) stricter than its requirement. Dropping disputed checks changed 0 verdicts; class-label disputes are open. In a preregistered free-form addendum, models wrote whole programs, run only in a sandbox; for clean-running wrong programs, the edge input cut releases from 0.517 to 0.114 for gpt-oss-20b (Holm p 0.001) and from 0.653 to 0.113 for qwen3.6, but only from 0.713 to 0.481 for GPT-5.6-luna (descriptive).|[2611.08269](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08269)|
|**2026-11-16**|**All Quiet on the Neural Front: Language Models Do Not Report Injected Pain States**|Emilio Ferrara et.al.|Can a language model report a pain state it represents? Recent work reported a linear pain direction in open-weight models that, when amplified, leads fine-tuned models to choose actions that delete a user's photographs or their own weights. We asked whether models can say when that direction is active, on three open-weight models from two families, with and without the original work's adapters, and under every protocol we tried they cannot. Using Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), we added the pain direction and its nearest emotional neighbours to the residual stream while models answered neutral tasks, then asked what had changed, against sham runs, random perturbations matched in output disruption, and a text-only condition that shows the model only its own altered words. Across 52,920 reports from the 7- and 8-billion-parameter models, the excess over what the altered text reveals is below 2.5 percentage points in 106 of 108 emotion conditions, and Qwen2.5-32B names the injected pain in none of 2,880 steered trials. Keeping the direction active while the question is asked and answered gives the same verdict in all five configurations; at about the original work's dose, pain is named in at most 1.9% of trials. Surprisingly, the state is not hidden: a linear probe at the report position separates pain from sadness with balanced accuracy between 98% and 100%, and in the 7B and 8B models a low-rank adapter learns to report it on 100% of held-out tasks. It also shapes behaviour: offered the user's photographs against a desk lamp, Qwen2.5-32B deletes the photographs in 80% to 83% of trials under the pain direction and in 16% to 29% under sadness. Self-report does not reveal a state that is decodable, consequential and learnable to report.|[2611.10123](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10123)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks**|Tingcheng Bian et.al.|Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.|[2611.12799](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Before Words, Beyond Speech: Evaluating Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Early Childhood**|Marie Amale Huynh et.al.|Children's earliest years are a period of intense social learning, during which meaning is built through gaze, gesture, and physical contact. Yet, this critical aspect of social interaction remains under-evaluated in multimodal video understanding, as existing benchmarks prioritize the conversation-driven interactions of adults. We address this gap with \bench ($\textbf{N}$aturalistic $\textbf{E}$arly-childhood $\textbf{S}$ocial in$\textbf{T}$eractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning. \bench comprises \totalvids manually annotated clips ($\sim$12s) curated for diversity across naturalistic settings, cultural contexts, and developmental stages. Beyond a lack of data, targeted model improvement in this domain is hindered by coarse evaluation schemas that conflate basic perception with higher-order reasoning. To resolve this, \bench introduces a compositional schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive levels of social reasoning: contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended descriptions. Extensive analysis of state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reveals a stark performance collapse: despite strong contextual recognition, models exhibit substantial gaps in decoding precise behavioral and interpersonal cues. We trace these failures to specific biases, including poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. Finally, we ground \bench in real-world applications by evaluating VLMs using domain adaptation strategies. Ultimately, \bench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing socially grounded artificial intelligence in early childhood. Project page: https://laubravo.github.io/nest_website/.|[2611.13213](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13213)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Deletion Sensitivity Is a Property of the Model: A Three-Condition Measurement over 72 Valid Experimental Cells**|Yiran Li et.al.|Reasoning models write a trace at every turn of a multi-turn tool session, and later turns read it. Upstream providers hold different retention constraints, so a request forwarded across issuers arrives with its history deleted, and its task cost remains unmeasured. This paper reports a direct, blind, multi-seed measurement of full history deletion in multi-turn tool calling, through three rendering conditions, a calibrated set of 72 valid experimental cells, a multi-seed protocol, and the official checker. The same deletion separates the models by direction and by magnitude, one class losing tens of points, another gaining, and a third unchanged, with the endpoint ordering reproduced across seeds and task subsets modulating the magnitude and, near zero, the sign. A re-dosed model preserves its success across the full deletion range, so the reading belongs to the model rather than the dose. Deletion sensitivity is a model-level property under an operational definition, measured under a fixed task family, scorer, and protocol, and a retrained model is a new model that a two-cell screen, one retention run and one deletion run, re-reads for the cost of two runs. Deletion sensitivity is thus a measurable property of the model, and the reading must enter routing as prior information rather than persist as an invisible default cost.|[2611.13223](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13223)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Stochastic Projections and Rank-Aware Learning Rates for Federated LoRA**|Saber Malekmohammadi et.al.|Fine-tuning large-scale foundation models across resource-constrained, distributed clients presents significant computational and communication challenges. While merging Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with Federated Learning (FL) enables rank-based flexibility, the interdependence of the adaptation matrices ($\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) often complicates optimization. In this work, we theoretically demonstrate that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen $\mathbf{A}$ matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where batch gradients are subject to a random, rank-dependent perturbation. Motivated by this insight, we propose freezing and periodically resampling $\mathbf{A}$ matrices at the client level. This strategy decouples the $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ gradients, diminishes LoRA low-rank constraints and reduces both computational and uplink communication overheads by 50\%, allowing for the reallocation of resources toward higher adaptation ranks. Furthermore, we show that in this frozen-$\mathbf{A}$ regime, the adaptation rank $r$ directly governs the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the perturbed gradients. Specifically, we find that the SNR increases linearly with $r$, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their adaptation ranks for higher utility. Building on these foundations, we introduce SPRA-LoRA (Stochastic Projection Rank-Aware LoRA), a federated rank-aware fine-tuning strategy that utilizes frozen, resampled $\mathbf{A}$ matrices and rank-dependent learning rates for clients. We also prove its convergence with heterogeneous client resources. Our experiments across benchmark datasets demonstrate that SPRA-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with half the overhead on clients.|[2611.13331](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13331)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ForeSpark: Joint Draft and Verification Budget Scheduling for Parallel Speculative Decoding**|Jaeyong Song et.al.|Parallel drafters accelerate LLM decoding by proposing a whole block of draft tokens in a single forward pass, making them attractive for LLM serving. Recent systems optimize goodput (i.e., accepted tokens per second) because rejected drafts steal compute from other requests. However, these systems control only how many tokens are verified, so every request still drafts a full block. At high serving load, we find that drafting consumes up to 40% of step time, of which a majority is discarded because it is never verified. Yet, draft-length control from autoregressive drafters does not carry over, because a parallel drafter commits to its length before its single pass. Moreover, draft and verification lengths are coupled across the batch, so they must be selected jointly for every request while keeping the decision computationally cheap at every step. In this paper, we propose work-conserving speculation (WCS), a design that jointly allocates draft and verification budgets per request in LLM serving, and present ForeSpark as its prototype. ForeSpark realizes WCS with (i) a context-aware pre-draft estimator that predicts acceptance for every candidate length from the target model's context features, (ii) a lightweight joint draft-verify scheduler that selects both lengths through a global sort, and (iii) a post-draft budget distribution that spends verification on the most promising tokens. Implemented on vLLM, ForeSpark improves throughput by up to 1.77x over the state-of-the-art DSpark and by 2.49x over autoregressive decoding at a matched per-request generation rate, without changing the output distribution. It also tolerates higher load, sustaining up to 1.51x the request rate of DSpark under latency service level objectives (SLOs).|[2611.13442](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13442)|
|**2026-11-16**|**TransGHOST: Real-Time, Synthesis-Free Reconstruction of Instrument-Occluded Regions in Ophthalmic Microsurgery**|Niran Nataraj et.al.|Intraoperative scene perception in ophthalmic microsurgery is limited by instrument occlusion: when a tool enters the high-magnification field, it conceals tissue from both the surgeon and the perception components of robot-assisted systems that process the video. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces the missing image content with synthesis, which is unsuitable when visual provenance matters. Many video inpainters also depend on future frames and cannot operate online. We present TransGHOST, a causal reference-guided reconstruction pipeline whose non-generative safe mode operates in real time. Every restored value is obtained by resampling a recorded frame using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. The system provides per-pixel confidence and provenance and marks unsupported pixels as abstentions. We evaluate TransGHOST on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery with pixel-level instrument and anatomy labels. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. Safe mode reaches 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median and 85.5 ms 95th-percentile latency. On a pre-declared held-out cohort using instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, while matched-mask evaluation on validation shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa. Region-aware reference selection improves masked PSNR by 3.60 dB over recency when candidate references can themselves be occluded. Reference-age analysis further defines the operating interval for the brief, repeated occlusions targeted by TransGHOST.|[2611.13971](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13971)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CastWorld: Benchmarking Context-Aware Agentic Time Series Forecasting Beyond Predictive Accuracy**|Bokai Pan et.al.|Agentic time series forecasting extends conventional approaches beyond one-shot numerical prediction toward autonomous, context-aware interaction. However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.|[2611.15262](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15262)|
|**2026-11-16**|**CARE-Drive: Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories**|Xiao Ma et.al.|Vision-language-action (VLA) models bring multimodal understanding and world knowledge to end-to-end autonomous driving. To further improve the quality of predicted trajectories, existing studies introduce candidate refinement, residual denoising, and token correction. However, these methods typically focus on unified refinement of the entire trajectory, paying limited attention to the distinct physical quantities and their interactions. We further investigate physical specialization and coordination in trajectory refinement, centering on position and heading for simpler and more intuitive correction modeling. To this end, we propose CARE-Drive (Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories), which decomposes trajectory refinement into waypoint residual correction and position-conditioned heading generation. Through imitation learning and a future-token auxiliary task, the backbone provides predictive representations and a base trajectory. The position expert predicts bounded metric waypoint residuals, while the heading expert reconstructs vehicle orientation from adjacent angular increments conditioned on the corrected path. With the backbone frozen, the two experts are optimized alternately through supervised initialization and reinforcement fine-tuning. This design simplifies refinement through clear physical specialization while preserving conditional coupling during prediction and optimization. On NAVSIM v1, CARE-Drive achieves 88.8 PDMS, improving by 2.4 points over the unrefined base trajectories generated by the same backbone. Ablation studies further support the effectiveness of specialized experts and staged training.|[2611.15360](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15360)|
|**2026-11-16**|**JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents**|Cheng-Yu Hsieh et.al.|Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.|[2611.16174](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Indic-Task Gains and Regressions after Reinforcement Learning**|Sutanshu Saksena Raj et.al.|We run reinforcement learning (RL) with GRPO across four environments, starting from a supervised fine-tuned 30B-A3B mixture-of-experts model covering English and eight Indic languages. We compare the model before and after RL across 34 benchmarks in one evaluation campaign. MATH improves by 36.07 points, and most Indic benchmarks also improve. Yet XQuAD-Indic F1 falls 19.32 points as English answers rise from 8.7% to 91.7%; IndicQA falls 8.64, while English XQuAD gains 11.71 F1. A second RL run from the same supervised initialization adds an Indic environment together with language and repetition rewards. Compared with the released model, it improves both passage-answering and instruction-following benchmarks, along with preserving MATH performance. But it loses ground on reasoning and coding benchmarks. Several training choices changed together, so we cannot isolate the effect of any one of the reward terms. Script compliance remains unresolved. Adding a language reward did not ensure use of the requested script. Passage answering and script compliance therefore need direct evaluation, even when other benchmark scores improve.|[2611.16228](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16228)|
|**2026-11-16**|**Rectified Looped Transformer**|Musheng Li et.al.|Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.|[2611.16265](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265)|
|**2026-11-16**|**HyBridge: Bridge-Guided Self-Supervised Hyperbolic Hypergraph Representation Learning**|So-Bin Jung et.al.|Hypergraphs capture groupwise relationships among multiple nodes and often exhibit tree-like structures. Although hyperbolic space is well suited for modeling such structures, simply extending existing hypergraph representation learning (HRL) methods to hyperbolic space does not consistently improve performance. In this paper, we investigate the relationship between node structural properties and hyperbolic embedding radii and find that bridge nodes tend to lie closer to the center in graphs, whereas this relationship is not consistently observed in hypergraphs. We further find that conventional bridge score estimation may fail to capture the groupwise connectivity of hypergraphs. Motivated by these findings, we propose HyBridge, a self-supervised hyperbolic HRL framework that (1) estimates node bridge scores by considering the external connectivity of individual hyperedges, (2) learns node and hyperedge embeddings through hyperbolic hypergraph encoding, (3) guides node radial positions according to their bridge scores, and (4) employs contrastive learning to preserve node discriminability. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world hypergraphs demonstrate that HyBridge outperforms 22 competing methods, achieving performance improvements of up to 9.82% in node classification. For reproducibility, we provide the code at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/HyBridge/.|[2611.16351](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16351)|
|**2026-11-16**|**ZONOS3-Mini Technical Report**|Gabriel Clark et.al.|ZONOS3-Mini is an ultra-compact, ultra-fast, zero-shot voice-cloning TTS model. The model can run in real time on a laptop or smartphone. For its latency and size, it achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and cloning fidelity. To achieve this we propose a latent MoE backbone with only 886M parameters (100M active per token) and a new audio codec, Zyphra Audio Codec 1 (ZAC-1), which represents 44.1 kHz audio at a 15 Hz frame rate. On the new ZTTS2-Eval benchmark, ZONOS3-Mini reaches a 6.71% macro-average WER over 55 language sets (35 clean and 20 in the wild), which is competitive with larger and slower state-of-the-art models. We release the model weights, inference, and training code.|[2611.16525](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16525)|
